这些心事，吉安-卢卡一概看不见，他照旧只在她笑里看见好意。然而——凭恋人不误的直觉——他开始莫名地不安。一来，楼上那间屋，再没有请他去喝过茶；二来，如今除了吩咐活计，她少同他说话了。一切都极微妙，极难指名，像鬼火一样抓不住；可不知怎么，老板娘在退，在一寸寸退远。如今他望着她，眼里带着恳求，穿着不合身的衣裳，高高立着，局促着；等她从架上摸酒瓶，或者往杯里放啤酒。有一回她撞见那眼神，皱了眉：

「快点！」她没头没脑地说。

他红了脸，伸出一只发抖的手，去接那还没好的杯子。

===Original===

The cloud of glory was certainly changing, becoming a damp, cold mist, through which Gian-Luca groped helplessly, unable to find his way. At times he would be stupid from sheer eagerness to please, bringing the Padrona the wrong thing; or he might grin at her familiarly, while his only desire was to be deferential. There were moments when his voice sounded sulky and gruff when he wished to be polite﻿—and always his eyes held that dumb appeal, the appeal of a creature in a trap.

Schmidt, intensely attracted by the lure of emotion, felt obliged to forgive Gian-Luca. He could never resist discussing such matters, and so began sympathizing.

“You think she not like you so much as before? Never mind, Gian-Luca, it all come right. You bring her a small bunch of flowers one day﻿—Ach! but she is *wunderschön*!”

He oozed sentiment now from every pore, he was like a ridiculous maiden. “I vill tell you about my girl,” he said sighing; “she is all pink and white like raspberries and cream﻿—she have lovely brown eyes and the big, round hips, what move all the time ven she walks﻿—”

“Go to hell!” growled Gian-Luca, who could not endure this coupling of his love with Schmidt’s.

“Very well then,” said Schmidt, as he turned away; “you think yourself vunderful, very high up! But one efning you come and you say to me: ‘Schmidt, you show me some jolly, nice girls!’ ”

“*Dio!*” groaned Gian-Luca; “will you leave me alone? I do not want any of your girls!”

===Chinese===

那朵荣光的云确实在变，变成一团又冷又湿的雾，吉安-卢卡在雾里乱摸，摸不着路。他有时急欲讨好反而犯傻，给老板娘拿错东西；有时冲她放肆地嘻笑，而他的本意是恭顺。有时他想客气，出口却滣声滣气——而他的眼睛里，永远盛着那种哑巴的恳求，落网之兽的恳求。

施密特被情感的香味强烈吸引，觉得不能不原谅吉安-卢卡。这种事他一谈就收不住，于是开始同情。

「你想她不像从前那样喜欢你了？不要紧，吉安-卢卡，会好的。哪天你送她一小束花——啊！她可真美极了（wunderschön）！」

此刻他每个毛孔都在渗感伤，活像个可笑的大姑娘。「我给你讲讲我的姑娘，」他叹着气说；「她红白分明，像树莓拌奶油——棕色眼睛好看着呢，还有那又圆又大的胯，走起路来一攝一攝的——」

「滚你的！」吉安-卢卡咆哮；他受不了把自己的爱同施密特的配成一对。

「那好罢，」施密特转身说；「你自以为了不起，高得很呢！可哪天晚上你来找我：『施密特，领我见识几个快活漂亮姑娘！』」

「天哪！」吉安-卢卡哼哼；「你放过我不行么？你的姑娘我一个也不要！」

#### III / 三
===Original===

The Padrone it was, and not the Padrona, who suddenly invited Gian-Luca to take part in a day’s excursion on the river. This was in September when business was still slack, they would all lunch at Maidenhead. The Padrone was anxious to appear polite to Fabio, in order that he might drive harder bargains, so his ever fertile mind hit upon the idea of doing a small kindness to the grandson.

“*Guarda*,” Mario shrugged, “you are getting very grand, you will soon not wish to come with me and Rosa.”

“Foolish words!” Gian-Luca told him. “Do I not love you and Rosa?” He was feeling far too happy to be cross.

The train was very crowded but this did not incommode them; the Padrone travelled grandly, first class. This had always been his way when things were going well; he drove hard and squeezed in business, but when on pleasure bent he spent his money freely, like a duke. The Padrona was most richly dressed in cherry-colored foulard; her shady hat had three white ostrich feathers. Round her neck she wore a large, expensive, puffy feather boa, and her little hands were squeezed into new gloves. She was feeling tired, however, and her face looked pale and fretful﻿—she leant back and closed her eyes during the journey. From time to time Gian-Luca stole a surreptitious glance; her long lashes lay so softly﻿—they were golden like her hair, but they darkened very slightly at their tips.

Arrived at Maidenhead, the Padrone hired a steam launch; they were very rich in everything today. They steamed up and down the river, the Padrone sprawling out with his greasy head supported by red cushions. He was smoking a cigar which he chewed from time to time, and then spat across his wife into the water. They went to lunch at Skindles’ to amuse the tired Padrona, who liked gaiety, or so her husband said. Skindles’ was very crowded and the service very slow; the Padrone made several little scenes.

“These English people!” he jeered to Gian-Luca: “they eat roast beef and cabbage and pickled onions. Their waiters are vile and their cooks are still viler; and when they come out to enjoy themselves, one would think they were attending a funeral.”

===Chinese===

突然邀吉安-卢卡参加一日河上游的，是老板，不是老板娘。那是九月，生意正淡，他们全要去梅登黑德（Maidenhead）吃午饭。老板急于对法比奥表示客气，好多砍他的价；他那永远多产的心思，便想出对孙子施一点小恩这条妙计。

「瞧（guarda），」马里奥耸肩，「你的谱越来越大了，快不屑同我和罗莎一起走了。」

「傻话！」吉安-卢卡告诉他。「我不爱你和罗莎么？」他快活极了，恼不起来。

火车挤得很，这不碍他们的事；老板坐头等，派头十足。他一向这样：顺的时候，生意上又逼又抠；一出来玩乐，花钱像公爵。老板娘穿一身樱桃色的软绸，盛装极了；遮阳帽上三根白鸵鸟毛；颈间围一条又大又贵、蓬蓬的羽毛围脖，小手挤在新手套里。只是她乏了，脸色白而烦躁——一路上往后靠着，闭着眼。吉安-卢卡不时偷觑一眼；她长长的睫毛软软地覆着——同头发一样的金色，睫梢却微微深一点。

到了梅登黑德，老板雇了一条汽艇；这一天他们样样阔气。汽艇在河里上下游弋，老板摊手摊脚，油头枕着红垫子。他抽一支雪茄，不时嚼一嚼，然后隔着太太朝水里喳。他们上斯金德尔斯（Skindles'）吃午饭，给乏透了的老板娘解闷——她爱热闹，至少丈夫这么说。斯金德尔斯人满为患，上菜慢得要命；老板小闹了几场。

「这些英国人！」他朝吉安-卢卡撇嘴：「吃烤牛肉、圆白菜、腌洋葱。他们的侍者恶劣，厨子更恶劣；出来找乐子，那副样子，倒像送葬。」

===Original===

The Padrone himself was growing rather noisy, he was drinking a good deal of wine. His morning had begun with several gins and bitters, and just before luncheon he had swallowed another in order to keep up his spirits. The Padrona was very quiet and aloof, she scarcely glanced at Gian-Luca; in his desperation he began making jokes at which the Padrone laughed boisterously﻿—the Padrona did not laugh at all. Gian-Luca was filled with the bitter knowledge of doing and saying the wrong thing; he was thankful when at last the meal came to an end and his host was disputing the bill. The Padrona left them to go to the cloakroom and Gian-Luca strolled into the garden, but after him hurried the redfaced Padrone and seized the lapel of his coat. The Padrone was now feeling melodramatic, his brown eyes were swimming, his lips sagged a little.

“If you knew how that woman torments me!” he began; “if you knew how she makes me suffer. I say: ‘Cesare, be careful, be very, very careful, she is young, any moment she may leave you!’ ”

“*Ma no*,” said Gian-Luca.

“*Ma si*,” babbled the Padrone; “I say; ‘She is young, she may leave you.’ ”

Gian-Luca had perforce to stand there and listen, the Padrone was really very drunk.

“If you knew﻿—if you knew﻿—” he kept on repeating, and his eyes filled with idiotic tears.

“I do not wish to know,” Gian-Luca told him, hot with embarrassment and shame.

“You are so discreet﻿—” gulped the tearful Padrone. “I would not tell anyone but you﻿—”

The Padrona came back, having powdered her nose, and they went for a walk by the river. She was still very silent, still very aloof; she walked primly between Gian-Luca and her husband, taking the latter’s arm. On the feeble pretext of showing her a boat, Gian-Luca got her away. The Padrone, scarcely seeming to notice, strolled on down the towpath alone.

“Signora, are you angry with me?” whispered Gian-Luca.

“Of course not,” she answered, smiling; but she looked straight passed him along the river, as one will look who is bored.

Words failed him. He suddenly seized her hand which he pressed and tried to retain; he felt that he must die of loving at that moment unless she would let him express it. He glanced at the unconscious back of the Padrone﻿—the Padrone did not turn his head.

“I love you so terribly!” gasped Gian-Luca; “I love you so terribly, signora!”

===Chinese===

老板自己渐渐吵嚷起来，酒下了不少。他这天早晨由几杯杜松子加苦酒开台，午饭前又灌了一杯提神。老板娘安静而疏远，几乎不看吉安-卢卡一眼；他绝望之下说起笑话，老板轰然大笑——老板娘一丝也不笑。吉安-卢卡满心苦楚的自觉：件件做错，句句说错；好不容易这顿饭吃完，主人开始同账单计较，他如释重负。老板娘去化妆间，吉安-卢卡踱进花园，红光满面的老板却追上来，攝住他的衣领。老板此刻正煽情，棕色眼睛水汪汪，嘴角搭拉下来。

「你要知道那个女人怎样磨我就好了！」他开腔；「你要知道她叫我怎样受苦就好了。我对自己说：『切萨雷，小心，千万千万小心，她年轻，随时会离开你！』」

「哪会呢（ma no）。」吉安-卢卡说。

「可不是嘛（ma si），」老板喋喋；「我说：『她年轻，她会离开你。』」

吉安-卢卡只好站在那里听；老板当真醉得可以。

「你要知道——你要知道——」他翻来覆去，眼里涌起呆呆的泪。

「我不想知道。」吉安-卢卡告诉他，窘得、臊得发热。

「你嘴这样严——」含泪的老板一口一口地咽，「这话我只告诉你一个人——」

老板娘扑完粉回来了，他们沿河散步。她仍旧很静，仍旧疏远；她端端正正走在吉安-卢卡同丈夫中间，挽着丈夫的臂。吉安-卢卡借口请她看一条船，把她引开。老板浑然不觉，独自顺纤道踱下去。

「夫人，您恼我么？」吉安-卢卡低声问。

「当然不。」她含笑答；可目光直直越过他，顺着河望过去，像烦闷的人那样望。

他说不出话了。他忽然攝住她的手，紧握，想留住；他觉得那一刻若不让他倾吐，他就要爱死了。他瞟一眼老板毫无知觉的背影——老板没有回头。

「我这样爱您，爱得受不了！」吉安-卢卡喘着说；「爱得受不了，夫人！」

===Original===

“Be quiet!” she said, wrenching her hand away; “I will not tolerate this folly.” And turning, she hurried after her husband, leaving Gian-Luca to follow.

For the rest of the day they were all very silent, the Padrone was growing sleepy. In the train going home he did fall asleep, and proceeded to snore quite loudly. Gian-Luca sat looking out of the window, not daring to look at the Padrona. His heart ached with a pain so intolerable and new that he wanted to protest, to cry out; but arrived at the station he helped them to alight﻿—the Padrone as well as the Padrona.

“So,” thought Gian-Luca; “so this is love!” And because of his heartache he felt a little frightened.

===Chinese===

「住口！」她说，猛地抽回手；「这种蠢话我不容。」说着转身，急急追她丈夫去了，丢下吉安-卢卡跟上。

那天余下的时光，大家都默默的；老板越来越困。回程的火车上他当真睡着了，鼾声很响。吉安-卢卡坐在那里望窗外，不敢看老板娘。他心口疼，疼得那样难忍、那样崭新，想抗议，想喊；可到了站，他还是扶他们下车——扶老板，也扶老板娘。

「原来，」吉安-卢卡想，「原来这就是爱！」因为心口疼，他有点怕了。

#### IV / 四
===Original===

By the time that his seventeenth birthday arrived, Gian-Luca had decided that no one in the world was so utterly unhappy as he was. If all had not been well with the Padrona before, it was certainly less well now; if he had felt that she was slipping away, she was now so far off as to be quite inaccessible, for now she no longer singled him out to attend to her needs at the bar. She selected Schmidt﻿—the plump Schmidt of all people﻿—to polish and tidy and fetch and carry; Schmidt who would have a sly look in his eye whenever he passed Gian-Luca.

“*Nun was!*” grinned Schmidt; “I do not wish it, you know﻿—I have my own girl I like much better, and this gives me nur more work.”

Gian-Luca eyed him with open contempt; did he think, the fool, that the Padrona admired him? “No, no, it cannot be that﻿—” thought Gian-Luca; “it is only that she now hates me.”

Yet why did she hate him? He had told her of his love, but surely she had known it long before he had spoken that day at Maidenhead? Then what was his transgression? A transgression of words? Yes, that must be it, he might love her it seemed, and she on her part might know that he loved her﻿—only, he must not say so. This made him laugh a little it seemed so very foolish; then it made him frown a little, it seemed so very mean.

“While I spoke as a child, she could smile,” thought Gian-Luca; “that day in her sitting-room I spoke as a child, I amused her perhaps; now I do not amuse her because she knows I am a man.”

For the quality of his love was gradually changing, the dew was no longer on the meadow. The knight-errant of youth was still in the saddle, but his armor was already slightly tarnished. Something deep down in Gian-Luca was conscious of this change and began to grow infinitely sad. It cried out because of the splendor that was passing﻿—that particular splendor that could never come again, for no two dawns are alike in this world. Gian-Luca began to feel very angry because of the sadness that was in him; he would glare at the Padrona from the end of the room, and all that she did would seem mean and unlovely, yet this in itself would fill him with loving, and then he would almost hate her.

===Chinese===

到十七岁生日那天，吉安-卢卡断定：天底下再没有谁比他更不幸福。如果说先前老板娘待他已不如意，如今更不如意了；如果说先前他觉得她在退远，如今她远得完全够不着了——如今她连吧台的差遣也不再点他。她挑中了施密特——偏偏挑中胖施密特——擦呀理呀取呀送呀；施密特每次打吉安-卢卡身边过，眼里都带着一机灵。

「我说（nun was）！」施密特咧嘴；「你知道我并不想要——我有自己的姑娘，喜欢得多；这白白多给我添活。」

吉安-卢卡公然轻蔑地瞪他；这蠢货莫非以为老板娘欣赏他？「不，不，决不会是那样——」吉安-卢卡想；「只是她如今恨我了。」

可她为什么恨他？他说出了爱；可梅登黑德那天之前，她不是早就知道了么？那他的罪过是什么？言语之罪？是了，必是这个：他仿佛可以爱她，她那方面仿佛也可以知道他爱她——只是，不许说出口。这叫他笑了笑，实在太傻；随后又叫他皱了皱眉，实在太卑吝。

「我以孩子的口吻说话时，她能笑，」吉安-卢卡想；「那天在她客厅里，我是孩子口气，兴许逗她乐了；如今逗不了了，因为她知道我是男人。」

他这份爱的质地正在悄悄变化，草上的露水干了。少年的游侠骑士还坐在鞍上，只是铠甲已微微失色。吉安-卢卡心底深处觉出了这变化，开始无限地悲伤。它为那正在逝去的华光喊出声——那种永不再来的华光，因为世上没有两个相同的黎明。吉安-卢卡为身内的悲伤恼怒起来；他从店堂尽头瞪着老板娘，她的一举一动都显得吝啬、不美；而这本身又填满他爱，随后他又几乎恨她。

===Original===

The poor Padrona would have many little tasks to perform in connection with the bar. She might be mopping up the counter, for instance, removing the stale-smelling, beery foam, or rubbing the blurs left by whisky and brandy; or perhaps she might be wiping the sides of a bottle that had grown sticky sweet with liqueur. Her face would almost certainly be flushed by the exertion of providing other people with drinks; from time to time she would pause to draw a cork, bending ungracefully to the bottle which she held between her round feminine knees. “Two mixed grill and mashed!” she might shout down the lift shaft, electing to speak in English; and when she did this her voice sounded Cockney, not soft and pleasing as when she spoke Italian.

The restaurant would smell stuffy, Schmidt’s forehead would be beaded, and Mario’s collar damp and creased; while downstairs in the kitchen, the sweating Moscatone might pause from time to time to pick his front teeth with a fork. And the beautiful Padrona with her fine Venetian hair, and her eyes the color of gentians, would seem strangely out of place amid her surroundings, yet very much a part of them too.

===Chinese===

可怜的老板娘在吧台上有的是小活计。比方擦柜台，擦掉那股陈啤酒沫；或抹去威士忌白兰地留下的印子；或是擦一只给力娇酒粘得甜腻腻的瓶颈。她的脸几乎准是被斟酒的劳顿蒸红的；不时停下拔一个软木塞，不雅观地朝夹在两条圆滚滚的女人膝盖中间的酒瓶俯下身去。「两份烤杂拌加土豆泥！」她可能朝升降机井里喊，偏用英语；这一喊，腔调就伦敦腔了，不像说意大利语时那样软和悦耳。

饭馆里闷气，施密特额头挂着汗珠，马里奥的领子潮了皱了；楼下厨房里，淌汗的莫斯卡托内不时停下，拿叉子剔门牙。而美丽的老板娘，一头上好的威尼斯头发，一双龙胆色的眼睛，在这环境里显得格格不入，却又是它分不开的一部分。

#### V / 五
===Original===

“You grow sulky,” said Mario to his foster-son; “what is the matter? You no longer work gladly.”

“I am tired of the Capo,” Gian-Luca told him; “I think I must find a better job.”

“You are young and a fool,” snapped Mario crossly. “I too have been foolish enough in my day, but never, no, never such a big fool as you are.”

“I am tired of the Capo,” Gian-Luca repeated, pretending not to understand.

It was all very lonely, more lonely by far than anything he had yet known. In the past he had been lonely but without the Padrona, whereas now he was utterly lonely with her; and to find oneself lonely with the creature one loves is to plumb the full depths of desolation. There was no one he could talk to, Mario did not sympathize and Schmidt was a low-minded fellow; as for Fabio and Teresa﻿—at the mere thought of them Gian-Luca could not help laughing.

He still had his books, and long into the night he would read the “Gioia della Luce.” All poetry hurt him a little it was true, but that poem could comfort while it hurt. There were many other poems which he reread in that book, some of them spiritual and placid﻿—they might have been written by a saint or a seer; but then would come others, crude outpourings of passion that he had not understood as a child. Like the “Gioia della Luce,” they partook of the greatness that, carnal or spiritual, belonged to the genius of their writer, Ugo Doria.

“A very curious book,” thought Gian-Luca; “I must read some more of this poet’s work.” So one day he made his way to Hatchard’s, where he heard that they sold foreign books.

“Ugo Doria?” said the salesman, smiling at Gian-Luca. “Oh, yes, he is getting quite famous. Will you have him in English or in Italian? We have all the translations of his earlier works, but his new book of essays has not been translated yet.”

“I can only afford one book,” said Gian-Luca; “I will take the essays in Italian.” And he went to the Capo with the book in his pocket, in case he could read it between luncheon and dinner.

===Chinese===

「你越来越恒了，」马里奥对养子说；「怎么回事？干活不带欢劲儿了。」

「卡波我待腻了，」吉安-卢卡告诉他；「我看我得找个好些的差事。」

「你年轻，你蠢，」马里奥没好气地抢白。「我年轻时也蠢过，可从没有——没有——从没蠢到你这个份上。」

「卡波我待腻了。」吉安-卢卡重复，装作没听懂。

这一切孤单透了，比他经历过的任何孤单都孤单得多。从前他也孤单，可那时没有老板娘；如今有她，还是全然地孤单；而同所爱的人处出孤单来，就是坠到了荒凉的底。没有人可说：马里奥不同情，施密特心思卑下；至于法比奥和特蕾莎——一想到他们，吉安-卢卡忍不住要笑。

他还有他的书；夜深了还读《光之喜乐》。一切诗都确实有点伤他，可那一首伤着还能安慰。那本书里还有许多他重读又重读的诗，有些空灵而安宁——简直像圣徒或先知写的；可跟着又来另一些，激情的粗粝喷涌，他小时候不曾懂得的。同《光之喜乐》一样，它们分有那一种伟大——或肉或灵——属于它们的作者乌戈·多里亚（Ugo Doria）的天才。

「一本顶古怪的书，」吉安-卢卡想；「这位诗人的东西我还得多读。」于是有一天他去了哈查兹（Hatchard's）书店，听说那儿卖外国书。

「乌戈·多里亚？」店员笑着对吉安-卢卡说。「哦，有，他渐渐大有名了。要英文的还是意大利文的？他早期的作品译本全有，新出的一本散文集还没译。」

「我只买得起一本，」吉安-卢卡说；「就要意大利文的散文集。」他揣着书去卡波，预备赶午饭晚饭之间的空当读。

===Original===

Gian-Luca could not know that the technique was flawless, that each word had been tried and weighed and considered, that side by side with his vast inspiration the writer possessed the mind of an explorer﻿—an explorer in the country of language. He could not know that all Italy was saying that Doria wrote with his pen dipped in gold dust, that never since Dante had there lived such a poet, and moreover, that his prose was even finer than his verse. But he did know that he, the sorrowful waiter, who could not write poems though his heart felt full to breaking, found solace and comparative comfort while he read, because of the beautiful lilt of the words. While he read he could almost forget the Padrona﻿—

“And so,” thought Gian-Luca, “he must be very great. I would like to see him, I would like to serve him, I myself would like to pour out his wine﻿—I wish that he would come to the Capo.”

But Doria never came to the Capo﻿—he happened to be living in Rome at that moment; and then, after all, he could only write﻿—he might make one forget the Padrona for a while, but he could not soften her heart.

===Chinese===

吉安-卢卡无从知道：那技法毫无瑕玷，每个词都试过、称过、掂量过；同浩大的灵感并肩，作者还长着一颗探险家的头脑——语言之国的探险家。他无从知道：全意大利都在说，多里亚的笔尖蘸的是金粉；但丁以后再没出过这样的诗人；何况他的散文比诗还好。可他确实知道：他，这个伤心的侍者，心满得要破、却写不出诗的侍者，一读那些词句美丽的抑扬，便得了安慰，得了相当的舒坦。读着的时候，他几乎能忘掉老板娘——

「这样看来，」吉安-卢卡想，「他必定非常伟大。我想见他，我想伺候他，想亲手给他斟酒——巴望他上卡波来。」

可多里亚从没来过卡波——他那时恰巧住在罗马；再说，他到底只会写——他能叫人一时忘掉老板娘，却软化不了她的心。

### XIII / 十三
===Original===



===Chinese===

十三

#### I / 一
===Original===

It was just after Christmas when Signor Millo marched into Fabio’s shop one day and handed his card across the counter.

“This way, signore, this way!” exclaimed Fabio, pink to the brow with pleasure and excitement. And he took him into Teresa’s back parlor and pushed up the most propitious chair.

Signor Millo was a man of forty-five, of medium height and broad-shouldered. His brown hair curled close to his round, shapely head; there was something about him that suggested the antique﻿—perhaps the Roman arena. But his brow was intellectual, and his mouth rather grave. His eyes, dark and set very wide apart, had a wonderfully wise and benign expression as though they neither questioned nor condemned. Seeing him thus, as he sat in the armchair with his hat held loosely between his knees, was to wonder what manner of man this was. An athlete? An author? A philosopher, perhaps? As a matter of fact he was none of these things; he was Francesco Millo, the director of the Doric, which, thanks to his skill, to his excellent judgment and elegant epicurean palate, had risen in the last few years to great fame among the restaurants of London.

That he should have come in person to the Casa Boselli was quite on a par with the rest of the man. He might have sent several intelligent people, all well up in their professional duties, but instead he had preferred to call on Fabio himself﻿—such little excursions amused him. It is said that in each man there lurks the hunter; the hunter of money, the hunter of lions, the hunter of fame, the hunter of women; and Francesco Millo was also a hunter, as keen on the trail and as steadfast as any, indeed he was tireless, hence the fame of his restaurant, for Millo was a hunter of food.

He had said to himself at the beginning of his career: “There are three very vital things; quality, variety and originality, and the last is perhaps the most vital of the three. A dinner should have, like a book or a picture, good workmanship, plenty of light and shade, and above all that individual touch, that original central idea.”

===Chinese===

圣诞节刚过，有一天，米洛先生走进了法比奥的铺子，隔着柜台递上名片。

“这边请，先生，这边请！”法比奥兴奋得满面通红，直红到额头。他把客人领进特蕾莎的后客厅，把最吉利的一把椅子推上前去。

米洛先生四十五岁，中等身材，肩膀宽阔。棕色的头发紧贴着圆润端正的头颅卷着；他身上有股古风的味儿——也许像罗马竞技场。可他额头聪明，嘴相颇为持重。一双深色的眼睛分得很开，带着一种极其智慧而慈和的神情，仿佛既不盘问也不谴责。看他这样坐在扶手椅里，帽子松松夹在两膝之间，你会纳闷这是何等样人。运动员？作家？还是哲人？其实都不是；他是弗朗切斯科·米洛（Francesco Millo），多立克餐馆的经理——多亏他的本领、他出色的判断力和优雅讲究的美食口腹，这家馆子近几年来在伦敦各家餐馆中声名鹊起。

他肯亲自登门博塞利之家，也全然合乎此人的做派。他满可以派几个精明的人来，个个业务娴熟，可他宁愿亲自拜访法比奥——这样的小小出游使他快活。据说每个人心里都潜藏着一个猎手：猎钱的、猎狮的、猎名的、猎女人的；弗朗切斯科·米洛也是个猎手，追踪起来一样起劲、一样坚忍，实在是不知疲倦——他那餐馆的名声正由此而来，因为米洛是个猎食的。

他在事业起步时对自己说过：“有三样性命攸关的东西：品质、花样、独到，而最后一样也许是三样中最要命的。一席晚餐应当像一本书、一幅画，有好的手艺，有充足的明暗，而尤其要有那一笔个人的色彩，那个独创的中心的意念。”

===Original===

And so, whenever the spirit moved him﻿—which was often, for he was a restless man﻿—Millo went forth in search of strange viands. Just now he was after some special funghi that grew in the woods not far from Turin. As luck would have it he had heard from a confrère that the Casa Boselli had but lately imported a case of those special funghi.

“*Ma sicuro*,” said Fabio, “we certainly keep them; they are fat, good funghi, you may see for yourself, you may smell them.” And he fetched a terrific looking toadstool for the great man’s careful inspection.

Millo sniffed it. “It is prime, as you say,” he remarked, and he promptly bought the whole case. “And now I will look at your stock, if you please; I would also like to see your price-list.”

Fabio was trembling with excitement by now﻿—the Casa Boselli and Millo. What a happening to make Nerone more jealous! What a snub for the Signora Rocca! He pottered about showing first this, then that, his *paste*, his hams, his tomatoes; then those elegant, more highly-specialized foods, caviar in jars, *carciofini* in oil, tunnyfish, fillets of anchovies with capers, and large, green, globular snails.

“All excellent, fresh, and quite inexpensive!” he kept chanting in a kind of litany. “All excellent, fresh, and quite inexpensive﻿—and we keep a great variety of foodstuffs.”

Signor Millo stood still, and surveyed him gravely. “I would speak,” he said, holding up his hand.

“*Prego!*” bowed Fabio, trembling more than ever in case he had talked too much.

“I am rather disposed to give you some orders, your shop is so excellently kept; I think also that there must be someone here who has an enterprising mind. If you get my custom, of course you are made, for I never stint recommendations; but, and this is important, so I beg that you listen, the first time you fail me I break you, signore. If ever you should send me a thing that is not fresh, a thing that could injure the stomach of a client, that day I write round to my confrères and tell them﻿—I cancel my recommendations, signore; and such is my system of management.”

Fabio bowed low; he was longing intensely for the reassuring presence of Teresa, but Teresa had unfortunately gone to the Capo to call on the Padrona that afternoon.

“It shall be as you say, I am honored,” babbled Fabio. “You shall have no cause for complaint.”

===Chinese===

于是，每当兴头上来——兴头常常上来，因为他是个闲不住的人——米洛便出门搜罗奇珍异味。这回他追的是一种特殊的菌子，长在都灵城外不远的林子里。碰巧他从一位同行那里听说，博塞利之家新近刚进口了一箱那种菌子。

“没错，”法比奥说，“我们确实有；个个肥壮，都是好菌子，您可以亲眼看，亲鼻子闻。”说着他捧出一只模样吓人的大家伙，供大人物仔细过目。

米洛嗅了嗅。“确实地道，如你所说。”他说，当即把整箱都买了下来。“现在请让我看看你的存货；还想看看你的价目单。”

法比奥这时激动得直哆嗦——博塞利之家和米洛！这可是叫内罗内眼红的一件大事！这是给罗卡太太的一记闷棍！他忙前忙后，一会儿端出这个，一会儿端出那个：他的面食、他的火腿、他的番茄；接着是那些高雅的、更专门的名贵食品：罐装鱼子酱、油浸洋蓟心、金枪鱼、带酸豆的鳀鱼片，还有大颗的、青绿色的圆蜗牛。

“样样上等，样样新鲜，样样便宜！”他像念经一样翻来覆去地唱。“样样上等，样样新鲜，样样便宜——我们货色齐全。”

米洛先生站定，正色打量他。“我有话要说。”他举起一只手。

“您请！”法比奥躬身，哆嗦得更厉害了，生怕自己话说多了。

“我颇有意照顾你一些生意，你这铺子办得太出色了；我想这里头必有个脑瓜活络的人。你若得到我的光顾，自然就发定了，我荐人从不吝惜；但是——这条要紧，请你听好——你头一回误我的事，我就毁了你，先生。你哪天要是给我送来一样不新鲜的东西、一样吃坏了客人肠胃的东西，当天我就写信遍告同行，告诉他们——我的荐举一笔勾销，先生；这就是我的经营之道。”

法比奥深深躬身；他极度渴望特蕾莎那令人安心的身影在旁，可惜那天下午特蕾莎偏偏到卡波看望老板娘去了。

“一切照您吩咐，不胜荣幸，”法比奥喋喋不休，“您绝没有抱怨的道理。”

===Original===

At that moment, Gian-Luca strolled in at the door, and seeing the stranger, paused. He looked very tall in the little shop as he towered over Fabio and Millo. His neat blue serge suit became him well﻿—it had not been bought secondhand, for Gian-Luca was particular about his clothes, and when in mufti he dressed with great care, he was even a little foppish. Standing there young, fair-haired and alert, there was something incongruous about him, something that seemed to set him apart from the salumeria in Old Compton Street, from Fabio, from Millo even.

“This is my grandson,” Fabio explained; “he is a waiter at the Capo di Monte.” Millo bowed slightly. “*Piacere*,” he murmured, as though he were thinking of something quite different; as a matter of fact he was all attention and his mind was working very quickly.

If Francesco Millo had a palate for good food, he had also an eye for a waiter; those who served were as carefully chosen by him as the dishes they were privileged to handle. Moreover, he prided himself on his flair. “I know in a moment,” he was wont to say proudly; “I know the moment my eye rests upon them. It amounts to a psychic faculty with me: I can pick the good waiter out of a thousand; the waiter is born and not made.” His eyes took in every line of Gian-Luca, without appearing to do so. “He is mine!” he was thinking, with that thrill of satisfaction that belongs to the ardent collector. “As it happens,” he said casually, turning to Fabio, “I am wanting a waiter next month; now if your grandson is free I might try him﻿—I suppose he has had some experience?”

“He has had three years at the Capo,” Fabio told him.

“That says nothing to me,” smiled Millo.

“The Capo di Monte in Dean Street, signore.”

“I have never heard of the place.” Then after a minute:

“But that need not matter, no doubt it serves some sort of meals. Perhaps you will give me the name of the Padrone so that I may take up your grandson’s reference; he will get a good reference, I think.”

All this time Gian-Luca had not opened his mouth, nor had he once been consulted; now, however, Millo turned and addressed him directly.

===Chinese===

正在这时，吉安-卢卡从门口踱了进来，一见生人，便停住脚。他站在小店里显得很高，高出法比奥和米洛一头。他那身匀净的蓝色哔叽西装很称他——不是二手货，吉安-卢卡对衣裳是讲究的，穿便服时穿戴得极其用心，甚至有几分纨绔。他站在那里，年轻、金发、机警，周身有一种不谐调的东西，仿佛把他同老康普顿街的这家熟食铺、同法比奥、甚至同米洛隔开了。

“这是我外孙，”法比奥解释道，“他在卡波迪蒙特当侍者。”米洛微微欠身。“幸会。”他喃喃道，像是想着别的心事；其实他全神贯注，脑筋转得飞快。

弗朗切斯科·米洛既然有品评美食的舌头，也就有品评侍者的眼光；伺候他的人，同他们有幸经手的菜肴一样，都经他精心挑选。而且他为自己的眼力得意。“我一眼便知，”他惯于自豪地说，“我的眼睛一落到他们身上就知道。这在我已成了一种通灵的本事：一千个侍者我能挑出好的那个；侍者是生就的，不是造就的。”他的眼睛不动声色地扫过吉安-卢卡的每一根线条。“他是我的了！”他心想，带着热切的收藏家那种战栗的满足。“碰巧，”他装作随意地转向法比奥，“我下个月正缺一个侍者；你外孙若是没有主顾，我倒可以试试他——想必他有些经验吧？”

“他在卡波干过三年。”法比奥告诉他。

“这对我说明不了什么。”米洛笑道。

“迪恩街的卡波迪蒙特，先生。”

“没听说过这地方。”过了一分钟又说：

“不过那不要紧，想必总也开点饭。请你把老板的姓名给我，我好去打听你外孙的保荐；他会得到一份好保荐的，我想。”

这工夫吉安-卢卡始终没开口，也没人问过他一句；此刻米洛却转过身，直接对他说起话来。

===Original===

“If your reference is good, as I think it will be, you may come on the morning of the twentieth, side-door, 9:30. I will let you know when I hear from your Padrone, so that you may order yourself a dress-suit.” He scribbled an address on a leaf from his notebook: “This is the tailor who makes for the Doric, he will know how I wish your dress-suit to be made, he will also tell you what else is required. As for terms﻿—they depend entirely on yourself; I pay high, but only for good service. You will not be dependent upon your tips, everyone at the Doric receives a salary﻿—I cannot stop to go into that now, I will speak to you when you come on the twentieth. If you prove satisfactory you will not complain of my terms; if you prove unsatisfactory there will not be any terms, for you go﻿—we will try you and see. I think that is all﻿—oh, do you speak English?”

“I do, Signore,” said Gian-Luca promptly, but before he could get in another word, the great one was leaving the shop.

“*Dio mio!*” breathed Fabio. “Our Lady is good; she is patient in spite of Teresa.”

But Gian-Luca said nothing, he was thinking deeply﻿—he was thinking about the Padrona.

===Chinese===

“你的保荐若是没有问题——我想不会错——你二十号早晨来，走边门，九点半。等我问过你老板就给你信儿，你好去订一套礼服。”他在记事本上撕下一页，草草写了个地址：“这是给多立克做衣裳的裁缝，他知道我要你的礼服做成什么样子，也会告诉你还要置备些什么。至于待遇——全看你自己；我出高价，但只为好的伺候。你不靠客人赏钱过活，多立克人人拿薪水——这会儿来不及细说，你二十号来了我再同你讲。你若让人满意，我的待遇你不会抱怨；你若让人不满意，那便谈不上待遇，因为你走人——我们试试看。就这些——哦，你会讲英语吗？”

“会，先生。”吉安-卢卡立刻答道，可还没容他再插一个字，大人物已经出了铺子。

“我的上帝！”法比奥喃喃道，“圣母是慈悲的；尽管有特蕾莎，她还是忍耐着。”

可吉安-卢卡什么也没说，他正想得深——想的是老板娘。

#### II / 二
===Original===

A new idea came to Gian-Luca that night, and he sat up suddenly in bed. “When I go she will miss me,” he thought triumphantly. “She shall find out for herself what it means to be without me, how lonely it will feel not to have the Gian-Luca who was always so ready to serve her.”

He pictured the Padrona sitting behind the bar, with her face in her hands﻿—weeping. “Gian-Luca, Gian-Luca, I want you!” she wailed. “I cannot get on without you!”

He would go there and see her, in a suit of fine clothes, they paid high for good service at the Doric. He would say: “I have come!” And she would reply: “Oh, but I am glad, Gian-Luca! I will meet you tomorrow between luncheon and dinner. Where can we go to be alone?”

“*Gia*,” thought Gian-Luca, nodding very wisely, “that is the way with women; when they have you they despise you, but when they have you not, then it is they find out that they want you.” He was very near tears in this moment of triumph, but his fighting instinct held. “I will go to the Doric,” he told himself bravely. “I am glad that I have come to a decision.”

This of course was the purest make-believe, for he knew that he had no choice in the matter; Fabio might be weak, but he would not consent to the loss of a fine job like this. Moreover, there was Millo’s custom to consider﻿—that might mean fame for the Casa Boselli. And then there was Teresa, the ambitious, the strong-minded, the dominating woman of affairs.

Nevertheless, he went on repeating: “I will certainly go to the Doric.” And he added: “Tomorrow I tell the Padrone, then we shall see what we shall see.”

===Chinese===

当夜，吉安-卢卡想出一个新主意，他从床上一骨碌坐起来。“我走了，她会想我的，”他得意地想，“她要亲身尝尝没有我是什么滋味，没有了这个随叫随到、伺候惯了她的吉安-卢卡，该有多冷清。”

他想象老板娘坐在账台后面，双手掩着脸——哭着。“吉安-卢卡，吉安-卢卡，我要你！”她哀哀地哭着，“没有你我撑不下去！”

他要穿着一身漂亮衣裳回去看她——多立克给好的伺候出高价。他要说：“我回来了！”她会答：“啊，我真高兴，吉安-卢卡！明天午市晚市之间我来会你。我们到哪里单独说话去？”

“对，”吉安-卢卡想，煞有介事地点着头，“女人就是这样：你有她，她就看轻你；你没她，她才知道要你。”在这得意的时刻他几乎落泪，但他的斗志撑住了。“我要去多立克，”他勇敢地对自己说，“我很高兴我拿定了主意。”

这当然纯属自欺，因为他心里明白这事由不得他：法比奥再软弱，也决不肯丢掉这样一份好差事；何况还有米洛的生意要考虑——那也许意味着博塞利之家的名声。更还有特蕾莎——那个野心勃勃、说一不二、专断能干的女人。

然而他还是一遍遍念叨：“我一定去多立克。”又添一句：“明天我就告诉老板，到时候看个分晓。”

#### III / 三
===Original===

The next morning he arrived at the Capo very early in order to tell the Padrone. By the greatest good fortune the Padrona was present, which was what Gian-Luca had hoped for. He did not look at the Padrone when he spoke, his eyes were on the Padrona.

The Padrone said wrathfully: “So this is gratitude!” and his facial expression was appalling. Then he went out, banging the door behind him, and his voice could be heard coming up from the kitchen with a dash of Moscatone thrown in.

The Padrona said: “This is very fine, Gian-Luca, I congratulate you, my child.” She was checking the accounts of the bar at the moment, and she went on checking her accounts.

Gian-Luca said uncertainly: “I am going in four weeks﻿—this means that I am going away﻿—”

“It does,” said the Padrona; “we must find another waiter, and that is always a bother. I shall not take a Swiss, I dislike them on the whole, though Schmidt is a very good fellow. By the way, we can give you an excellent reference, you have always been capable and quick.”

Gian-Luca left her without another word. His lip shot out, his eyes were very bright. He said to Mario later:

“I am lucky, my good Mario, I shall make my fortune now, you will see!”

===Chinese===

第二天一早，他早早到了卡波，好去禀告老板。天大的运气，老板娘正在——这正是吉安-卢卡盼的。他说话时不看老板，眼睛只望着老板娘。

老板怒冲冲地说：“这就是你的报答！”脸上的表情怕人。随后他摔门而出，人还没到厨房，骂声就从楼下传上来，还夹着一口莫斯卡托甜酒。

老板娘说：“这很好呀，吉安-卢卡，我恭喜你，孩子。”她这会儿正在核对酒吧的账，说完接着核她的账。

吉安-卢卡没把握地说：“过四个星期我就走了——这就是说，我要离开了——”

“是的，”老板娘说，“我们得另找一个侍者，那总是件麻烦事。我不打算要瑞士人，总的来说我不喜欢他们，虽说施密特人是好人。顺便说一句，我们可以给你一份极好的保荐，你一向能干麻利。”

吉安-卢卡一言不发地离开了她。他的嘴唇翘起来，眼睛亮得很。他后来对马里奥说：

“我走运了，我的好马里奥，我这下要发财了，你看着吧！”

#### IV / 四
===Original===

The last four weeks at the Capo di Monte passed like an evil dream. For one thing there was Mario, very sorrowful and servile; he was going to miss Gian-Luca, and he said so. His manner to the boy had now completely changed, it was that of failure towards success. He bragged less about the Capo and not at all about himself.

“I am just an old lame mule,” he would say humbly, remembering that taunt of the Padrone’s.

“He is not so lame as all that!” Gian-Luca would try to think; “he puts it on in order to get pitied.” But he knew that this was not so; Mario’s lameness was quite real, and moreover, it was often very painful.

Mario said: “Since you were little and Rosa gave you milk, I have always been so fond of you, Gian-Luca﻿—now you go out into the world with no old Mario near. You be careful; do not listen to people like this Schmidt, who are always thinking about women.”

“Women!” frowned Gian-Luca, “I want no more of women!”

And at that Mario’s mouth twitched a little; but he went on very gravely: “You are angry with our Padrona, you have great unkindness in your heart towards her. Now that is what I fear, *piccino*, anger in the heart﻿—I know, for I too have felt such anger. It is dangerous, it is stupid, it makes a man a beast; it makes him forget how strong he is. The Padrona is a woman and therefore you should pity, and moreover she is really very right. For one thing she is nearly old enough to be your mother, for another she considers her business, for another she remembers the Padrone no doubt﻿—a terrible man when in anger.” He paused, for Gian-Luca had turned his back, but presently he went on still more gravely: “Forget her when you leave here, it would be better so﻿—but if you must remember, do so kindly.”

“Is it I, then, who must show all the kindness?” exclaimed Gian-Luca.

“Precisely﻿—that is so,” Mario told him. “You have nothing to forgive, and if you had, remember the Padrona is only a woman.”

===Chinese===

在卡波迪蒙特的最后四个星期，像一场噩梦似的过去了。先说马里奥，又伤心又低声下气；他舍不得吉安-卢卡，也这样说了。他对这孩子的态度如今全变了，是失败者对成功者的态度。他不再吹卡波，更不再吹自己。

“我不过是一头瘸腿的老骡子。”他常谦卑地说，想起老板那句挖苦。

“他哪瘸成那样！”吉安-卢卡勉强这样想，“他装出来的，好叫人可怜他。”可他知道不是；马里奥的瘸千真万确，而且常常疼得很。

马里奥说：“打你小时候罗莎喂你奶起，我就一直疼你，吉安-卢卡——如今你到世上去闯，身边没有老马里奥了。你小心着点；别听施密特那种人的话，那种人整天想的就是女人。”

“女人！”吉安-卢卡皱起眉，“女人我再也不沾了！”

听了这话，马里奥的嘴角抽了一下；可他仍旧郑重地说下去：“你在生我们老板娘的气，你心里对她存着很大的狠。这正是我怕的，小家伙，心里存狠——我知道，因为我也存过。这危险，这愚蠢，这叫人变成畜生；叫他忘了自己有多强壮。老板娘是个女人，女人就该怜惜；再说她也实在非常有理。一来她差不多够做你的母亲，二来她惦着她的生意，三来她多半还惦着老板——那人动起怒来是可怕的。”他停了停，因为吉安-卢卡已转过身去；可他随后说得更郑重了：“你离开这里就把她忘了吧，那样更好——可你若忘不掉，就善待着记她。”

“这么说，该表示好意的全是我了？”吉安-卢卡喊起来。

“正是——就是这样。”马里奥告诉他，“你没有什么可宽恕的；就算有，也记住，老板娘不过是个女人。”

===Original===

They were standing in the pantry. Gian-Luca glared at Mario; then he noticed that his hair had greyed a little. He looked old and sad and tired; the coarse texture of his sock was showing through the slits across his shoe. Nor was he very clean; his white shirtfront was spotted and the buttons of his coat were stained and frayed. He fidgeted self-consciously under Gian-Luca’s eyes, and his hand went up to straighten his white necktie. They began collecting things; Mario could not find the napkins, he swore because the table-drawer had stuck; and presently he grumbled:

“It is hard, this life of ours, always standing, always running, always serving someone else. I feel at times as though I must go out and climb a mountain so as to look over something wide.”

Gian-Luca only grunted, and picking up a tray, he hurried off to set his luncheon tables.

Mario watched his youthful back disappearing through the door. “He is very young,” thought Mario, “he is very innocent﻿—yet I think he grows a little proud.”

But Gian-Luca at that moment felt anything but proud; all he wanted was to get away from Mario. He did not want to pity, to see any cause for pity﻿—and Mario was very pitiful.

===Chinese===

两人站在配餐室里。吉安-卢卡瞪着马里奥；忽然注意到他头发又灰了些。他显老、显愁、显倦；粗纱袜从鞋面的裂缝里露出来。他也不太干净了：白衬衫的前胸渍着斑斑点点，上衣的纽扣又渍又毛。他在吉安-卢卡的注视下不自在地扭动着，抬手去扶正白领结。他们开始收拾东西；马里奥找不着餐巾，桌子抽屉卡住了，他骂了一句；随后他咕哝道：

“苦啊，我们这份营生，整天站着，整天跑，整天伺候别人。有时候我真想出去爬一座山，好把什么宽宽的东西望一望。”

吉安-卢卡只哼了一声，端起托盘，赶去摆午餐的位子了。

马里奥望着他年轻的背影消失在门口。“他还年轻，”马里奥想，“他还天真——不过我看他有点骄傲起来了。”

可吉安-卢卡此刻哪里顾上骄傲；他一心只想躲开马里奥。他不愿去怜悯，不愿看见可怜之处——而马里奥实在可怜得很。

#### V / 五
===Original===

Gian-Luca’s worst times were in the afternoons, for he never quite knew whether to go home. If he sat beside the bar the Padrona did not come, but if he left the Capo he always thought that she might have come if he had remained. At home would be Teresa who detested the Padrona and said so between her rows of knitting. She would say it from the cash-desk, from the shop or from the parlor, whenever she could do so with discretion. In the shop there would be Fabio very jubilant and proud, relating for the hundredth time his interview with Millo. Rocca might look in a moment, slap Gian-Luca on the back and say; “*Ecco*, you are grand now, Generale!” Then Nerone, who was genuinely pleased about Gian-Luca, was forever stumping round to see his favorite. He loved the boy, and consequently only saw his virtues﻿—by contrast he detested Rosa’s children.

“You are lucky, you old brigand!” he said one day to Fabio. “Now my Rosa’s children are disgusting. That Geppe will not work and he steals my cigarettes, and when I leave he does not guard the shop. As for Berta, she is awful, she speaks only Cockney English and what she would have us think is French; our language is no longer good enough for her, it seems, and also she is very vain and ugly.”

===Chinese===

吉安-卢卡最难熬的是下午，因为他总拿不定主意回不回家。他若坐在酒吧旁，老板娘不来；可他若离开卡波，又总想着说不定他留下来她就来了。家里有特蕾莎，她厌恶老板娘，一边织着一行行的毛线一边说。她会从账台后面说、从铺子里说、从客厅里说，只要说得谨慎。铺子里有法比奥，欢天喜地、得意洋洋，第一百次重述他同米洛的会面。罗卡兴许过来坐坐，拍拍吉安-卢卡的背说：“瞧，你如今发达了，将军！”还有内罗内，他是真心替吉安-卢卡高兴，三天两头拄着腿过来瞧他的心肝。他疼这孩子，因而只看得见他的好处——相形之下，他恨透了罗莎的孩子们。

“你走运了，老强盗！”有一天他对法比奥说，“我罗莎那几个孩子才叫恶心。那个杰佩不肯干活，还偷我的烟，我一走他就不看铺子。至于贝尔塔，她糟透了，只会说伦敦土腔英语，还有她非要我们当作法语的东西；看来我们的语言已配不上她了，而且她又虚荣又丑。”

===Original===

He would ramble on and on, always cross, always complaining, and finally would try to pick a quarrel with his friend. “Your prices are too high﻿—you no longer sell good food﻿—that is why you grow so proud and rich, no doubt.” Sometimes Rosa appeared too, just to pass the time of day, and if it were a weekend she brought her Berta with her. Berta worked for Madame Germaine﻿—née Smith and wedded Bulgin﻿—who sold exclusive models in her shop near Wardour Street. Berta ran all the errands and took models home to clients﻿—one in every dozen costumes might, with any luck, be French. Berta was temperamental and thought she loved Gian-Luca; she was always making eyes﻿—enormous eyes. Her hair had grown more frizzy and, if possible, more black, and she wore it in a heavy pompadour. She refused to speak Italian, but had learnt a little French from the sisters at her convent school; this she tried hard to remember so that she could plague Nerone who loathed the French because about a hundred years before they had stolen those bronze stallions from St. Mark’s. For the rest she spoke in English; not the rather stilted English of Gian-Luca who had never really learnt to clip his words, but in the English of the workrooms; and she spoke it without accent, that is, of course without a foreign accent.

Gian-Luca loathed and feared her; she was always edging close, and he lived in mortal terror that one day she would kiss him. Her hand was always waiting to be squeezed or stroked or cuddled; he felt sure of this whenever he observed it. And so, when Berta came, which was as often as she could, Gian-Luca would rush back to the Capo. The bar would be deserted until the dinner-hour, still, it was at least a sanctuary from Berta.

===Chinese===

他就这样滔滔不绝，永远气恼，永远抱怨，最后总要找磎同朋友吵一架。“你的价钱太高——你卖的已不是好货——难怪你这样神气，这样发财。”有时罗莎也露露面，不过是问声好；要是赶上周末，就把贝尔塔也带来。贝尔塔在热尔梅娜太太手下做事——太太娘家姓史密斯，嫁了人姓布尔金——在沃杜尔街附近开一家专卖独一份时款的铺子。贝尔塔包揽跑腿，把成衣送到主顾家——运气好时，一打衣裳里兴许能有一件是法国货。贝尔塔喜怒无常，自以为爱着吉安-卢卡；总向他使眼色——好大好大的眼睛。她的头发比从前更卷，若是可能，也更黑了，沉沉地堆高在额前。她拒绝讲意大利语，却在修道院学校的修女那里学过一点法语；这点法语她拼命记着，好去折磨内罗内——内罗内恨法国人，因为约莫一百年前，他们偷走了圣马可教堂的青铜马。其余时候她讲英语；不是吉安-卢卡那种略显生硬的英语——他从没真正学会把字音削短——而是作坊里的英语；她说来不带口音，当然，是不带外国口音。

吉安-卢卡又厌又怕她；她总往他身边贴，他天天担惊受怕，唯恐哪天被她亲一口。她那只手永远等着人捏、人抚、人搂——他每次看见那只手就断定如此。因此贝尔塔一来——她来得能多勤就多勤——吉安-卢卡便飞奔回卡波。酒吧要到饭点才有人，但至少它是躲避贝尔塔的一处圣所。

#### VI / 六
===Original===

Gian-Luca dressed one morning with almost painful care; he was going to say goodbye to the Padrona. In two days’ time he would take up his new work, but this morning he was going to say goodbye. It had all seemed so queer and unreal the night before﻿—the pantry, the kitchen with its giant Moscatone, the long low restaurant, the chattering diners, the clanking of the glasses and bottles in the bar; and above all, he himself, in his shabby old dress-suit, writing orders, serving dishes, fetching drinks, striking matches. It had seemed as though his body did these duties out of habit, while he stood aside and thought of the Padrona.

The suit he wore this morning had been a present from Fabio, and very smart it was, an immaculate grey tweed.

Fabio had said: “I would not have you shabby before Millo. We wish him to observe that our little business prospers. Is that not so, Teresa?”

And she had nodded: “That is so.”

Gian-Luca stared at his reflection in the glass, and in spite of his heartache he approved of what he saw. He gave a final touch to his necktie and his hair, then, picking up his hat, he went downstairs.

“Do not be long,” called Fabio from the shop. “I must consult you﻿—I have certain business matters to discuss regarding Millo.”

Old Compton Street was foggy, there was mud on the pavement: Gian-Luca stooped and turned up his new trousers, for one part of him remembered that he wore expensive clothes. At Nerone’s door stood Rosa with her hair still done in curlers; she was shaking out a very dusty mat. She smiled broadly at Gian-Luca:

“How smart you look, *piccino*! What would Berta say, I wonder!” And she laughed.

Geppe peered across her shoulder, he looked spotty and unwashen. “He is lucky, not like me who have to stop at home,” he grumbled.

“Continue with your sweeping!” said Rosa, turning quickly. “You cannot have swept out half the shop.”

There were lights in Rocca’s window because it was so foggy. Rocca himself was moving in and out among his corpses. As Gian-Luca passed he saw him neatly slicing off a shoulder with a quick, experienced sweep of his knife. The whole street was very busy preparing for its business, which consisted of supplying other people’s daily needs.

“They need so much, so very much﻿—how funny!” thought Gian-Luca, who himself was only conscious of needing the Padrona.

===Chinese===

一天早晨，吉安-卢卡穿衣裳时仔细得近乎痛苦；他要去同老板娘道别。再过两天他便上新的差事了，可今天早晨，他要去道别。头天夜里，这一切显得那样古怪而不真切——配餐室，飘着大瓶莫斯卡托甜酒的厨房，那间低矮狭长的饭馆，喋喋不休的食客，酒吧里杯瓶的叮当；尤其是他自己，穿着那身破旧的礼服，写菜单、上菜、取酒、划火柴。仿佛他的身子出于习惯在做着这一切活计，他自己却站在一旁，想着老板娘。

他今早穿的这套衣裳是法比奥送的礼物，非常体面，一身无可挑剔的灰色粗花呢。

法比奥当时说：“我不能让米洛看见你寒酸。咱们要他瞧瞧，咱们这点买卖是兴旺的。是不是，特蕾莎？”

她点头：“是这样。”

吉安-卢卡凝望着镜子里的自己，尽管心里作痛，还是赞许所见的一切。他最后理了理领带和头发，然后拿起帽子下了楼。

“别去太久，”法比奥在铺子里喊，“我有话同你商量——关于米洛，有几桩生意上的事要谈。”

老康普顿街上雾蒙蒙的，人行道上带着泥；吉安-卢卡弯下腰，把新裤脚翻上来——他身上有一部分还记得自己穿着贵重衣裳。内罗内门口站着罗莎，头发还卷着卷子，正抖一领积灰的垫子。她朝吉安-卢卡咧嘴一笑：

“瞧你多神气，小不点儿！不知道贝尔塔见了会说什么！”说完她笑了。

杰佩从她肩后探出头来，一脸疙瘩，没洗过似的。“他真走运，不比我，只能闷在家里。”他嘟囔。

“接着扫你的地！”罗莎转身喝道，“你那半个铺子还没扫完呢。”

雾大，罗卡橱窗里点着灯。罗卡本人正在他的尸体中间进进出出。吉安-卢卡走过时，看见他手起刀落，利利索索片下一只肩。整条街都在忙忙碌碌地备各自的营生——无非是供给别人每日所需。

“他们要的真多，真多啊——多可笑！”吉安-卢卡想，他自己只觉得需要的，是老板娘。

===Original===

The Padrone was waiting for him when he reached the Capo; he was in a great hurry to go out.

“Here you are!” he said impatiently, glancing at his watch. “Be good enough to sign the wages book.” Then he held out his hand, turning affable, it seemed: “Well, I wish you good fortune. I am sorry to lose you, but when one is young one must think of oneself.” The Padrone did not wish to quarrel with Fabio, it was much too convenient to deal at his shop.

“May I see the Padrona?” faltered Gian-Luca.

“*Sicuro*, she will want to wish you luck. Gemma!” he called. “Gian-Luca is here.”

“Let him come up,” came a voice.

“Go up,” said the Padrone, “you know the way. I have to go to the City.” He shook hands once again and turned to the door. Gian-Luca went slowly up the stairs.

The Padrona was sitting on a low settee. The firelight fell on her thick coils of hair and slanted across her averted face, which was partially shielded by her hand. She did not look round, and Gian-Luca in the doorway stood watching her in silence for a moment, then he closed the door quietly and stepped into the room.

“It is I,” he said softly. “Gian-Luca.”

She nodded; and now she was looking at him, smiling very kindly, he thought. “I hope you will be happy, Gian-Luca,” she said, and then fell silent again.

A coal crashed into the grate and lay there smoking; the Padrona pushed it with her foot. Outside in the passage a cuckoo clock struck nine; the small, childish sound of it seemed to fill the room. Gian-Luca’s strange eyes were very wide open, his breath came a little fast. As he stood there he could hear the beating of his heart, he could hear the Padrona’s breathing. Then he drew himself up, he felt suddenly strong, he was filled with a knowledge of his manhood. He called her﻿—standing there motionless, he called her. “Gemma!” And then again, “Gemma!”

She got up and stared blankly at him for a moment as though bewildered﻿—uncertain. There was something very like appeal in her face﻿—he saw it and his heart thumped with triumph.

“Gemma!” His voice was loud and compelling, and after he had called her he smiled. Then all of a sudden she was lying in his arms, giving him back his kisses.

As they stood there the years came down on the boy and dropped away from the woman; they were just two creatures welded into one by an impulse of mutual passion.

===Chinese===

他到卡波时，老板正等着他，急着要出门。

“你可来了！”他不耐烦地说，看了看表，“劳驾在工钱簿上签个字。”随即伸出手来，似乎变得和气了：“好吧，祝你走运。你走了我可惜，可人年轻，总得为自己打算。”老板不愿同法比奥闹翻——在他铺子里记账赊货，实在方便得很。

“我能见见老板娘吗？”吉安-卢卡讷讷地问。

“当然，她也要祝你交好运的。杰玛！”他喊道，“吉安-卢卡来了。”

“叫他上来。”一个声音说。

“上去吧，”老板说，“路你认得。我得进城去。”他又握了握手，转身出门。吉安-卢卡慢慢走上楼梯。

老板娘坐在一张矮榻上。炉火照着她浓密的发髻，斜斜掠过她偏着的脸，那脸有一半掩在手里。她没有回头。吉安-卢卡立在门口，默默望了她片刻，然后轻轻带上门，走进屋里。

“是我，”他柔声说，“吉安-卢卡。”

她点点头；此刻她望着他了，还——他想——十分和蔼地笑着。“希望你幸福，吉安-卢卡。”她说，随后又不作声了。

一块煤哗啦塌进炉膛，躺在那里冒烟；老板娘用脚把它拨了拨。外面过道里，一座布谷鸟钟敲了九下；那细弱的、孩子气的声音仿佛涨满了整间屋子。吉安-卢卡那双奇异的眼睛睁得老大，呼吸有些急促。站在那里，他听得见自己的心跳，也听得见老板娘的呼吸。随后他挺直了身子，忽然觉得自己强壮，浑身充溢着对自己男子气概的认知。他唤她——一动不动地站在那里，他唤她。“杰玛！”接着又是一声，“杰玛！”

她站起身，茫然盯了他片刻，像是给弄糊涂了——拿不定主意。她脸上有一种极像哀恳的神情——他看见了，心房得意得直撞。

“杰玛！”他的声音响亮而逼人，唤过之后，他笑了。突然间，她已躺在他怀里，把吻还给他。

他们站在那里，岁月压上了这少年，又从这女人身上褪去；他们只是两个生灵，被一阵共同的情热熔成了一个。

===Original===

He began to speak hotly: “My beautiful! My joy! I will never let you go any more. All my life I have waited, and now I have got you﻿—I am all burning up with this loving.” Then his mood changed, his eyes filled with sudden tears. “You are holy,” he whispered softly. “I must treat you very gently﻿—you are little and weak, and you need Gian-Luca because you are so little.”

But something, perhaps it was the sound of his whispering, made her stiffen as she lay in his arms. With a cry of anger she pushed him away.

“Stop!” she said shrilly. “Stop!”

And then the Padrona sinned in her fear as she had not sinned in her passion. In her fear she struck out wildly like an animal at bay; she accused, she insulted, she degraded. She might have made an appeal to his goodness, to the chivalry that was in him, to that great, shy, virginal spirit of youth that was wrestling with him even now. But instead she confronted him, flushed and disheveled, with the crude, ugly words on her lips.

“Go!” she said finally, pointing to the door. “Go﻿—or I tell my husband!”

And he went, without one more look at the Padrona. He was utterly bewildered, incredulous and outraged. Alone in her room the Padrona wept bitterly, but Gian-Luca’s eyes were quite dry.

===Chinese===

他热烈地说起话来：“我的美人！我的欢乐！我再也不放你走了。我等了一辈子，如今你是我的人了——这爱把我浑身都烧着了。”随后他情绪一变，眼里涌上泪水。“你是神圣的，”他轻轻耳语，“我得万分温柔地待你——你这样小，这样弱，你缺不了吉安-卢卡，就因为你这样小。”

可是不知什么——也许是那耳语声——使她在他怀里僵住了。她愤怒地喊了一声，把他推开。

“住手！”她尖声说，“住手！”

于是老板娘在恐惧中犯下的罪，比她在情热中犯下的更重。恐惧中的她像困兽一样乱扑乱咬；她控诉，她辱骂，她作践自己。她本可以唤他的善良，唤他心中的侠气，唤那此刻仍在同他挣扎的、巨大、腼腆、童贞的青春之魂。可她没有，她红着脸、披散着头发站在他面前，嘴边尽是粗鄙丑恶的字眼。

“走！”她最后说，指着门，“走——不然我告诉我丈夫！”

他走了，再没看老板娘一眼。他全然糊涂了，不能相信，满腔愤懑。老板娘独自在房里失声痛哭，吉安-卢卡的眼睛却干干的。

#### VII / 七
===Original===

That night Gian-Luca returned to the Capo, but this time he did not go in. He hid like a thief in the opposite doorway, watching for the waiters to leave. Mario came first; that was good. He was glad. He shrank farther into the shadow, but Mario passed him with never a glance and went limping away down the street. Then came the new waiter accompanied by the boy; they said goodnight and the waiter caught a bus. The boy looked round him then lit a cigarette﻿—obviously one that he had stolen﻿—after which he too walked away down the street, whistling softly between puffs.

In an upper window of the Capo a light showed﻿—that was the Padrona’s sitting-room. Gian-Luca watched it, and as he did so he was filled with a queer, ugly sense of pleasure. He was glad that the light should stream out between the curtains; it meant that the Padrona was near; and he thought of what he was about to do and felt glad that she should be near him. A door opened and shut, Schmidt came across the road﻿—he was now all but touching Gian-Luca.

“Schmidt!”

“*Nun was!*” Schmidt jumped as though frightened. “Oh, hallo! Is that you, Gian-Luca?”

Gian-Luca jingled the money in his pockets, then he slipped his arm into Schmidt’s. “Will you take me to see those girls?” said Gian-Luca, and he turned his face up to the lighted window.

“*Ach so!*” murmured Schmidt. “You make up your mind﻿—come along then, I understand.”

===Chinese===

那天夜里，吉安-卢卡又回到卡波，可这一回他没有进去。他像贼一样藏在对面门洞里，等着侍者们出来。马里奥先出来；这很好，他高兴。他往阴影里又缩了缩，可马里奥一眼也没朝他望，一瘸一拐地顺街走远了。接着是新来的侍者，同那小伙计一道出来；两人道了晚安，侍者赶上一辆公共汽车。小伙计四下望了望，点上一支烟——明摆着是偷来的——随后也一边喷着烟、一边轻轻吹着口哨顺街走远了。

卡波楼上一扇窗里亮着灯——那是老板娘的起居室。吉安-卢卡望着它，心头涌起一种古怪而丑恶的快感。他高兴那灯光从窗帘间漏出来；这说明老板娘就在近处；他想到自己就要做的事，又高兴她就在自己近旁。一扇门开了又关上，施密特穿过马路走来——此刻他离吉安-卢卡只有一步。

“施密特！”

“嗯，怎么！”施密特吓得跳起来。“哦，喂！是你吗，吉安-卢卡？”

吉安-卢卡把口袋里的钱摇得叮当响，随即挽起施密特的胳膊。“你带我去看那些姑娘好不好？”吉安-卢卡说，同时仰起脸朝那扇亮灯的窗望去。

“啊，这样啊！”施密特低声道，“你拿定主意了——那就走吧，我懂。”

## Book / 第二部
===Original===

II

===Chinese===

第二部

### I / 一
===Original===



===Chinese===

一

#### I / 一
===Original===

Great changes had come to the Casa Boselli. It was now six years since that eventful day when Millo had ordered his first case of funghi. For six years now Gian-Luca had served him and meanwhile the Casa Boselli had prospered. A large plate-glass window had been recently added, and the lease of the next-door shop had been purchased. A green motor-van with the name in gold letters﻿—“Casa Boselli”﻿—painted large on its sides, might be seen any morning unloading its wares at the back doors of fashionable restaurants. Fabio was no longer permitted to serve; there were three young assistants for that. Obedient in all things to the wishes of Teresa, Fabio dressed himself neatly and wandered about in and out of the shop, empty-handed and foolish; sad too, missing his salami and cheeses, while Teresa sat in a businesslike office in the basement of the new shop next door.

The years were dealing lightly with Teresa; she had scarcely changed at all in appearance, nor had she softened towards life in general; one thing only could bring a smile now to her lips, and that was the success of some new business venture, and of such a venture she was thinking one morning as she sat at her crowded desk. The May sunshine filtered down through the thick, greenish skylight and partially illumined the room, but Teresa switched on her reading-lamp and pulled a sheaf of papers towards her.

“*Duecento cinquanta sterline*,” she murmured, “two hundred and fifty pounds﻿—” then she stretched out a hand and groped for her passbook, “and five hundred pounds we have borrowed from the bank﻿—that makes seven hundred and fifty.” She began making long calculations on the blotter. “And we save on the freight?” She considered a moment, then she opened a drawer and looked over some bills. “Ah,” she said smiling, “it is just as I thought, I am scarcely a centesimo out.”

===Chinese===

博塞利商店（Casa Boselli）发生了巨大的变化。自米洛（Millo）订下第一箱蘑菇的那个重大日子起，已经过去六年了。六年来吉安-卢卡一直伺候他，这期间博塞利商店兴旺起来。新近添了一扇大块平板玻璃橱窗，又买下了隔壁店面的租约。一辆绿色送货汽车，车身两侧用金字漆着大大的「Casa Boselli」，每天早上都能见到它在时髦餐馆的后门卸货。法比奥（Fabio）不再被允许伺候顾客了，那活计交给了三个年轻伙计。法比奥事事听特蕾莎的，穿得整整齐齐，空着两手，傻乎乎地在店里店外踱来踱去；还有点伤心——他惦记他的萨拉米香肠和奶酪呢——而特蕾莎（Teresa）则坐在隔壁新店地下的一间办公室里办公。

岁月对特蕾莎格外宽厚；她的模样几乎全然没变，待世事的心肠也丝毫没有软下来；如今唯有一件事能引她唇边露出笑意，就是某桩新生意的得手。一天早晨，她坐在堆满文件的书桌前，想的正是这样一桩生意。五月的阳光透过厚而发绿的玻璃天棚滤下来，勉强照亮了屋子，特蕾莎还是打开台灯，把一叠单据拉到面前。

「duecento cinquanta sterline，」她喃喃道，「二百五十镑——」她伸手摸出银行存折，「加上从银行借来的五百镑——一共七百五十。」她在吸墨纸上做起长长的演算。「运费上还能省一笔？」她盘算片刻，拉开抽屉翻看几张账单。「啊，」她微笑着说，「和我想的一分不差，一个分尼索（centesimo）都不差。」

===Original===

Teresa, who had saved money all her life, had begun to spend recklessly of late. Thrifty to a fault, there yet lurked within her the gambler’s instinct﻿—she was gambling in business, emboldened by recent successes. And now she was launching the greatest venture of her whole long business career, a venture hidden away out of sight in a room behind the new shop. It was nothing less than the making of pasta; the mixing and rolling and cutting and drying and tinting of excellent, freshly-made pasta, a thing that had never been attempted before in London, or indeed in England. On her latest price-list there appeared these words: “The Casa Boselli will make your macaroni; no need to eat it many months old, we make it fresh every day!” And Teresa smiled gently whenever she read them; her smile was possessive, maternal even, for her heart that had gone so long empty and childless had taken to itself the Casa Boselli. The machinery required for the making of pasta had had to be imported from Milan; that was what had taken the extra money, so much money indeed that Fabio trembled whenever he thought about it.

“We spend!” he said faintly from time to time.

“And we earn,” his wife replied firmly. “One must always strike while the iron is hot; our iron is hot so I strike.” There were nights, however, when Fabio could not sleep for thinking of that debt to the bank.

“Are we not going to pay it off?” smiled Teresa. “You grow old, my Fabio, you grow old and afraid. Now I am not young, yet I am not afraid; I drink little, I work hard, I am always on the watch, and above all my ears are open. Millo says: ‘The pasta is such a trouble, I would like them to eat it fresh; I am more than a little ashamed of my pasta. If only the English could make macaroni! But no doubt they would make it very badly.’ Then I say to Millo: ‘You shall have your fresh pasta; the Casa Boselli will make it.’ And that,” she would conclude, “is the genius for business; that is why we now deliver our goods in a motor instead of with a horse and cart.”

===Chinese===

特蕾莎一辈子省吃俭用，近来却大手大脚地花了起来。她节俭得过了头，骨子里却潜藏着赌徒的本能——她在生意场上赌，连番得手壮了她的胆。如今她正干她漫长经商生涯里最大的一桩买卖，一桩藏在新店后面一间屋子里的买卖，不显山不露水。不是别的，正是做意面（pasta）：和面、压面、切面、晾面、上色，做上好的新鲜意面——这在伦敦、乃至整个英格兰，都是前人没有尝试过的。她最新的价目单上印着这样的话：「Casa Boselli为您现做通心粉；不必再吃存放了好几个月的陈货，我们每天现做新鲜的！」每次读到这几句，特蕾莎就温柔地微笑；那笑是据为己有的笑，甚至是母亲式的笑——她那颗久已空落无出的心，早已把博塞利商店当成了自己的孩子。做意面需用的机器不得不从来兰进口；额外花掉的正是这笔钱，钱花得那么多，法比奥一想起来就发抖。

「我们这是在花钱哪！」他不时虚弱地说。

「我们也在挣钱，」妻子斩钉截铁地回答，「打铁总要趁热；我们的铁正热，所以我打。」可是有多少个夜晚，法比奥想着银行那笔债，翻来覆去睡不着。

「咱们还不还得上吗？」特蕾莎笑答，「你老了，我的法比奥，人老了就怕事。我也不年轻了，可我不怕；我少喝酒，我肯干，我时刻留神，最要紧是耳朵灵。米洛说：『进货的意面太麻烦，我巴不得客人吃新鲜的；说起我用的意面，我自己都有几分羞愧。要是英国人会做通心粉就好了！不过他们准做得一塌糊涂。』我就对米洛说：『您会有新鲜意面的；Casa Boselli来做。』」说到这里她总要收尾，「这就是做生意的天分；这就是如今咱们送货用汽车、不用马车的原因。」

===Original===

And truly she possessed a great genius for business, as her friends were all bound to admit. Nerone, Rocca, the Padrone of the Capo, Mario and Rosa were all lost in admiration; even Francesco Millo would smile and call her the Napoleon of the Salumeria. Only poor Fabio, in his stuffy black coat, would sigh a little for the past, when he had handled his salami and cheeses, when the money had all lain snugly in the bank instead of uneasily in plate-glass windows, new leases, and machinery brought from Milan.

“*Si, si*,” he would think, “it was more peaceful then, and Teresa and I grow old.”

But on this May morning Teresa felt young as she got up briskly from her desk. “I will go and inspect my little factory,” she murmured, and, climbing the stairs, she passed through the shop and into a room beyond.

She stood quite still just inside the doorway to enjoy this, her latest acquisition. Near the ceiling were purposeful, whirring wheels, and the sound of their whirring was as music to her ears. At a table in the corner a youth in white drill was mixing a mountain of flour. Sixty pounds he would mix, with an egg to each pound, and from time to time he must pause to wash his hands, a rule imposed by Teresa. The great, generous mixture came up to his elbows as he kneaded and stirred and pressed.

“*Va bene*,” said Teresa, and she smiled with approval. “*Va bene*, but be careful of the eggshells.”

As soon as a portion of the pasta was mixed it was fed to a rotund machine, and there it was pummeled and kneaded afresh until it was ready for the large, wooden rollers. This process of rolling fascinated Teresa; she would gladly have watched it for hours. In went a shapeless lump of the pasta, and out came a species of rubber sheeting, cool to the touch and flawless in texture. Then back it would go to be rolled yet again, with each fresh rolling to grow finer and thinner, until in the end it was almost transparent, so elegant had it become.

Teresa would sometimes whisper about it. “One hundred times it must pass,” she would whisper; “one hundred times it must pass through my rollers?” And then she would laugh a little to herself, thinking of this great new adventure.

===Chinese===

她也确实有做生意的天分，这是她的朋友们不得不承认的。内罗内（Nerone）、罗卡（Rocca）、卡波（Capo）餐厅的老板（Padrone）、马里奥（Mario）和罗莎（Rosa）个个叹服得五体投地；连弗朗切斯科·米洛都会笑着称她为「熟食铺里的拿破仑」。只有可怜的法比奥，裹着他那件气闷的黑外套，为往昔轻轻叹气——那时候他经手自己的萨拉米和奶酪，那时候钱稳稳当当地躺在银行里，而不是悬在平板玻璃橱窗、新租约和米兰运来的机器上。

「si, si，」他想，「那时候清闲多了，而且特蕾莎和我也都老了。」

可是这个五月的早晨，特蕾莎觉得自己年轻。她利落地从书桌后站起来。「我去看看我的小工厂。」她喃喃说着，拾级而上，穿过店面，走进后面一间屋子。

她在门口静静站住，欣赏这桩她最新的产业。天花板附近，轮子嗡嗡地各司其职，那嗡嗡声在她耳中如同音乐。角落里一张桌子旁，一个穿白斜纹布的青年正在和一大座面粉山。他要和六十磅面，一磅面配一只蛋，还得时时停下来洗手——这是特蕾莎定下的规矩。那慷慨的大面团一直没到他胳膊肘，他揉着、搅着、压着。

「va bene，」特蕾莎说，赞许地笑了，「va bene，可当心蛋壳。」

面和好一部分，就送进一台滚圆的机器，在那里再受一顿捶打揉压，直到可以上大木压辊。这道压面的工序看得特蕾莎着迷，她能一口气看上去几个钟头。一团不成形状的意面进去，出来的是一匹橡胶般的面片，摸上去凉凉的，质地毫无瑕斑。然后它再回去压，每压一遍就变得更细更薄，到最后几乎透明，雅致到了极点。

特蕾莎有时会为这个悄悄念叨。「得过一百遍，」她悄声说，「得从我的压辊里过一百遍。」然后自己轻轻笑一阵，想着这桩了不起的新冒险。

===Original===

There were other contrivances in that room, among them an uncannily intelligent machine for the cutting and molding of pasta. What was your pleasure? Bircorni, Conchiglie, Stelline? Just touch a particular gadget and presto!﻿—your etherealized rubber sheeting assumed any one of the fifty odd shapes that your need or your fancy had dictated. Upstairs would be waiting those new electric fans that sent out a stream of cold air, whereby your *bicorni*, *conchiglie* or *stelline* would be hardened and rendered almost immortal﻿—that is until they were eaten.

Teresa’s defiant black eyes were glowing; they had no need of glasses to help their sight. “You have dropped in a trifle of eggshell, Francesco!” she said suddenly, pointing an accusing finger at the youth with the mountain of flour.

Those who worked for Teresa were always Italian. “The English do not work, they spend,” she would say; and her work-people feared her intensely, but respected. She was everywhere at once with her terrible black eyes, yet although her tongue lashed them they gave of their best. They said behind her back: “*Che donna maravigliosa!*” A grand old woman they thought Teresa, and one who knew well how to drive a hard bargain, as they would have done in her place.

===Chinese===

那间屋里还有些别的巧妙装置，其中有一台切面、压花样的机器，灵巧得近乎妖异。您想要什么花样？比科尔尼（bicorni）、孔基列（conchiglie）、斯泰利内（stelline）？只消碰一下某个机件，嗖——您那仙气飘飘的橡胶面片就变成了五几十种形状里您中意或您幻想指定的任何一种。楼上候着的是新装的电扇，吐出一股股冷风，您的比科尔尼、孔基列或斯泰利内经它一吹便坚硬起来，几乎万寿无疆——也就是说，直到被人吃掉为止。

特蕾莎那双倔强的黑眼睛灼灼发光；它们不需要眼镜帮忙。「你掉进去一星蛋壳，弗朗切斯科！」她突然说，指控的手指直指面粉山旁的青年。

给特蕾莎干活的从来都是意大利人。「英国人不干活，他们只会花钱，」她说；她的伙计们怕她怕得要命，却也敬她。她那双可怕的黑眼睛无处不在，舌头虽然抽得他们生疼，他们却个个拿出最好的活计。他们背地里说：「che donna maravigliosa!（多了不起的女人！）」他们觉得特蕾莎是位了不起的老太太，而且深谙狠狠杀价之道——换了他们处在她的位置也会这么干。

#### II / 二
===Original===

Nerone was less fortunate in his affairs, a fact for which he blamed Geppe. Geppe was a lazy and insolent young man with a predilection for philandering. He hated the shop though he liked its contents, to which he helped himself freely. This so much enraged the miserly Nerone that he would actually threaten to send for the police; then Rosa would weep and implore forgiveness for her plump and unsatisfactory offspring.

“I spare him this time, but the next time I send,” Nerone would babble in a fury. “This is all Mario’s fault; he was always a fool; he has spoilt the young, idling ruffian!”

Grandsire and grandson hated each other, and their feuds robbed the house of all peace; for Geppe could bellow much louder than Nerone﻿—youth gave him a laryngeal advantage.

Geppe, who was now nearly nineteen years old, wished to be a commercial traveler. This struck him as a pleasant, safe way to see life, and one that would release him from the shop. He had met a young commercial traveler one day through a friend of Berta’s at Madame Germaine’s; a very smart fellow with plenty of money and a staggering knowledge of the world. Geppe of course had little to spend﻿—only what Mario could give him﻿—and being the grandson of Nerone, it was natural that he thought a great deal about money. But unlike Nerone, and herein lay the trouble, he liked it for what it could buy; to keep it in a till or to send it to a bank seemed to Geppe the height of all foolishness, and he said so when Nerone was listening.

===Chinese===

内罗内的家事却不那么顺遂，他把这归罪于杰佩（Geppe）。杰佩是个又懒又浑的年轻人，专好拈花惹草。他恨这家铺子，却喜欢铺子里的东西，随手就拿。这把吝啬的内罗内气得要命，甚至真会嚷嚷着要叫警察；于是罗莎便哭哭啼啼，替她那个虚胖而不成器的儿子求饶。

「这次我饶了他，下次我就叫人。」内罗内气得语无伦次，「都怪马里奥，他一向是个傻瓜，把这个游手好闲的小无赖给惯坏了！」

祖父和孙子彼此憎恨，他们的争斗闹得全家不得安生；因为杰佩嚎起来比内罗内响亮得多——年轻给了他喉咙上的优势。

杰佩如今快十九岁了，想做个旅行推销员。在他看来这是个又舒服又稳妥的见世面的路子，还能把他从铺子里解脱出来。有一天，他借贝尔塔（Berta）的朋友引见，在热尔梅娜夫人（Madame Germaine）店里认识了一个年轻的旅行推销员；那人穿得漂漂亮亮，钱多得淌油，见识广得吓人。杰佩当然没什么可花的——只有马里奥偶尔给的几个钱——而且身为内罗内的孙子，他天生就把钱看得很重。可他跟内罗内又不一样——毛病正出在这里——他爱钱是为了钱能换来东西；把钱锁进钱柜或存进银行，在杰佩看来是愚蠢透顶，而且他当着内罗内的面也这么说过。

===Original===

Nerone had refused point-blank to pay him wages, and this was a very sore point. Geppe would have liked to run away to sea﻿—he had read of such things in his paper-backed “shockers”﻿—but whenever he thought of the sea for too long he invariably felt rather sick. He had many wonderful adventures in his mind, but his body shrank weakly from hardships. He was soft; his skin was now colorless and flabby, his hands would easily blister; woolen vests made him itch; and now, when he shaved, the razor brought up little pimples. He was lazy with youth and would lie long abed; when he did appear at last it would be yawning. In the evening, however, he was as wakeful as an owl. In the evening he would go to a cinematograph, a form of entertainment which he liked above all others. He would sit in the darkness and watch desperate deeds, with a pleasant conviction of safety. And sometimes, if he found the heroine attractive, he would conjure up all sorts of amorous scenes in which he himself was the hero. Geppe was greedy, he still loved jam tartlets, he also loved chocolate creams; he would stroll about eating the latter while smoking, a perversion of the palate that disgusted Nerone.

“*Che bestia!*” he would mutter, unable to resist a morbid desire to look. And if Geppe noticed his grandfather looking, he would open his mouth and show chocolate creams in the process of mastication.

Nerone said that his grandson’s place was behind the counter of the shop; as for wages, what was the good of wages? The shop might be Geppe’s one day. He also said that cinemas were evil and encouraged immoral behavior. Now Geppe was longing to be immoral, his difficulty was to find a partner in sin. He would often try to borrow money from Gian-Luca, who must obviously be very rich; but Gian-Luca would find an excuse for not lending.

“He is mean,” thought Geppe in bitterness of spirit. “He himself is always after women.”

Mario was anxious, inadequate and pious, for he felt that his sins might be finding him out; he remembered those bygone halcyon days, when Rosa had had cause to be jealous of the barmaid﻿—and many other things did Mario remember that Rosa had never known.

“He is like me and yet he is not,” mourned Mario, “for I at least never feared hard work﻿—and yet it is natural for a boy to want money﻿—I think it is time the Babbo paid him something.”

===Chinese===

内罗内一口回绝了给他工钱，这成了杰佩一块最疼的心病。杰佩本想逃到海上去当水手——他在那些平装本的惊险小说里读到过这种事——可只要一想到海想得太久，他就准会犯恶心。他脑子里历险无数，身子却弱弱地退避苦难。他娇气；皮肤苍白松弛，手极易磨出泡；毛线背心叫他那痒；如今刮脸时，刀片还会刮出一粒粒小疙瘩。他年轻而懒，赖在床上的工夫很长；好容易露面时，总是打着哈欠。可一到晚上，他又醒得像只猫头鹰。晚上他去看电影，这是他顶顶喜欢的消遣。他坐在黑暗里看那些亡命的勾当，怀着一种安全感，颇为受用。有时女主角若中他的意，他还会在脑子里编出种种旖旎的场面，自己充当英雄。杰佩贪嘴，仍旧爱吃果酱小塔，也爱吃奶油巧克力；他常一边抽着烟一边溜达着吃后者——这种口味的倒错叫内罗内恶心。

「che bestia!（畜生！）」他嘟囔着，却忍不住要多看几眼的病态欲望。杰佩若察觉祖父在看，就张开嘴，把嚼到一半的奶油巧克力露给他看。

内罗内说，孙子的位置在铺子柜台后面；至于工钱，要工钱干什么？铺子早晚是杰佩的。他还说，电影院是邪恶的，会教人伤风败俗。偏偏杰佩正巴望着伤风败俗，难的是找不到同伙。他常常想向吉安-卢卡借钱——此人必定很有钱；可吉安-卢卡总能找出推托的理由。

「小气鬼，」杰佩心里恨恨地想，「他自己倒一天到晚追女人。」

马里奥又焦心，又无能，又虔诚——他觉得自己的罪孽快要找上门来；他记得那些金色的往日，罗莎曾为酒馆女招待吃过醋——马里奥还记得许多事，是罗莎从来不曾知道的。

「他像我，可又不像，」马里奥悲叹，「至少我从来不怕吃苦——不过孩子想要钱也是天性——我看是时候让老爸给他点钱了。」

===Original===

But neither he nor Rosa dared anger Nerone, who still had to contribute towards their support, for Mario’s wages at the Capo had not risen, though the Capo was rising every day. All sorts of great people now dined at the Capo and got drunk on its excellent liquor. There was Munster the painter, and Jenkins the sculptor, and their wives and their women and their models and their offspring, to say nothing of a certain broad-minded countess who had reincarnated from a Babylonian suburb﻿—there was also the poet who had once admired Gian-Luca and who, having lately married, had become very rich. He no longer wrote poems, his wife fed him too well﻿—in the winter he wore a magnificent coat with a collar of Russian sable. But of him be it said that he was faithful to the Capo﻿—grateful, let us hope, for past favors﻿—for he brought many friends to eat costly dinners, and his wife always seemed to have his purse in her bag.

Oh, but many grand people now rejoiced the Padrone, who was somewhat less fierce than he had been. The best cure for bad temper is prosperity, of course﻿—what a pity that we cannot all be prosperous! And then there was now something living upstairs, a tiny, red, bawling Padroncino; a thing with eyes the color of gentians, and lungs that left no manner of doubt regarding its paternity. Whenever its father could spare a minute he would rush upstairs and yell: “Bimbo!” Or perhaps he would tickle, when the small Padroncino would respond with such vigor that Munster would look up from his tumbler of brandy and smile a large smile. For if Munster loved women he also loved babies﻿—which in his case was fortunate, perhaps.

There were now red silk curtains at the windows of the Capo, and Mario began to think himself a prophet, as indeed he had been in all but one thing, and that was his own promotion. The Padrone had now four waiters in all, but as yet he had no head waiter, so that Mario continued to live in hopes. But his present circumstances did not warrant interference with Nerone on behalf of his son.

“After all,” he told Rosa, “the time is approaching when Geppe will have to go to Italy to serve. His military service will make a man of him, and when he returns we will then talk to Babbo﻿—at the moment I think it unwise.”

===Chinese===

可他和罗莎谁也不敢惹内罗内——他们还得靠内罗内贴补家用，因为马里奥在卡波的工钱一直没涨，而卡波却一天比一天红火。如今各路大人物都来卡波用餐，喝它上好的酒喝到酩酊。有画家蒙斯特（Munster），雕塑家詹金斯（Jenkins），还有他们的太太、情妇、模特和儿女，更不必说某位思想开通的伯爵夫人——她是从巴比伦郊区转世来的——还有那位曾经赏识过吉安-卢卡的诗人，此人新近结了婚，变得非常有钱。他不再写诗了，妻子把他喂得太好——冬天他穿一件俄罗斯貂皮领的华贵大衣。不过得替他说句公道话：他对卡波很忠实——但愿是出于对往日恩惠的感激——因为他带许多朋友来吃昂贵的晚餐，而他的钱包似乎总在太太的手袋里。

哦，多少显贵如今都让老板欢天喜地，他也不像从前那么凶了。发财当然是坏脾气的特效药——可惜我们不能人人都发财！何况如今楼上还多了个活物，一位小小的、红彤彤、嗓门嘹亮的小少爷（Padroncino）；那双眼睛是龙胆草的颜色，那副肺活量把他的血统交代得毫无悬念。做父亲的但凡得一刻空闲，就冲上楼去喊：「bimbo（宝宝）！」或者去呵痒，小少爷便拼命回应，惊得蒙斯特从他那杯白兰地上抬起头来，咧开大嘴笑。因为蒙斯特爱女人，也爱婴儿——在他这情形，或许倒是件幸事。

卡波的窗上如今挂起红丝窗帘，马里奥开始觉得自己是个先知——事实上除了一件事他全都料中了，那就是他自己的升迁。老板如今总共雇了四个侍者，却还没有一个领班，于是马里奥继续生活在希望里。但以他眼下的境况，还不便为儿子的事去顶撞内罗内。

「再说，」他告诉罗莎，「杰佩回意大利服役的日子快到了。兵役会把他炼成个男子汉，等他回来，咱们再跟老爸谈——眼下我觉得还不是时候。」

===Original===

And Rosa would often say to her son: “It is splendid to think of my Geppe as a soldier﻿—how I envy you, *caro*, to see Italy again! But then, I was forgetting, you have never yet seen it.”

Geppe would look sulky and mutter something vague about wanting to see a bit of life, not service; for the last thing on earth that poor Geppe wanted was to become a soldier. Rocca, who knew that the hour was approaching, added greatly to Geppe’s torment. He would come to Nerone’s and buy cigarettes for the pleasure he took in talking at Geppe and making him feel afraid. Rocca would jab at the air with his stick:

“It is thus, and thus, with the bayonet,” he would say, “and the little, sharp twist in the pit of the stomach; one should always aim low for their bellies.”

Geppe’s pale face would turn even paler, and his hand would instinctively grip at his middle. Then Rocca would laugh:

“*Avanti, capitano!* I can see you leading your men into battle. ‘Italia! Italia! Italia!’ you shout, and then you give the small twist with your sword, for surely they will make you a captain!”

===Chinese===

罗莎则常对儿子说：「一想到我的杰佩要当兵就美得很——亲爱的（caro），我多羡慕你，又要见到意大利了！——哦对了，瞧我忘了，你还没见过它呢。」

杰佩沉着脸，含糊地嘟囔什么想见见世面，不是去服役；因为天底下可怜的杰佩最不想干的事就是当兵。罗卡知道日子临近了，更大大添了杰佩的苦恼。他常到内罗内的铺子来买烟，为的是拿话敲打杰佩、看他害怕的乐趣。罗卡会用手杖朝空气里戳刺：

「就这样，就这样，用刺刀，」他说，「照着心窝里那么利落地一拧；记着，永远瞄低点，照他们的肚子上招呼。」

杰佩苍白的脸愈发惨白，手不由自主地捂住肚子。罗卡便大笑：

「avanti, capitano!（前进，上尉！）我都看得见你率队冲锋的样子了。『意大利！意大利！意大利！』你这么喊着，然后拿军刀那么一拧——他们准会升你当上尉！」

#### III / 三
===Original===

It was all most distressing, especially for Mario, who was more overworked than ever at the Capo; and then there was Berta, not much of a comfort either, although she was now twenty-two. Berta no longer carried boxes and ran errands; she was now very smart and served in the shop. Madame Germaine thought the world of Berta, who could always persuade a woman of fifty that she looked like nineteen in a model.

“Oh, modom, you look charming!” Berta would lie, skillfully patting and tweaking. “Too stout? Oh, no, modom, I cannot agree﻿—this model gives such long lines.”

That was the way Berta talked in the shop; with her own special cronies it was different. “Damned old fools, if you saw them!” giggled Berta. “Heaving their stomachs up to their chins till they look like a lot of pouter pigeons!”

Berta herself had become quite slim owing to rigorous fasting; her fasts had nothing to do with the Lord, they were purely an offering to Venus. Berta had now many young lady friends who, like her, used lipstick and giggled. Their Sundays were spent on the river in summer; they were usually accompanied by one or two “boys.” Berta was young, she loved a good time, and she worked very hard all the week, so it soon came about that she missed Mass on Sundays﻿—a new outrage to rouse up Nerone.

“Why you not go very early?” inquired Rosa. “The Mass he go on from six.”

“Good heavens!” laughed Berta. “I can’t get up at five﻿—I’m dog-tired, anyhow, by Sunday.”

Rosa sighed; she was racking her brain for words which never came correctly in English. But Berta refused point-blank to speak Italian; she declared that she had almost forgotten it. This placed her mother at a great disadvantage, as Berta was very well aware; she was fond of her mother, but she loved her own way, and she found it much easier to get it in English.

“You who were teached by the sisters and all, and you who are a child of Mary,” wailed Rosa.

“Well, I can’t help that, it wasn’t my fault,” said Berta with disrespect.

Nerone had decided to be dumb with Berta; he ignored her existence, for which everyone was thankful. She was sharper than Geppe, and just once or twice she had got the better of her grandsire. He and Rosa and Mario would go off to Mass, dragging the discontented Geppe. Geppe was terribly bored with his Church, but was fettered by a firm belief in Hell.

===Chinese===

这一切都烦心得很，尤其是对马里奥，他在卡波的活计比以往更重了；再说还有贝尔塔——她也不大能给人安慰，虽然已经二十二了。贝尔塔不再搬箱子跑腿了；如今她打扮得漂漂亮亮，站柜台。热尔梅娜夫人把贝尔塔看重得了不得，她总能哄得一位五十岁的太太相信，自己穿上某件新款像十九岁。

「哦，太太，您穿着多好看！」贝尔塔一面熟练地又拍又扯，一面撒谎，「嫌胖？哦，不，太太，我不同意——这款式线条多修长。」

贝尔塔在店里是这样说话；跟她那些小姐妹私下里就是另一套了。「一帮该死的老糊涂，你见了就知道了！」贝尔塔咯咯地笑，「把肚子挺到下巴颏，活像一群球胸鸽！」

贝尔塔自己倒瘦下来了，靠的是严格的斋戒；她的斋戒跟天主毫无干系，纯粹是献给维纳斯的祭礼。贝尔塔如今有许多年轻的女友，同她一样涂口红、爱咯咯笑。夏天的礼拜天，她们在河上消磨，照例有一两个「男孩子」作陪。贝尔塔年轻，爱玩，一星期苦干下来，到礼拜天便起不来——于是礼拜天的弥撒渐渐不去了——这又是一桩激起内罗内义愤的新罪状。

「你干吗不早早地去？」罗莎问，「弥撒从六点就开始。」

「天哪！」贝尔塔笑道，「我可五点起不来——到礼拜天我早就累得半死了。」

罗莎叹气；她搜肠刮肚找词，可英语里的词总不到位。贝尔塔却一口回绝说意大利语；她宣称自己都快忘光了。这叫她母亲大吃其亏，贝尔塔心里明镜似的；她爱母亲，可爱自己随心所欲，而且她发现用英语随心所欲容易得多。

「你可是修女们教出来的，还是圣母玛丽亚的孩子呢。」罗莎哀叹。

「那可怪不着我，又不是我的错。」贝尔塔没大没小地说。

内罗内决定对贝尔塔闭嘴；他只当她不存在，为此人人感激。她比杰佩嘴利，有过那么一两回，还占过外公的上风。他和罗莎、马里奥去望弥撒，拖上满肚子不情愿的杰佩。杰佩腻烦他的教会腻烦得要命，可地狱的信仰牢牢拴住了他。

===Original===

Mario said to his wife in their bedroom one night: “I am thinking about our children.”

Rosa sighed: “There is much need for thought, my Mario: they are very different from us.”

Mario scratched his head, then he looked very wise, and when he spoke he did so slowly. “I was born in Milan, my Rosa. As for you, it was lucky that you came too soon, and so you got born in Siena. The baby drinks in the air at its birth, and the air it drinks goes all over. It touches the heart, it touches the brain, I think it gets into the blood. English air may agree with English babies, but it has not agreed with ours; our babies were Italian, they needed the air and the sun of their patria. And so,” he concluded a little sadly, “no child should be born on strange soil. We think only of money and we sacrifice our children﻿—yet some of us still remain poor!”

“If we lose our children we are very poor indeed, even when we become rich,” said Rosa.

===Chinese===

一天夜里，马里奥在卧房里对妻子说：「我在想咱们的孩子。」

罗莎叹气：「是得好好想想，我的马里奥：他们跟咱们太不一样了。」

马里奥挠挠头，做出一副高深的样子，说起来慢条斯理。「我生在米兰，罗莎。你呢，幸亏你来得急，才落生在锡耶纳。婴儿一落地就喝进了那里的空气，喝进去的空气走遍全身。它碰到心，碰到脑子，我想它还渗进血里。英国的空气兴许配英国的婴儿，可配不上咱们的；咱们的孩子生下来是意大利人，他们需要祖国的空气和太阳。」他收尾时有点伤感，「所以呀，孩子不该生在异乡的土地上。我们光想着钱，把儿女牺牲了——可我们有些人到头来还是穷！」

「要是失掉了儿女，那才是真的穷，就算发了财也一样。」罗莎说。

### II / 二
===Original===



===Chinese===

二

#### I / 一
===Original===

If Teresa had changed but little in six years, this was not the case with Gian-Luca, for to him had come the fullness of manhood. The touching lankiness of adolescence had given place to a well-knit figure, he was thin in proportion to his height, but his shoulders were wide above his narrow flanks. His face was less gentle, and his eyes less mysterious; they no longer seemed to be searching the beyond. Their expression was keener and more concentrated, so that now, when they rested on a client or a waiter or a table or the most minute appointments of the table, they took in at a glance significant details from which their owner would draw his conclusions, conclusions that were usually right. Very observant and prompt was Gian-Luca, and those who worked under him found him a hard master, one to whom constant small misdemeanors meant more than occasional flagrant transgressions. Gian-Luca had been known to forgive a subordinate who had come to his work very drunk one morning, having merely warned him of what would happen were the offence repeated; but a youth who habitually forgot to examine the mustard-spoons for signs of verdigris, and who, moreover, was careless of his nails, had been promptly reported to Millo and dismissed on Gian-Luca’s representations. And so, although he controlled his hot temper, scarcely ever raising his voice these days; although he was admitted to be a fine waiter and one under whom a lad got a first-class training; although they admired his arresting appearance and the ability that had put him in charge of a room when not yet quite twenty-four; although they worked hard, because to work for Gian-Luca was to fall beneath the spell of his mighty will to work, not a waiter that he ruled had the least affection for him﻿—they found him a little inhuman.

“I care nothing for what they may think,” said Gian-Luca. “So long as they do their work well, I care nothing.”

===Chinese===

如果说特蕾莎这六年变化甚微，吉安-卢卡却不是这样——他已长成了十足的男子汉。少年时那惹人怜惜的瘦长，换成了匀称结实的身量；以他的个头而论他算瘦，但窄腰之上双肩宽阔。他的脸不再那么温顺，眼睛也不那么神秘；它们不再像在探问彼岸。眼神更锐利、更专注了，如今这双眼睛落在客人、侍者、餐桌或餐桌上最细小的陈设上时，一眼便收尽要紧的细节，主人据此推断，而推断通常不错。吉安-卢卡眼尖手快，在他手下做事的人都觉得他是个厉害的东家——在他那里，日日出现的小过失比偶尔一次的大过错更不可饶恕。曾有这样一个例子：一个下属某天早晨醉醺醺地上工，吉安-卢卡原谅了他，只警告他再犯会有什么下场；可一个老忘检查芥末勺上有无铜绿的青年，加上又不讲究指甲，吉安-卢卡一报告米洛，立刻就被辞退了。于是，虽然他管得住火爆脾气，如今几乎从不抬嗓门；虽然大家公认他是出色的侍者，年轻人跟他能学到一流的本事；虽然人人佩服他出众的仪表，佩服他不到二十四岁便执掌一间餐厅的能力；虽然人人都卖力干活——因为替吉安-卢卡做事，就会被他那股工作的强大意志罩住——可他手下的侍者没有一个对他有半点感情——他们觉得他有点不近人情。

「他们怎么想，我毫不在乎，」吉安-卢卡说，「只要活计干得漂亮，我什么都不在乎。」

===Original===

And yet he did care, for with one part of him he wished very much to stand well with his fellows. A childish longing to be loved and praised, to be popular with his subordinates even, to be thought a good comrade, a boon companion, would come over him strongly at times. He would think of his schooldays, when, try as he might, he had always been left just outside. In those days he had been a stranger to his mates, an alien among the English children; but here at the Doric he was not quite an alien, for all the waiters were Italians. And yet here also there stretched that little gulf, that sense of being just outside﻿—that queer, empty feeling of having no real country and hence no real ties with those who had.

He would lie in bed at nights thinking only of himself and of life in connection with himself; his babyhood, his boyhood, his painful adolescence﻿—and then he would remember the Padrona. Looking back on the Padrona after nearly seven years Gian-Luca would feel almost kind; and that, he remembered, was what Mario had hoped for. Mario had said that anger in the heart could make a beast of a man. Oh, well, it had made a beast of him, Gian-Luca, who had tried to revenge himself on the Padrona, who had tried to insult her by insulting his love, or so he had thought at the time. Now he knew that it had not been only the Padrona, but his love on which he had wished to be revenged﻿—that great, soft, foolish and selfless thing that had upset all his resolutions. For that was precisely what had happened; it had made him forget Gian-Luca. It had changed his motto﻿—“I have got Gemma.” it had made him write﻿—that was what it had done. And of course he had never had Gemma for a moment, never for a single moment. Poor Gemma! How he must have worried her with loving, just as he had worried old Teresa long ago. He must always have suffered from a kind of craze for giving﻿—and people had not wanted what he had to give.

“It is now my turn to receive,” he would think, smiling a little. “There must surely be someone who is ready to love me?”

For in spite of his success he would feel so very lonely, so very much in need of being loved; he did not want to love, he wanted to be loved.

===Chinese===

可他其实在乎，因为他身上有一部分非常想和同件们处得好。一种孩子气的渴望——渴望被人爱、被人夸，渴望连下属也喜欢自己，被人当作好伙计、好酒友——时时强烈地攫住他。他会想起上学的日子，那时无论怎么努力，他总是被撇在外圈。那时候他是同学们的陌生人，是英国孩子中间的外人；可在多立克（Doric），他不算外人，侍者全是意大利人。然而这里也横着那道小小的鸿沟，那种被撇在外圈的感觉——那种古怪而空落落的滋味：没有真正的祖国，因而与有祖国的人也没有真正的牵连。

夜里躺在床上，他只想自己，想与自己相连的人生；婴儿时，少年时，苦涩的青春期——然后他会想起老板娘（Padrona）。隔了将近七年回头看老板娘，吉安-卢卡心里几乎生出温情；他记得，这正是马里奥当年所指望的。马里奥说过，心里的怒火能把人变成畜生。唉，那怒火就把他吉安-卢卡变成了畜生——他曾想对老板娘报复，想用糟蹋自己的爱来羞辱她，或者说他当时是这么以为的。如今他明白了：他想报复的不仅是老板娘，还有他的爱——那件巨大的、温柔的、愚蠢而不自私的东西，那件搅乱了他全部决心的事。事实恰恰如此：那件事让他忘了吉安-卢卡自己。它改了他的座右铭——「我有杰玛了」；它让他拿起了笔——它干的就是这个。当然，他从来不曾拥有过杰玛，一刻也不曾。可怜的杰玛！他那份爱一定烦透了地，就像很久以前他烦透了老特蕾莎。他想必一直害着一种拼命想给予的痴症——而人家并不想要他给的东西。

「如今该轮到我领受了，」他会这样想着，微微一笑，「总该有个人预备好了来爱我吧？」

因为尽管事事得意，他还是觉得那么孤独，那么需要被人爱；他不想去爱人，他想要被爱。

===Original===

“It is wiser, and it leaves a man more free for his business. When one loves one is all misery, all body and no brain﻿—one becomes a fool, one does and says nothing but foolish things,” he told himself, remembering those days with the Padrona. No, assuredly he did not want to love.

“I will go and buy a dog,” he suddenly decided. “A good dog will give me his affection.” But then he reflected that a good dog never spoke, that a good dog could not tell him of its love.

There were Mario and Rosa of whom he was quite fond, and who gave him much fondness in return. There was cross old Nerone, who perhaps gave more than fondness﻿—but then he was just cross old Nerone.
