「浪费了这么多！」她嚷，「你得让面粉多出数。」

「冇（cawn't）做不到！」他嘬着牙花子大声向她保证。

「必须做到，」特蕾莎冷冷地说，「你做不到，有的是做得到的人。」

有一天他回嘴：「那好啊，你找去。」说完讨了工钱，走了。

「蠢货！走了倒好，」特蕾莎皱眉，「甩掉这只邋遢猪我倒高兴。我得找个年轻点的人接这活。」可她找到的人没一个称心。

#### II / 二
===Original===

That Christmas the pains in Fabio’s back were rather more violent than usual, and in consequence he thought a great deal about God and much less about the Casa Boselli.

It was now his duty to help Maddalena to price their diminishing stock; his the task of watching a fluctuating market that bucked up and down like a turbulent bronco, thoroughly out of hand. But somehow those pains in his poor old back, together with his thoughts about God, made such trifles as customers, food and money, seem very far off and unimportant, so that sometimes now he would make mistakes, on the wrong side, too, for the Casa Boselli. Then it was that Teresa would undress him and rub him and swathe him in yards of red flannel; after which she would push him back again between the shafts, like an antiquated horse that only stands up because it is strapped to its toil. He would hobble about consulting his price list and dolefully shaking his head.

“Is it the bacon that has risen?” he would ask, “or is it the last of those imported hams?” And Teresa would have to desert her cash-desk and reprice the stock herself.

Maddalena did all in her power to help them, but she had her own trouble to bear, for Gian-Luca’s letters from France were very brief. He had not yet got his transfer, it seemed, and was working as a sergeant in charge of a mess somewhere far back at the Base. He said very little because of the censor, but his wife could read between the lines, and what she read there was a great discontent. Those were dreary little letters that Gian-Luca wrote home from the Officers’ Mess at the Base. And yet she was thankful, oh, deeply thankful to know that her man was safe; and now she redoubled her prayers to the Madonna, begging that the transfer might never be granted, begging that the creature she loved might come home unharmed when the war was ended. But sometimes while she prayed she would feel strangely guilty as though she were betraying Gian-Luca; as though she were plotting behind his back, as though she and the Madonna were plotting.

“It is only my fancy﻿—” she would try to tell herself. “Every poor woman, all over Europe, is praying for her husband’s safety.” Yet so anxious was she that she went to St. Peter’s to consult old Father Antonio.

===Chinese===

那年圣诞节，法比奥背上的疼痛比往常更凶，于是他想上帝想得多了，想博塞利商店想得少了。

如今给日渐见底存货标价的活计归他；盯那个市场的活计也归他——那市场像匹撒野的劣马，上蹿下跳，全无章法。可他可怜的老背上的疼，加上他想着上帝，把顾客、食物和钱这些琐事显得又远又不要紧了，于是他如今时不常出错，还是亏商店的错。每逢这种时候，特蕾莎就替他脱衣裳、给他揉、给他裹上几码红法兰绀；裹完再把他推回车轭里，像一匹老马，只因拴在苦役上才站得住。他一瘸一拐地踱着，对价目表，悲哀地摇头。

「是涨价的是咸肉？」他问，「还是最后那几只进口火腿？」于是特蕾莎只得撇下钱柜，自己去给存货重标价。

马达莱娜竭尽全力帮他们，可她也有自己的苦处：吉安-卢卡从法国来的信很短。调令似乎还没下来，他在后方某地的基地当军士、管一个食堂。因为检查制度他写得很少，可妻子能读出字缝里的东西——她读出的是大大的不满。那是些从基地军官食堂寄回家的、凄凄惨惨的短信。可她感恩，哦，深深地感恩她的男人平安；于是她加倍向圣母祈祷，求那道调令永远别批，求她爱的这个人战争结束时不损毫发地回家。可有时她一面祈祷，一面会奇异地觉得负疚，仿佛在背弃吉安-卢卡，仿佛在背后算计他，仿佛她和圣母串通一气。

「只是我的胡思乱想——」她试着宽慰自己，「全欧洲每一个可怜的女人，都在为丈夫的平安祈祷。」可她放心不下，还是去了圣彼得堂，请教老安东尼奥神父。

===Original===

Father Antonio smiled at her confession and proceeded to reassure her. “Your prayers will be answered as God thinks best. He knows that you pray for your husband’s conversion, and He knows that you must pray for his safe return, for is it not He who puts love in the heart, and who knows the way of that love? I would not worry about it, my daughter, I would just confide in the Madonna, and she, through God’s grace, will do what is best﻿—I think I would leave it all to her.”

After that Maddalena felt a little more happy, but she wished that Gian-Luca could get leave. His letters said nothing about coming home; perhaps he was afraid of losing his transfer﻿—perhaps he was staying out there by choice, hoping to get sent into the trenches if he stayed﻿—could he do that? She wondered.

Meanwhile Teresa now sat long into the night, trying to balance her accounts. She would have to go upstairs to rub Fabio’s lumbago, but when she had done this she would go down again to her office and get out her ledgers. Perhaps a letter would have come that evening﻿—a letter from the bank, requesting payment of the interest that was overdue; a letter that she dared not ignore for a moment, while not knowing how to answer. Alone in her office sat hard old Teresa facing defeat and disgrace; facing an incredibly empty world as she had done nearly thirty years ago. Then she had lost the fruit of her womb, Olga, the beloved; and now she might lose the fruit of her brain, the fruit of a lifetime of unceasing toil﻿—the beloved Casa Boselli.

“*Cento, duecento cinquanta﻿—*” she would mutter, staring down at the figures; and then she would get up to pace her small office, and then she would sit down again. She would think: “If only we could get our supplies, if only we could get our supplies!” There were moments now when she would have walked to Rome in order to fetch a tin of tomatoes. Then her thoughts would begin to spin round and round like the wheels in the macaroni factory; “My pasta﻿—my wonderful pasta﻿—” she would murmur, and her voice would be almost tremulous with sadness. “Who can I find who will make me my pasta, now that Francesco has gone?”

===Chinese===

安东尼奥神父听了她的告解，微微一笑，宽她的心。「你的祈祷会按上帝认为最好的方式应验。祂知道你求丈夫皈依，祂也知道你必须求他平安归来——把爱放进人心里的岂不正是祂，爱怎么走法岂不正是祂最清楚？这不必犯愁，我的女儿，只管把自己托给圣母，她会靠上帝的恩典安排最好的——我想，一切就交给她好了。」

此后马达莱娜心里松快了些，可她盼望吉安-卢卡能休假回来。他信里只字不提回家的事；也许他怕丢了他那道调令——也许他自己情愿留在那边，指望多待就能进战壕——他能那样做吗？她纳闷。

与此同时，特蕾莎如今深夜久坐，想平她的账。她得上楼去揉法比奥的腰痛，揉完再下地下室办公室，搬出账本。也许那天傍晚来了封信——银行来的，催讨过期的利息；一封她一刻也不敢怠慢、却又不知怎样回复的信。倔强的老特蕾莎独自坐在办公室里，面对失败与耻辱；面对一个空得难以置信的世界，如同将近三十年前那样。那时她失去了腹中的果实奥尔加，那心爱的；如今她可能失去脑中的果实、一生不辍劳作的果实——心爱的博塞利商店。

「一百、二百五十——」她盯着数字喃喃；然后起身，在小小的办公室里踱步，然后又坐下。她想：「要是能进到货就好了，要是能进到货就好了！」有些时候，为了一罐番茄她都情愿走到罗马去。然后她的思绪开始一圈圈旋转，像通心粉工厂里的轮子。「我的意面——我美妙的意面——」她喃喃，声音悲得几乎发颤。「弗朗切斯科走了，我能找谁给我做意面？」

#### III / 三
===Original===

After Christmas Fabio felt a little better, and so he thought less about God. Indeed, by the New Year he had almost recovered, and this was to the good, for the factory machines were crying out loudly for attention. Fabio, so his wife had discovered since the war, had a very cunning hand with machines; he could make fine adjustments with accuracy, oiling and coaxing the while.

“*Sii buono, sii bravo; suvvia!*” he would coax; and then, strange to say, the machines would run smoothly, as though they wished to pleased Fabio.

That January Fabio adjusted the machines, after which they gave much less trouble; but by March there was no one in the factory to run them, for the latest acquisition had thrown up his job, preferring to make munitions. Now indeed Teresa had her back against the wall, for she could not replace the man. There was plenty of good work going those days, and better wages than she paid to be earned. Then week by week the flour was growing worse, becoming more difficult to mix; she would gladly have taken on the factory herself but that she must needs be always in the cash desk; and as for Maddalena, she was wanted in the shop, besides which she had never learnt how to make pasta. Yes, but old Fabio had﻿—he might try to deny it, but Teresa knew that he lied. As a lad he had worked in a macaroni factory, he had told her so many times in the past, it was therefore quite useless to lie. And, moreover, it was being a deserter, a traitor to the Casa Boselli in its need.

“It is you who must make the pasta,” she told him, “it is you who must run the machines, it is you who must help me to save our business. *Ma che!* You are old, but you are still a man, and you know how the pasta should be made.”

“No, no,” he whimpered, “I do not know, Teresa. If I once knew, then I have forgotten﻿—and my arms have grown weak for the kneading of flour, and my back is weak too from those horrible pains. As God is my witness I cannot do it! I am old﻿—very old, Teresa.”

“Yes, and I too am old,” she answered harshly, “but I do not whine like a cur. I say: ‘No, I am not yet utterly defeated, and while I have breath I fight.’ ”

“But my back, my miserable back﻿—” he pleaded, peering at her face with dim eyes.

“A little work will limber your back; a little work hurts no man,” said Teresa.

And knowing that further resistance was useless, Fabio muttered “*Si, si.*”

===Chinese===

圣诞过后，法比奥觉得好些了，于是想上帝想得少了。到新年他几乎痊愈，这是好事，因为工厂的机器正嗷嗷待哺。特蕾莎自开战以来发现，法比奥对付机器有一双极巧的手；他能精细地调校，一面给机器上油，一面哄它们。

「sii buono, sii bravo; suvvia!（乖啊，好样的；来嘛！）」他哄着；说也奇怪，机器竟转得顺顺当当，仿佛存心讨法比奥欢心。

一月里法比奥调好了机器，此后机器省事多了；可到三月，厂里再没人开机了，因为最新招的那个辞了工，宁可去造军火。这回特蕾莎真被逼到墙根了，那人她补不上。那些日子好活计有的是，工钱比她给的高。而且面粉一周比一周糟，越来越难和；她倒情愿亲自进厂，可她离不开钱柜；至于马达莱娜，店里离不开她，何况她从没学过做意面。可是——老法比奥学过——他尽可以抵赖，可特蕾莎知道他撒谎。他小时候在通心粉工厂干过，从前他亲口对她说过不知多少遍，再撒谎全无用处。何况这也是当逃兵，是趁博塞利商店之危的背叛。

「必得由你来做饭面，」她告诉他，「必得由你来开机，必得由你来帮我救咱们的生意。ma che!（得了！）你是老了，可你到底是个男人，而且你知道意面该怎么做。」

「不，不，」他哀声说，「我不知道，特蕾莎。就算从前知道，也忘了——和面的胳膊早没力气了，腰也叫那些要命的疼弄坏了。上帝作证，我干不动了！我老了——老得很了，特蕾莎。」

「是啊，我也老了，」她声色俱厉地答，「可我不像条癞狗那样哼哼。我说：『不，我还没彻底败，只要还有一口气，我就斗。』」

「可我的腰，我这条倒霉的腰——」他央告，昏花的眼睛觑着她的脸。

「干点活正好活动你的腰；干点活伤不了人。」特蕾莎说。

法比奥知道再拗也无益，咕哝了一声「si, si」。

===Original===

So all through that war-racked, agonizing spring Fabio tried to make pasta; tried to lift and carry and knead and roll the strange-colored flour, till his aged arms were corded with hard, blue veins. It was pitiable how little he made, considering his long hours of toil﻿—not enough to throw to a coop of chickens, according to Teresa﻿—but Fabio worked on in an anguish of spirit; dumb, too, because when a man had grown old what was the use of complaining? The sweat would go pouring down both his cheeks and drip into the dough unperceived; and sometimes there would come a queer singing in his ears, and long, floating black things in front of his eyes, so that he must needs stop and take off his glasses, in order to brush away those black things. But the black things would remain, and now in addition some flour would have got into his eye; and when this happened he would go to Maddalena, who would make him wet his handkerchief with the tip of his tongue, after which she would wipe out the flour.

“*Poverino!*” she would think as he turned to leave her. “*Poverino!* He grows very feeble. It is cruel to make him do such heavy work﻿—but then Teresa is cruel.”

===Chinese===

于是整个那个被战争蹂躏、苦苦煎熬的春天，法比奥都在试着做意面；试着搬、扛、揉、压那些颜色古怪的面粉，直到苍老的胳膊鼓起条条青筋。以他劳作的长久而论，他做出来的东西少得可怜——照特蕾莎的说法，不够喂一笼鸡——可法比奥怀着满心的苦楚干下去；也不作声，人老了，抱怨还有什么用？汗水顺着两颊淌下来，滴进面团里也没察觉；有时耳朵里嗡嗡地响起奇异的鸣唱，眼前飘起长长的黑影，他只得停下来摘下眼镜，去挥那些黑影。可黑影挥不去，这会儿又进了面粉渣；每逢这时他就去找马达莱娜，马达莱娜让他用舌尖舔湿手帕，再替他把面粉擦出来。

「poverino!（可怜的人！）」他转身走开时她想，「poverino! 他实在支撑不住了。叫他干这么重的活，太狠心了——不过特蕾莎本来就狠心。」

===Original===

Yet in spite of herself she was forced to admire this woman of steel and iron, this gallant old pilot who clung to the wheel while the storm increased in fury; this woman who did not spare Fabio, it was true, but who did not spare herself either. When Maddalena wrote to Gian-Luca these days, she wrote with admiration of Teresa; but she did not tell him what she herself suspected, namely, that the Casa Boselli must go under in spite of those hands on the wheel. Not for all the world, much less for Teresa, would she have worried her man; nor would she have touched one penny of his savings for fifty Casa Bosellis. Now Maddalena also had her back to the wall, but she was fighting for Gian-Luca. He had little enough cause to be grateful, she felt, to this woman who would not love him. He had worked ungrudgingly all his young life, and what he had earned he should keep. Yes, he should keep it, though the Casa Boselli were split to bits on the rocks. For though neither Teresa nor Fabio mentioned money, Maddalena had eyes in her head, and what she was never told of she guessed﻿—and then there was Nerone, who began to talk freely about Teresa’s large debts. Teresa’s speculations were now an open secret, as most secrets were in Old Compton Street.

“*Dio!* That Fabio is a fool!” said Nerone. “Any man is who is ruled by a woman, but then Fabio was always a poor fool.”

===Chinese===

可她不由自主地不能不佩服这个钢筋铁骨的女人，这位风暴愈狂愈紧攥舵轮的英勇老舵手；这个女人，不饶法比奥是真的，可也不饶她自己。那些日子马达莱娜给吉安-卢卡写信，字里行间满是对特蕾莎的钦佩；但她不告诉他自己的疑心——那就是，任凭那双手攥着舵轮，博塞利商店怕是终究要沉。她绝不肯——无论如何不肯——为了特蕾莎，更不会为了五十个博塞利商店去烦她的男人，也不会动他一分积蓄。如今马达莱娜也背靠着墙了，可她是为吉安-卢卡而战。他觉得，这个不肯爱他的女人，实在没多少值得他领情的；他年轻的生命里一直在不辞辛苦地干，挣来的就该归他留着。是，就该归他留着，哪怕博塞利商店在礁石上撞得粉碎。因为虽然特蕾莎和法比奥都不提钱，马达莱娜头上长着眼睛，没人告诉她的她猜得到——何况还有内罗内，他开始大大咧咧地谈论特蕾莎的大笔欠债了。特蕾莎的投机如今成了公开的秘密，老康普顿街的秘密多半都这样。

「dio!（天哪！）法比奥是个傻瓜！」内罗内说，「受女人辖制的男人都是傻瓜，不过法比奥一向就是个可怜的傻瓜。」

#### IV / 四
===Original===

That summer came a series of rather bad air raids, and Fabio was openly afraid. He would sit in the office under the pavement, praying, with his fingers stuffed in his ears, or begging Teresa to take him to the Tube, where Rosa had gone with the twins. Teresa, however, despised such precautions.

“If we die, we die,” was her motto. “The Tube is for mothers with little children, not for old men like you and old women like me. No, I will not let you go to the Tube,” she would say. Then Fabio would begin to cry.

The raids added much to his misery, for now he could never go to sleep. “Do you think they will come tonight?” he would enquire, peering anxiously up at the sky.

Nor could he be sure that they would not come by day﻿—there had been a bad daylight raid﻿—and sometimes now, while he kneaded his dough, he would pause to listen, mistaking the whirr of the wheels for an aeroplane. He began to suffer from stupefying headaches and a full, tight feeling in his head; the dough he was kneading would go round and round, and with it the machines and the room. If he stood in a draught he would feel his lumbago, that terrifying pain across his back﻿—supposing a raid should come at that moment and catch him unable to move﻿—

But one day in October, the God of his lumbago drew nearer, becoming the God of his soul; and Fabio’s old knees gave under him, and his head fell forward and lay upon the table, and his cheek lay buried deep in the flour that his weak hands had failed to mix.

That was how they found him two hours later﻿—just a little, old bundle that had once been a man, with flour on its clothes, on its hands, on its face; flour, too, on its halo of white hair. All foolish weakness he had been, that Fabio, and very often afraid; afraid of Teresa, afraid of God, and latterly terribly afraid of the Germans. He had little enough to tell of himself, now that he must face St. Peter at the Gate﻿—but perhaps he said: “I tried to make pasta﻿—I did try very hard to make pasta﻿—”

===Chinese===

那年夏天来了一连串相当严重的空袭，法比奥公开害怕了。他坐在人行道下的办公室里祈祷，手指头堵住耳朵，或者求特蕾莎带他去地铁车站，罗莎带着双胞胎就躲在那儿。可特蕾莎睥睨这一套。

「要死就死，」是她的信条，「地铁是给带小孩的母亲的，不是给你这样的老头子、我这样的老太婆的。不，我不许你去地铁。」她说。于是法比奥就哭。

空袭大大添了他的苦，因为他再也睡不着了。「你看他们今晚会来吗？」他焦急地仰望天空，问。

白天也难保他们不来——出过一次厉害的白昼空袭——于是如今他有时揉着面会停下来听，把轮子的嗡嗡错当成飞机。他开始害起令人发木的头痛，脑袋发胀发紧；他揉的面团会打转，机器和屋子也跟着打转。一站到风口上他就觉出腰痛，那横贯后背的骇人的疼——万一空袭偏在这时候来，正赶上他动弹不得——

可十月里的一天，他腰痛的上帝走近了，成了他灵魂的上帝；法比奥苍老的膝盖一软，头朝前栽下去，伏在桌上，脸颊深深埋进那堆他无力的双手没能和成的面粉里。

两小时后人们找到他时，他就是那副样子——一小捆曾经是人的老东西，衣裳上、手上、脸上都是面粉，一圈白发上也是面粉。他这一生实在是个软弱的傻瓜，而且常常害怕；怕特蕾莎，怕上帝，后来又怕德国人怕得要命。如今必须到天国的门前面对圣彼得了，他替自己辩解的本钱少得可怜——不过也许他说了：「我试着做意面了——我真的非常用力地试着做意面了——」

#### V / 五
===Original===

Now that he was dead and gone, everyone knew how much they had liked poor Fabio. They missed the mild-eyed, deprecatory figure that had wandered about Old Compton Street for more years than they cared to remember. But Nerone knew how much he had loved Fabio, and that was a very different thing. Nerone mourned the friend of his youth, and with him the passing of his own generation.

“I suppose it will be my turn next,” said Nerone. “I am not so very much younger than he was; but God grant that I die in Italy﻿—when this war is ended Nerone goes home.”

“So you shall, papa,” comforted Rosa.

“*Ma sicuro!*” Mario said kindly.

After the funeral Nerone spoke little, but he went to the cupboard and found his dominoes. He turned them out on to the sitting-room table, where he dusted them one by one; from time to time he spat on his finger and rubbed some dirt off an ivory face, then, he laid them back gently, reverently even, as though they were poor little corpses. He made Rosa go out and buy him some striped ribbon﻿—the green, white and red that they were selling in the shops﻿—and with this he carefully tied up the box, then put it at the back of the cupboard. Thus, the dominoes had a small military funeral, being laid to rest in the colors of their country; and all this for the love and honor of Fabio, who had not had a military funeral.

===Chinese===

人一死，人人都知道自己原是多么喜欢可怜的法比奥。他们想念那个目光温顺、见人就道歉的身影，他在老康普顿街踱来踱去，踱了多得他们不愿去数的年头。可内罗内知道自己曾经多么爱法比奥——那是另一回事。内罗内哀悼他年轻时的朋友，也随他哀悼自己一代人的逝去。

「我想下一个就该轮到我了，」内罗内说，「我并不比他年轻多少；只求上帝让我死在意大利——等仗打完，内罗内就回老家。」

「一定的，爸爸。」罗莎安慰他。

「ma sicuro!（那是自然！）」马里奥和气地说。

葬礼之后内罗内话很少，可他去碗柜里找出了他的骨牌。他把骨牌倒在客厅桌上，一块一块地擦；不时往手指上啐口唾沫，擦去某张象牙面上的污迹，然后轻轻放回去，几乎是恭敬地放回去，仿佛它们是一具具小小的尸首。他叫罗莎出门给他买一段条纹缎带——铺子里正卖的那种绿白红三色的——仔细把骨牌盒捆好，放回碗柜深处。就这样，骨牌得了一场小小的军人葬礼，裹着祖国的颜色入土安息；这一切都是为了对法比奥的爱与敬意——法比奥自己，可没能有一场军人葬礼。

===Original===

Teresa was alone now at the Casa Boselli, alone, too, at night in her bed. No need to lie stiffly not twitching a muscle, for now there was no old husband to wake﻿—Fabio was sleeping very soundly. All night long she could think undisturbed. Oh, and Teresa had very many thoughts, some of them coming unbidden to her mind﻿—queer, faraway thoughts about sunshine and youth at a time of the gathering-in of the grapes. And the thoughts would paint pictures for old Teresa, and then she would begin to remember. Into these pictures that worried and perplexed her would come walking a quiet, unimportant little man; a man with the eyes of a patient dog whose importunate loving wearies the master, who, nevertheless, must keep it to guard him. Then, less dimly, would come the figure of that other﻿—so gallant, so merry, so passionately young, so anxious to drink youth down to the dregs﻿—ay, and to make her drink with him. And face to face they would stand, those two men, as perhaps they were standing now﻿—who could tell? For she was the debt that had lain between them, the debt that Fabio had paid for that other, who had been unwilling to pay.

How futile a thing was this so-called life, which always ended in death﻿—the death of Olga, the death of Fabio, the approaching death of the Casa Boselli. Struggle and sweat and sweat and struggle to make fine good pasta in the turmoil of war﻿—that was what Fabio had done, and had failed, for down he had dropped like a little old bundle, beside his huge mountain of flour﻿—Fabio the patient, the timid, the foolish﻿—Fabio, the father of Olga.

Thoughts, always thoughts, intolerable thoughts; but not pity, no, for pity was weakness﻿—weakness that might lead you to pray for the dead; you, who had long since done with prayers.

Teresa would sit up stiffly in the darkness, with her thin hands clenched on the bedspread. Her hard black eyes would be staring at nothing, now that she had them wide open. Then one night she must suddenly speak to the Madonna to whom she had not spoken for years.

“You think I am beaten!” she told her fiercely. “You are glad to think that Teresa is beaten, Teresa who will not serve you. But no, you are wrong, for Teresa is not beaten﻿—she will never be beaten while she lives! If she has to sell matches as a beggar in the streets she will not be beaten by you to her knees.”

===Chinese===

特蕾莎如今独自守着博塞利商店，夜里也独自睡她的床。再不必直挺挺地一动不动了，因为再没有老丈夫可惊醒——法比奥睡得很沉。整夜她可以不受打扰地想。哦，特蕾莎有许多许多念头，有些不请自来——在收葡萄的时节，关于阳光和青春的、古怪而遥远的念头。这些念头为老特蕾莎画画，然后她开始回忆。走进这些搅扰她、困惑她的画面里的，是一个安静而无关紧要的小个子男人；一双耐心的狗一般的眼睛，那种纠缠的爱让主人厌烦、却又不得不留它看家的狗。然后，不那么模糊地，走来另一个的身影——那样英姿，那样快活，那样火热地年轻，那样急着把青春一饮而尽——还拉着她一起饮。那两个男人面对面站着，或许此刻正面对面站着——谁知道呢？因为她就是横在他们之间的那笔债，法比奥替另一个还了的债，那另一个本不肯还。

这所谓的生涯是多么徒劳，它总以死收场——奥尔加的死，法比奥的死，博塞利商店追近的死。挣扎、流汗、流汗、挣扎，在战乱里做上好的意面——法比奥干的就是这个，而且败了，因为他像一小捆老东西似的倒下去，倒在他巨大的面粉山旁——法比奥，耐心的、胆小的、愚蠢的法比奥——奥尔加的父亲。

念头，永远是念头，难堪的念头；可不是怜悯，不，怜悯是软弱——那种引你去为死者祈祷的软弱；你，早就和祈祷断绝了的人。

特蕾莎会在黑暗里僵硬地坐起来，枯瘦的手攥紧床罩。她那双坚硬的黑眼睛睁得大大的，瞪着虚空。后来有一夜，她忽然必得对圣母说话了——多少年没说过话的圣母。

「你以为我败了！」她狠狠地对她说，「你以为特蕾莎败了就高兴，特蕾莎这个不肯伺候你的人。可是不，你错了，特蕾莎没有败——她活着就永远不会败！哪怕落到沿街乞讨卖火柴，她也绝不会向你下跪。」

===Original===

And then she listened as though for an answer, an answer that did not come. For not in poor, faltering human speech could the Mother of God reply to Teresa.

“Ah!” said Teresa. “You answer me nothing, you wish me to think that you are angry. The foolishness of it! You are a thing of plaster that my hands destroyed easily many years ago. Less than a minute it took to destroy you﻿—of course you can answer me nothing!”

===Chinese===

然后她侧耳听，仿佛等一个回答，回答没有来。上帝的母亲无法用可怜的、期期艾艾的人间言语回答特蕾莎。

「啊！」特蕾莎说，「你一言不答，你要我想你在生气。多蠢！你不过是一尊石膏像，多年前我双手一挥就轻易毁掉了。毁了你还用不上一分钟——你当然答不上我的话！」

### VIII / 八
===Original===



===Chinese===

八

#### I / 一
===Original===

In the days that followed after Fabio’s death, the members of the clan showed their metal; and their metal, at bottom, was pure gold it seemed, for one and all they rallied to Teresa, rallied to the Casa Boselli. Nerone sent Rosa to help in the shop, preparing his own midday meal. Every morning at nine she arrived with the twins, who were shut up to play in Olga’s old bedroom. The afternoon frequently brought kind Mario who was anxious to do odd jobs; he might be seen dusting, or polishing brass, or even cleaning a window. Rocca discovered a clubfooted boy who happened to be an Italian, and Teresa engaged him to sweep and run errands﻿—only he could not run. Rocca would sometimes send round a small present, the tongue of an ox, the feet of a pig, or even a couple of kidneys; and these, considering the shortage of meat, were tokens of very real friendship. The Signora Rocca put on her old clothes and over them a large apron, then she went to Teresa and said abruptly:

“I am here to wash down and tidy those shelves. ‘Gold begets gold, but dust begets dust,’ as my mother would say when I was a child.”

And Teresa replied with another true proverb: “ ‘To find a staunch friend you must dig deep in trouble.’ ” Then she added: “I thank you, signora.”

Sometimes Nerone would come in the evenings to drink a glass of Amarena, and he and Maddalena would talk to Teresa while she sat silently knitting. Maddalena would tell her the little she knew of Gian-Luca’s life out in France, hoping that now Teresa might soften, might even express some interest.

“So he is still working in the mess, the Gian-Luca﻿—”

===Chinese===

法比奥死后的那些日子，族人各自显出了成色；他们的成色，骨子里看，竟是足金——因为他们一个不落地集结到特蕾莎身边，集结到博塞利商店身边。内罗内派罗莎去店里帮忙，自己备中午饭。每天早晨九点，她带着双胞胎到，双胞胎被关在奥尔加从前的卧室里玩。下午常常带来和气的马里奥，他热心找零活干：时而见他掸灰，时而擦铜器，甚至擦窗户。罗卡发现了一个歪脚的男孩，碰巧是意大利人，特蕾莎雇他扫地跑腿——只是他跑不了。罗卡有时差人送来小礼：一条牛舌，几只猪脚，甚至一对腰子；在肉荒的日子里，这些是极真挚的友谊凭证。罗卡太太换上旧衣裳，外面罩一条大围裙，走到特蕾莎面前，直截了当地说：

「我来把这些架子洗刷收拾。『金生金，灰生灰』——我小时候我妈就这么说。」

特蕾莎回了另一句真谚语：「『要寻患难之交，须向患难深处掘。』」然后补一句：「谢谢你，太太。」

有时内罗内傍晚过来喝杯阿玛雷娜酒，他和马达莱娜陪特蕾莎说话，特蕾莎坐着默默织毛线。马达莱娜把知道的那点吉安-卢卡在法国的情形讲给她听，盼着如今特蕾莎能软下来，哪怕表示一点兴趣。

「这么说他还管着食堂，那个吉安-卢卡——」

===Original===

Teresa would say. “Oh well, and why not? All our lives we have studied the art of feeding; no doubt he is better suited for that job than for firing off guns at the Germans.” And then she would appear to forget Gian-Luca, frowning a little at her knitting, murmuring softly over her stitches: “Knit one, purl two, knit one.” Even Aunt Ottavia, who was not of the clan, felt called upon to assist; she it was who could teach Teresa new patterns, she it was who discovered a fine book on knitting, which she promptly bought for Teresa. Berta turned up, a little through kindness, and a great deal through wishing to talk about Albert. Albert had received the Military Cross, and just after that﻿—which was more to the point﻿—a “blighty,” which was likely to keep him in London for many a day to come. Berta would soon have to take the twins home in order to pacify Albert.

There was someone, however, as high above them all as the Lord is above little fleas, and this person was Millo, who sat in his office and considered the Casa Boselli. Millo was omnipotent and so he knew all things, at least he knew all things connected with food, and one fine day he put on his hat and went forth to call on Teresa.

He said: “I would like to have a few words, suppose we go into the parlor.” And after a minute when they were alone: “Suppose you show me your ledgers.”

Teresa stood tall and defiant before him, but Millo divined the anguish in her heart. He smiled very kindly at the gallant old woman﻿—she was well worth saving because of her business, but above all because of her courage.

She said: “My ledgers are private, signore; I do not show my accounts.”

“And yet you will show them to me,” he told her, “for the Casa Boselli is about to go bankrupt﻿—and that would be a great pity, signora; we need the Casa Boselli.”

“I will save it yet,” Teresa answered without a tremor in her voice. “You may have heard rumors, and perhaps they are true, but the Casa Boselli will be saved.”

“*Sicuro!*” said Millo; “I have come here to save it. Now suppose you bring me those ledgers.”

For one awful moment she stared at him speechless, while the great tears welled in her eyes. For one awful moment she thought that she must weep, standing there before Millo. But the moment passed, and once more Teresa could look him squarely in the face.

===Chinese===

特蕾莎会说，「哦，那又怎样？不行吗？我们一辈子研究的就是伺候人吃饭这门手艺；比起朝德国人开炮，无疑他干那个更合适。」然后她似乎就把吉安-卢卡忘了，对着毛线活微微皱眉，嘴里轻轻念着针法：「平针一，反针二，平针一。」连不算族中人的奥塔维娅姨妈也觉得义不容辞；是她能教特蕾莎新花样，是她寻到一本好编织书，立刻买给特蕾莎。贝尔塔也露面了，一半出于好心，一大半为了讲阿尔伯特。阿尔伯特得了军功十字勋章，紧接着——这才是要紧的——挨了一处「回国伤」，八成要让他留在伦敦好些日子。贝尔塔很快就得把双胞胎领回家去安抚阿尔伯特了。

然而有一位，高高在上，如同主之于跳圣之高，这人就是米洛，他坐在办公室里盘算着博塞利商店。米洛全能，所以无所不知——至少凡是同食物有关的事他无所不知。一个晴好的日子，他戴上帽子，出来拜访特蕾莎。

他说：「我想同你说几句话，我们到客厅去吧。」过了一分钟，单独相对时：「把你的账本拿给我看看。」

特蕾莎高高地、倔强地立在他面前，可米洛看穿了她心里的苦楚。他和气地朝这位倔强的老太太微笑——她值得救，因为她的生意，更因为她的胆气。

她说：「我的账本是隐私，先生；我的账不给人看。」

「可你会给我看的，」他告诉她，「因为博塞利商店就要破产了——那太可惜了，太太；我们少不了博塞利商店。」

「我还要救它，」特蕾莎答，声音纹丝不颤，「你或许听见了些风声，兴许是真的，可博塞利商店会得救。」

「sicuro!（那是当然！）」米洛说，「我就是来救它的。现在把那些账本拿来吧。」

有那么难堪的一瞬，她瞪着他，说不出话，大颗的泪涌上眼眶。有那么一瞬，她想自己必得在米洛面前哭出来了。可那一瞬过去了，特蕾莎又能直直地看进他的脸。

#### II / 二
===Original===

“Why should you trouble about me, signore﻿—about the Casa Boselli?”

“That,” he said gently, “is quite beside the point﻿—I am rich enough, say, to pay for my fancies, and I have a fancy to offer you a partner﻿—one Francesco Millo, signora.”

Then Teresa turned without another word and brought him those miserable ledgers; brought him the accounts that she could not balance, and the copies of the deeds that she had signed for the bank, pledging her two long leases. He spread them out on the parlor table and made her sit down at his side; and presently he and Teresa were scheming, putting their business heads together, forgetful of all save the Casa Boselli and of how it might best be served. When at last he got up to go he was courteous, but a number of papers were reposing in his pocket, papers that Teresa had wished to keep secret for the prestige of the Casa Boselli.

“Our solicitors shall meet not later than next week, and meanwhile I must send you some help,” he said gravely; then he added: “I thank you for accepting this partner,” and stooping he kissed her hand.

Alone, Teresa sat very still, she was staring down at the table. Millo had saved the Casa Boselli, its debts would be paid, its credit restored, its future secured and cared for. Never again need she lie awake at night tormented by fears for her factory, never again need she worry about money, pacing up and down in her office. True, but never again would the Casa Boselli be all hers, all her own; never again would she unsheath her sword to do battle in its dear defense. She saw herself surrendering that sword. All battered and bent it was﻿—for love of the Casa Boselli she surrendered; so now in the moment of great salvation, she laid down her head and wept bitterly. She who had faced life with hard, dry eyes, she who had shed no tears over Fabio, now wept for love of the Casa Boselli as she had not wept since the death of Olga, thirty long years ago.

The great news spread quickly. Such wonderful news! Everyone congratulated Teresa.

“If only Fabio could know!” sighed Nerone. “He would surely have been so proud.”

===Chinese===

「你何必为我操心，先生——为博塞利商店？」

「这个嘛，」他温柔地说，「全然题外话——我的钱够多，可以说，付得起我的兴致；我正起意给你引一位合伙人——一位弗朗切斯科·米洛，太太。」

于是特蕾莎一言不发地转身，把那些寒磣的账本取来了；把他平不了的账、把她签给银行的两份长租抵押契据的抄件，都取来了。他把它们摊在客厅桌上，让她坐在自己身旁；不多时，他和特蕾莎已在一起盘算，两颗做生意的脑袋凑在一处，忘掉了一切，只记得博塞利商店和怎样对它最有利。末了他起身告辞，彬彬有礼，可口袋里装走了一批文件——特蕾莎本想为博塞利商店的体面而保密的文件。

「我们的律师最迟下星期碰头，这期间我给你派几个帮手来。」他郑重地说；随后补一句：「多谢你肯收下这位合伙人。」说着俯身吻了她的手。

独自一人，特蕾莎久久呆坐，低头瞪着桌子。米洛救下了博塞利商店，债会还清，信誉会恢复，前途有了保障和照应。夜里再不必为工厂提心吊胆地睡不着，再不必在办公室里踱来踱去为钱发愁。是的，可博塞利商店再也不会全属于她、全归她 own 了；再也不会拔剑出鞘，为亲爱的它一战的。她看见自己交出了那柄剑。剑已经磕碰弯曲——为了爱博塞利商店，她交出了它；于是在这场大拯救的时刻，她伏下头，恸哭起来。这个以干硬的眼睛面对人生的她，这个法比奥死时没掉一滴泪的她，如今为爱博塞利商店而哭，哭得像三十年前奥尔加死时以来从未哭过的那样。

天大的喜讯传得飞快。这样的好消息！人人都向特蕾莎道贺。

「法比奥要是知道就好了！」内罗内叹道，「他该多骄傲啊。」

===Original===

An engineer arrived from the Doric, to grease and lay up the factory machines, for Millo advised no more making of pasta until the war came to an end. He had managed to find three Italian women, whom he sent with orders to help in the shop; and many other things he did for Teresa, setting the Casa Boselli in order to withstand the siege of the war.

Maddalena stayed on at Teresa’s side, as head of the new assistants; but Rosa went home again to Nerone, and Mario could rest in the afternoons, for now there were others to polish the brass, to dust, and to clean the windows. Teresa purchased a new black dress; grimmer than ever she stood in her cash-desk﻿—a tall old woman with the face of an eagle, and hair that refused to turn grey.

Now that the storm had been safely weathered, Maddalena wrote of these things to Gian-Luca. She wrote guardedly, however, to spare his feelings; that was how she had written of Fabio’s death, careful to omit the details.

And Gian-Luca sighed as he read her long letters, because he had taken to sighing lately; and his sighs would come from a weariness of spirit, from a sense of futility born of his boredom; for a transfer had not yet been granted Gian-Luca who was such an efficient mess-sergeant.

===Chinese===

多立克派来一名机修工，给工厂的机器上油封存，因为米洛建议，仗打完之前别再做意面了。他还没法找来三个意大利妇女，派到店里帮忙；此外他为特蕾莎做了许多事，把博塞利商店整顿得能撑住战争的围困。

马达莱娜留在特蕾莎身边，做新帮手的头儿；罗莎回内罗内家去了，马里奥下午也能歇歇了，因为如今擦铜器、掸灰、擦窗都有别人了。特蕾莎购置了一件新黑裙；她比以往任何时候都冷峻地站在钱柜后面——一个鹰脸的高个子老妇人，头发硬是不肯白。

风暴安然渡过，马达莱娜把这些写信告诉吉安-卢卡。不过她写得很有分寸，怕刺痛他；她当初写法比奥的死讯也是这样，小心地略去细节。

吉安-卢卡读她的长信时叹气，因为近来他染上了叹气的毛病；他的叹气出自一种心灵的倦怠，出自无聊生出的徒劳感；因为调令至今没有批给这么能干的食堂军士吉安-卢卡。

#### III / 三
===Original===

Death, that was striking at heart after heart, now struck at the hearts of Mario and Rosa; for that December Geppe was killed in the twelfth great battle of the Isonzo. Geppe had gone through those weeks of hell without so much as a scratch, and then, *zipp!* a large splinter of shrapnel in the stomach, and presently no more Geppe!

Mario was unexpectedly quiet, saying little, not weeping at all. He just gathered Rosa into his arms: “*Povera, povera donna*,” he murmured, rhythmically patting her shoulder.

Rosa’s tears came gushing up from her heart in a great, irresistible flood; she was all dissolving with love and pity, weeping less for the death of her only son than for what she knew must have been his fear in those last few agonizing hours.

Nerone sat silently staring at his birds and rubbing his wooden leg. When at last he did speak his voice was solemn. “A hero has died,” said Nerone slowly; “my daughter Rosa is the mother of a hero. Let it be always remembered among us how nobly our Geppe died.”

There had been nothing noble in poor Geppe’s death, unless death itself be noble﻿—he had died as hundreds of others had done, because there was no escape. Scourged and tormented by vile engines of war, by terrible, nerve-breaking, soul-sickening noise, by the smell and the sight and the slime of blood﻿—crowding, thrusting, yelling, retreating, in a welter of maimed, half-demented men﻿—that was how Geppe had died. But now he must needs be a hero to Nerone, and Rosa must mourn him as a hero, and Mario must speak of “My brave son Geppe,” as though Geppe had been willing and glad to die in that awful retreat of Caporetto.

===Chinese===

死神挨着人心一个一个地敲，如今敲到了马里奥和罗莎的心上：十二月，杰佩在伊松佐河第十二次大会战中阵亡了。那几个星期的地狱杰佩毫发无损地捱过来了，可是，唰！一大块弹片进了肚子，转眼间就没有杰佩了！

马里奥出奇地安静，话很少，完全不哭。他只把罗莎揽进怀里：「可怜的，可怜的女人（povera donna），」他喃喃着，有节奏地拍她的肩。

罗莎的泪从心里涌上来，如不可抗拒的洪流；她整个人被爱与怜融化了，与其说哭独子的死，不如说哭她明知他在最后那痛苦时辰里必有的恐惧。

内罗内默默坐着，瞪着他的鸟，搓他的木腿。他终于开口时，嗓音是庄严的。「一位英雄死了，」内罗内缓缓地说，「我的女儿罗莎是一位英雄的母亲。让我们永远记住，我们的杰佩死得多么高贵。」

可怜的杰佩的死毫无高贵可言，除非死本身就高贵——他像成百上千的人一样死了，因为无处可逃。被战争的可憎机器鞭挞折磨，被骇人的、磨碎神经、败坏灵魂的噪音，被血的气味、血的景象和血的泥泞——挤着、推着、喊叫着、溃退着，在残肢断臂、半疯半狂的人堆里——杰佩就是这样死的。可如今在内罗内眼里他必得是英雄，罗莎必得把他当英雄哀悼，马里奥必得开口「我勇敢的儿子杰佩」，仿佛杰佩是心甘情愿、欢天喜地死在卡波雷托那场大溃退里的。

===Original===

Nerone went out with his snapshot the next day, and two weeks later there arrived an enlargement﻿—an enormous affair in a wide gilt frame﻿—showing Geppe’s weak face beneath a steel helmet, and a little blurred at that. Nerone hung the picture in the shop, above a low shelf just opposite the door, where all who came in must see it; he draped an Italian flag round the frame, a silk one, befitting a hero. Mario wrote out a little inscription which he stuck at the bottom of the picture: “Of your charity pray for the soul of Giuseppe Varese, who died that Italy might live.” Then he made the sign of the Cross on himself and muttered: “God grant that he rest in peace.” After which he hurried away to the Capo, for the living would be wanting their dinners. But Rosa came later and cleared from that shelf all the tins of tobacco and cigarettes, and in their place she set flowers before Geppe, so that now he looked more like a warrior-saint than the poor, frightened soldier he had been﻿—Geppe, who had always liked cigarettes infinitely better than flowers!

Whenever a customer glanced at the picture, Nerone would tell him about it. “*Ecco!* My grandson, Geppe!” he would say; “as gallant a lad as ever went to war. *Ma che!* The boy was a veritable tiger, but what will you, he was young, and the young are too reckless﻿—” And then he would spin out those long romances that his mind had woven around Geppe.

Rosa would hear him and shake her head sadly, thinking that her father had aged a great deal, thinking that the customer looked a little bored, a little anxious for his change. But not for the world would she have told old Nerone that he grew too voluble these days, that his yarns about Geppe were quite without foundation.

“After all, I believe he was fond of the boy,” she would think, and the thought would bring comfort with it; for nothing is so sweet a consolation in grief as the affection of others for our dead.

===Chinese===

第二天内罗内带着他那张小照片出门，两星期后寄来一张放大的——宽大的金框，好大一幅——钢盔底下杰佩那张无神的脸，还照得有点虚。内罗内把像挂在铺子里，正对门的一排矮架上方，进门的人谁都得看见；他又给框子缠上一面意大利国旗，绸子的，配英雄正合适。马里奥写了一小段题词，贴在相片底下：「请以您的慈悲，为朱塞佩·瓦雷塞（Giuseppe Varese）的灵魂祈祷，他为意大利之生而死。」然后他给自己画了个十字，咕哝：「求上帝赐他安息。」说完赶忙去卡波了，活人还等着吃晚饭呢。可罗莎后来又来，把那一架的烟草和香烟全撤了，原处给杰佩摆上鲜花——这下他更像个武圣，而不像他生前那个吓坏了的可怜士兵——杰佩，那个一辈子爱香烟远胜过爱花的杰佩！

每当有客人朝相片看一眼，内罗内便要讲起它。「ecco!（瞧！）我外孙，杰佩！」他说，「天底下头一等勇敢的战士。ma che!（可不是！）这孩子是只真老虎，可有什么法子，他还年轻，年轻人就是太不管死活——」然后他便铺陈起那些他在脑子里为杰佩编织的漫长传奇。

罗莎听着，悲哀地摇头，心想父亲老得厉害，心想那客人有点听腻了，有点惦记他的找头。可她无论如何不肯告诉老内罗内，说他这些日子话太多，说他那些杰佩的故事全无根据。

「说到底，我想他是疼这孩子的，」她想，这念头带来安慰；因为悲伤之中，再没有比别人也爱着我们的死者更甜的慰藉了。

#### IV / 四
===Original===

Meanwhile there was no one who was quite without their troubles; the Padrone, for instance, had troubles and to spare, for not only must he cope with inadequate service, but he could not afford a band at the Capo, in addition to which there was no room for dancing. Yet never before in the history of England had there been such a craze for movement; such a gliding and hopping, such a swaying and clinging; such a stamping and clapping and grabbing of food. Two mouthfuls and then: “Come on, let’s have a turn!” After which more grabbing of food.

Millo, wise man, had foreseen what was coming, and now there was dancing every night at the Doric. The supper-tables had been carefully arranged to leave a large space in the restaurant.

The New Year brought with it a couple of air raids, for six hours on end the bombs banged about London. “Play louder,” Millo murmured to his band; and pale but determined the band played its loudest: “Honey bee, give us a kiss!”

Sometimes Millo would linger to watch his dancers, and the smile on his lips would grow rather grim, then after a little he would shrug his broad shoulders. In Berlin they were cursing, in Paris they were praying, and in London they were dancing﻿—oh, well, such was war﻿ ﻿…

But Death was in nowise daunted, it seemed, for the spring came in on a great tide of blood. Yet this was not nearly enough for the glutton, who must needs go seeking out peaceable people who neither danced nor made war. Humble civilians just lay down and died from this cause or that, or from no cause at all except that they felt very weary. It was strange how many such people were dying; strange too, that as often as not they were those who had no one to lose in battle, and among them was talkative, small Aunt Ottavia, who caught cold in May and died early in June, leaving all that she possessed to Maddalena.

Who could have thought that the small Aunt Ottavia would have had so much money to leave? Four thousand pounds, securely invested in first-class American railroads. She had flatly refused to sell her good shares to the English Government or to anyone else; she had lent them to the Government for the time being only, receiving a tip in the process. But in spite of all her astuteness she had died, and her shares had remained behind her, to say nothing of the house in Coldbath Square, its contents, and one or two boarders.

===Chinese===

这段日子谁也没少有心事；比如老板，他的心事就多得冒尖：不但要对付不济的人手，卡波还请不起乐队了，再说也没地方跳舞。可英国有史以来还从没这么疯迷过运动：这样的滑步和蹦跳，这样的摇曳和偎贴；这样跺脚、击掌、抓东西吃。两口下肚，便喊：「来，转一圈去！」转完再抓东西吃。

米洛是个明白人，早料到了这一着，如今多立克夜夜有舞。晚饭桌精心排布，给餐厅腾出一大块空场。

新年带来了两场空袭，炸弹围着伦敦响了六个钟头。「奏响点，」米洛对乐队低语；乐队脸色发白而意志坚决，奏到最响：「honey bee, give us a kiss!（蜜峰儿，亲我一个！）」

米洛有时驻足看他的舞客，唇边的笑渐渐带上几分冷峻，过了一会儿便耸耸宽阔的肩膀。柏林在咒骂，巴黎在祈祷，伦敦在跳舞——唉，战争就是这样……

可死神似乎毫无惧色，春天踏着血的大潮来了。而这远填不饱那饕餮，它还要寻那些既不跳舞也不打仗的太平人。卑微的平民躺倒便死，死于这样或那样的缘故，或者干脆没有缘故，只因为觉得太乏了。奇怪的是这样的人死了那么多；更奇怪的是，他们多半是家里没有人在战场上送命的人。其中有那个多嘴的小个子奥塔维娅姨妈，她五月里着了凉，六月初便死了，把她的一切都留给了马达莱娜。

谁能想到小个子奥塔维娅姨妈竟留下这么多钱？四千镑，稳稳投在一流的美国铁路上。她断然拒绝把好股票卖给英国政府或任何别人；只肯暂时借给政府，还顺势捞了点小费。可任凭她再精明，她死了，股票留在了身后，更不必说科德巴斯广场的房子、屋里的家什，和一两个房客。

===Original===

Maddalena felt suddenly rather helpless, missing the tactless Aunt Ottavia. She longed intensely for her husband’s return, almost frightened by so much money. Gian-Luca wrote advising that the house should be sold, as he did not wish to live in Coldbath Square, and fortunately a purchaser turned up, who bought the house, boarders and all. Maddalena added the proceeds of the sale to Gian-Luca’s deposit at the bank; and now all her letters to him were full of new plans regarding the future.

“We can sell out the stocks,” she wrote happily, “and then you can buy your restaurant; only think of all that this means to us, *caro*! I am grateful to Aunt Ottavia.”

His answers were kind but curiously vague; it was almost as though he had scarcely grasped the extent of his own good fortune. And why did he never come home on leave? Surely he might have got home once on leave? But his answers were vague upon this point also.

He wrote: “I have not yet obtained my transfer.” As thought that could explain his not getting leave to a woman who loved as she did.

Maddalena was praying more earnestly than ever that the transfer might not be granted. How could she well do otherwise, when Fabio was dead and Geppe was dead, and now the poor Aunt Ottavia? The thought of so much death had struck terror to her heart, terror because of one life.

“Give me his life, dear Mother of God!” she entreated; and surely her prayers were being answered, for was he not working in comparative safety at the Officers’ Mess at the Base?

===Chinese===

马达莱娜忽然觉得没了依靠，想念起那个不知分寸的奥塔维娅姨妈来。她热切盼望丈夫回来，这么多钱几乎让她害怕。吉安-卢卡来信建议卖掉房子，他不愿住在科德巴斯广场；恰好来了个买主，连房带房客一并买下了。马达莱娜把卖房所得加进吉安-卢卡的银行存款；此后她写给他的信里，满是对将来的新打算。

「咱们可以把股票卖掉，」她欢欢喜喜地写，「然后你就能盘下你自己的餐馆；想想这对咱们意味着什么，亲爱的（caro）！我感激奥塔维娅姨妈。」

他的回信和气，却出奇地含糊；简直像他几乎没弄明白自己交了多大的好运。可他为什么不回来休假呢？他总该回来看过一次吧？可他在这点上回答得也含糊。

他写：「我的调令还没批下来。」仿佛这句话能对一个爱他如生命的女人解释他不休假似的。

马达莱娜比以往更恳切地祈祷调令别批。她还能怎样呢，法比奥死了，杰佩死了，如今又添了可怜的奥塔维娅姨妈？这么多的死，把恐惧打进她心里——为一条性命的恐惧。

「把他的命留给我，亲爱的圣母！」她恳求；她的祈祷想必蒙了应允，因为他不是正安然无恙地在基地的军官食堂当差吗？

#### V / 五
===Original===

Yes, and that was where the Armistice found him, at the Officers’ Mess at the Base. All of a sudden the guns stopped firing; a stillness so intense that men clasped their heads, bewildered, came down on the blood-drenched pastures of France. Gian-Luca stared round him at the Officers’ Mess, and knew that the war was over.

He thought: “It is gallant, this record of mine, it is something that a man can be proud of! All my battles have been fought with the peasants over chickens. A fine victory the capture of a couple of fowls, a splendid victory! And what a defeat to return to the Mess empty-handed!”

From the Doric to the Officers’ Mess at the Base, there had only been a difference in degree. Food, always food, the thing they had lived by, Fabio and Teresa, Rocca and Millo, and he himself who had planned to grow rich via the media of people’s stomachs﻿—oh, well, it had followed him out to the war, certain, no doubt of his allegiance: and now that the war was ended, more food﻿—for Gian-Luca must prepare a very fine dinner in honor of victory and peace.

===Chinese===

是的，停战就是在这里找到他的——基地的军官食堂。枪炮声戛然而止；一片深不可测的寂静，落到法国血浸的牧场上，惊愕的男人们抱住自己的头。吉安-卢卡在军官食堂里环顾四周，知道战争结束了。

他想：「这份履历多英勇啊，多能让一个男人自豪啊！我的全部战役，都是同乡下人围绕鸡打的。缴获两只母鸡，好一场大捷；空手回食堂，好一场惨败！」

从多立克到基地的军官食堂，只有程度之差。食物，永远是食物——法比奥和特蕾莎、罗卡和米洛、还有他自己赖以为生的东西，他本打算借众人的胃发射——唉，它一路跟着他跟到了战场上，可见忠贞不疑；如今战争结束了，还是食物——因为吉安-卢卡要为胜利与和平备一桌极好的晚餐。

#### VI / 六
===Original===

Three months later his train steamed into Victoria, and there on the platform stood Maddalena, scanning the passing windows. The train stopped and she had him safe in her arms, all the bigness and the manhood of him. Her cheek was pressed tightly against his shoulder; and because she could feel the roughness of his coat, and the strong, even beat of his living heart, her gratitude leapt to her lips in words.

“Blessed be God!” said Maddalena.

He kissed her, then pushed her gently from him, as though in some way he rebuked her; but her joy was too great to feel his rebuke.

“*Amore, amore*, you are safe﻿—” she whispered.

“Yes, I am quite safe,” he answered.

That evening she urged him to talk about himself, to tell her about his life out in France: “Your letters told nothing at all, *caro mio*, why did you tell me so little?”

“Because there was so little to tell﻿—” said Gian-Luca; “they never gave me my transfer.”

“Oh, I am glad, I am glad!” she murmured. “Day and night I have prayed to the Virgin.”

But at that he smiled, and seeing the smile she must get up and kiss his mouth.

Then he took her in his arms, for was she not a woman made more lovely through her love of him? And he kissed her again and again on the lips, until she must needs believe that he loved her﻿—that at last he had come to love her.

“*Amore mio﻿—amore*,” she whispered, filled with the joy of his nearness.

And he answered: “*Amore mio, amore*,” while he strove to find peace and contentment of spirit, even as she had found it.

All night they lay in each other’s arms, and her cup of happiness was full, for she thought that all night he came nearer and nearer﻿—yet all night he was slipping farther away from the arms that she fancied held him.

The next day he was gentle but strangely silent, and she studied his face more closely. Then it was that she knew that her husband had aged.

“Can it be that he has fretted himself, my Gian-Luca, because they would not transfer him?” she mused. “But why should he fret?﻿—he was doing his duty, he was doing all that they asked.”

But now she discovered yet another thing about him; he preferred not to talk of the war.

He said: “It is over, let us try to forget it; the English did not wish me to fight﻿—*va bene*, and now I must think of Millo and the Doric, I must go and see Millo tomorrow.”

===Chinese===

三个月后，他乘的火车驶进维多利亚站，月台上站着马达莱娜，在扫视一扇扇掠过的车窗。车一停，她便把他整个儿搂进怀里——连同他的高大和男子气。她的脸颊紧紧贴着他的肩；因为她摸得到他大衣的粗糙，摸得到他活着的心的沉稳搏动，感激便涌到唇边。

「感谢上帝！」马达莱娜说。

他吻她，然后轻轻把她推开一点，仿佛在某种意义上责备她；可她的欢喜太大，觉不出他的责备。

「爱人（amore），爱人，你平安——」她悄声说。

「是，我平安无事。」他答。

那天晚上她催他讲讲自己，讲讲他在法国的生活：「你的信里什么都没讲，我亲爱的，为什么讲得那么少？」

「因为没什么可讲的——」吉安-卢卡说，「他们始终没批我的调令。」

「哦，我真高兴，真高兴！」她喃喃，「我日日夜夜向圣母祈祷。」

可听了这话他微微一笑，见了这一笑，她忍不住起身吻他的唇。

于是他把她拥进怀里——她难道不是因为爱他而变得更美的女人吗？他一遍又一遍吻她的唇，直到她不能不信他爱她——信他终于爱她了。

「我的爱人——爱人，」她悄声说，满是他怀抱近旁的欢喜。

他答：「我的爱人，爱人，」一面在她身上寻他自己也寻得的安宁与知足。

整夜他们相拥而眠，她幸福的杯盏满溢，因为她觉得他整夜离她越来越近——可整夜，他其实离那双她自以为拥着他的手臂，越滑越远。

第二天他温存，却出奇地沉默，她更细地端详他的脸。于是她知道了：她的丈夫老了。

「难道他是愁坏了，我的吉安-卢卡，因为他们不肯调他？」她纳闷，「可他愁什么呢？——他在尽职，人家叫他做什么他做什么呀。」

可如今她又发现他一件事：他不愿谈战争。

他说：「结束了，咱们想法忘掉它吧；英国人不要我打仗——va bene（好吧），如今我得想米洛和多立克了，明天我就去看米洛。」

===Original===

As the weeks went on Maddalena was puzzled by a curious change in Gian-Luca. “They worked him too hard out in France,” she would think; but this explanation was unsatisfactory, for he did not seem physically tired.

To interest him she talked of Aunt Ottavia, and of all the money she had left them: “You will soon be the head of your own restaurant,” she told him, smiling proudly.

“Yes, I know, but not just yet,” he answered; “I cannot leave Millo in the lurch﻿—I shall stay at the Doric for a few years longer﻿—at least, I think I shall stay.” And then he quite suddenly changed the subject, as though it actually bored him.

His place had been waiting for him at the Doric, he was Millo’s chief headwaiter, and far more important than Riccardo had been, who had only had charge of the restaurant. Everything was just as he had wished it to be; he had four very excellent aides-de-camp, to say nothing of a large, well-trained staff of waiters, now that the war was over. Yet he seldom discussed his work with Maddalena, and this made her anxious and unhappy. He had grown very careful these nights not to wake her, he would steal through the hall and into their bedroom, having first taken off his shoes. And suspecting this, she would keep herself awake, feeling lonely at sleeping without him.

“Is that you, Gian-Luca?” she would say, sitting up.

And then he would frown: “Go to sleep, Maddalena, it is past two o’clock in the morning.”

Then Maddalena would talk to the Madonna: “Blessed Mary, help me with Gian-Luca,” she would whisper; “you who so mercifully kept him out of danger, show me what I must do. He is changed, he no longer wants to tell me about things﻿—he is not like a child any more﻿—how can I make him grow younger again﻿—I who have no other child?”

And perhaps she might pause as though expecting an answer, an answer that did not come﻿—for not in poor, faltering human speech could the Mother of God reply to Maddalena.

===Chinese===

一星期一星期过去，马达莱娜被吉安-卢卡身上一种古怪的变化弄糊涂了。「他们在法国把他使唤得太狠了。」她想；可这个解释不能令人满意，因为他身体上并不显累。

为了引他高兴，她讲起奥塔维娅姨妈，讲她留下的那一大笔钱：「你很快就要当你自己餐馆的老板了。」她骄傲地笑着告诉他。

「是，我知道，可还没到时候，」他答，「我不能扔下米洛不管——我要在多立克再待几年——至少，我想我会待下去。」随后他忽然干脆换了话题，仿佛这事真让他腻烦。

多立克的位子一直给他留着，他是米洛的首席领班，比当年只管餐厅的里卡尔多要紧得多。一切都如他所愿：如今仗打完了，他有四位极得力的副手，更不必说一支训练有素的大班人马。可他很少同马达莱娜谈他的工作，这让她又急又难过。这些天夜里他非常小心，唯恐吵醒她，先脱了鞋，才蹑手蹑脚穿过前厅进卧室。她起了疑，便强撑着不睡，独衾而眠，觉得孤单。

「是你吗，吉安-卢卡？」她会坐起来说。
<arg_value><b88a6f17>他便皱眉：「睡吧，马达莱娜，已经后半夜两点了。」

于是马达莱娜去同圣母说话：「有福的玛利亚，帮帮我待吉安-卢卡，」她悄声说，「您那么慈悲，保他躲过了危险；求您指点我该怎么办。他变了，他不再什么都告诉我了——他不像个孩子了——我怎么能让他重新年轻起来——我这个再没有别的孩子的人？」

也许她会停一停，仿佛等一个回答，回答没有来——因为上帝的母亲，也无法用可怜的、期期艾艾的人间言语回答马达莱娜。

## Book / 第三部
===Original===

III

===Chinese===

第三部

### I / 一
===Original===



===Chinese===

一

#### I / 一
===Original===

There had never been such a season at the Doric as the season that followed the Armistice; everyone was flocking to the restaurants now, in a kind of hilarious reaction. Millo was in the seventh heaven of delight, and so were most of his waiters, for people were recklessly spending their money, eating up banknotes with every mouthful, and washing them down with champagne or spirits, so that, naturally, when they got up to go, they left a fat tip behind them.

Millo had long since had to duplicate his band, for a supper without dancing was unheard of. It was said that among other excellent things, he possessed the best jazz-bands in London. The craze for dancing was on the increase, and now there was no age limit; the white-haired, the portly, the withered, the ailing, young or old, maid or matron, they must get up and dance; while a decadent Pan﻿—discarding his reed-pipe﻿—turned sobriety to bibbing and dignity to folly, with the help of a water-whistle.

Gian-Luca inspected his rooms one May morning and quietly nodded his head. Everything was perfect, from the vases of flowers to the well-polished plate and glass.

“*Va bene*,” he murmured, “it is all as it should be; that Daniele is a competent fellow.” And then he observed a fork out of place and hastily put it straight.

A man in a baize apron, with a dustpan and brush, was stalking a couple of waiters; every few minutes he would suddenly swoop at some microscopic speck on the carpet. At a long side-table a person in shirtsleeves opened endless packets of matches; he filled the match-stands with infinite care so that each little red head stood neatly in its place awaiting its coming cremation. Daniele was giving a last expert touch to the wicks of the spirit-lamps, pinching them firmly between finger and thumb, then spreading out their tips as though they were petals; for these wicks were important, since they would assist in the making of the temperamental Crêpe Suzette!

At last all was in order for the business of the day﻿—and what a momentous business! The Doric would feed many hundred stomachs, postwar stomachs too, long deprived of their fill and now determined to get it.

The telephone began to ring every few minutes﻿—people calling up to book tables; Gian-Luca must consult that complicated list which stood on a desk by the door.

===Chinese===

停战之后的那一季，多立克从没有过那样的旺季；人人像欢天喜地的反弹一般涌向餐馆。米洛乐上了七重天，他的侍者多半也一样，因为人们挥金如土，每一口都在吞钞票，还拿香槟或烈酒往下冲，于是自然而然，起身走时，总留下丰厚的小费。

米洛早就不得不把乐队添成两套，因为晚饭不带跳舞，闻所未闻。据说他旗下的种种好东西里，还包括全伦敦最好的爵士乐队。跳舞的热有增无减，而且再没有年龄限制：白发的、发福的、干瘪的、多病的，老也好少也好，姑娘也好太太也好，都得起身跳；一位堕落的潘神——撂下了他的芦笛——借一支水哨之助，把清醒变成豪饮，把威严变成颠狂。

五月的一个早晨，吉安-卢卡巡视他的厅堂，静静点了点头。一切都完美，从瓶插的鲜花到抛亮了的银器玻璃。

「va bene（好），」他喃喃道，「都各就各位；那个丹尼尔（Daniele）是个得力的小伙子。」随后他发现一把叉子放歪了，赶紧扶正。

一个系粗呢围裙的男人，拿着簸箕和刷子，跟在两个侍者身后捕猎；每隔几分钟，他就朝地毯上某个显微镜才看得清的斑点猛扑下去。长边桌旁，一个只穿衬衫的人在拆无穷无尽的火柴包；他以极度的细心装满火柴架，让每一颗小小的红头整整齐齐立在各人的位置上，等候它即将到来的火化。丹尼尔正给酒精灯的灯芯做最后一道专业处理：用指头把它们捏紧，再把端头摊开，仿佛摊开花瓣；这些灯芯要紧，因为它们将协助调制那娇贵的火焰薄饼（Crêpe Suzette）！

终于，一天的大事各就各位——何等重大的大事！多立克要喂饱成百上千只胃，还是战后的胃：长久没喂饱过、如今铁了心要吃饱的胃。

电话每隔几分钟就响——打来订座的；吉安-卢卡得去查立在门边桌上的那张复杂的单子。

===Original===

“What name do you say is asking, Daniele? No, I do not know that name, we have not got a table.” Or: “The Duchess of Sussex? Yes, of course; how many, five? She can have her usual table in the window; and see that you put some flowers by each plate﻿—say a couple of those pink roses.”

And now Gian-Luca was much in request, for the clients were calling in person. “Good morning, signore, a table for three﻿—”

“Certainly, Milady, a table in the corner﻿—will you take that one over there?”

“Ah, *scusi*, signorina, have you been waiting long? Yes, yes, I have seen to everything for supper, it shall be exactly as you wish.”

Gian-Luca was all ingratiating smiles, he seemed so anxious to please you. To see him was to think that he liked you for yourself﻿—not for what you would eat for the good of the Doric; no, he liked you because you were just yourself﻿—that was what was so charming about him. It made you feel genial, you glanced round the room in the hopes of finding someone to nod to, and there stood Roberto, that capital waiter with the really good knowledge of wine.

“Good morning, Roberto. So you’re back again. That’s splendid; I’m jolly glad to see you!”

And Roberto grinned shyly and bowed from the waist as though you had done him an honor﻿—that was what was so nice about Roberto, he made you feel like a sultan. And there was Giovanni, and he too was smiling, smiling and rubbing his hands﻿—Giovanni who knew how you liked your cold beef, not too underdone, just pink.

“Good morning, Giovanni! So you’re safe and sound. Glad to get back here, aren’t you?”

And Giovanni looked flattered: “I thank you, signore. I hope the signore is well?”

Oh, and there was Millo himself, also bowing, waiting to catch your eye﻿—that was what was so nice about Millo, he never forgot the face of a client; a wonderful gift, and it went a long way towards making you feed at the Doric.

===Chinese===

「你说订座的是什么名字，丹尼尔？不认识，这名字没订过，没有位子。」或者：「萨塞克斯公爵夫人？当然有；几位，五位？她可以坐她惯常的靠窗那桌；注意每只盘子旁边摆上花——就摆两支那种粉玫瑰吧。」

这会儿客人亲自上门了，吉安-卢卡忙得不可开交。「早上好，先生，一桌三位——」

「当然，夫人，角落里一桌——那边那桌您中意吗？」

「啊，对不起（scusi），小姐，久等了吧？是，是，晚饭的一切我都交代好了，保准照您的意思。」

吉安-卢卡满脸讨好的笑，一副急欲取悦于你的样子。见了他，你会以为他喜欢的是你这个人——不是你将为多立克的好处而吃下去的东西；不，他喜欢你，就因为你是你——这正是他迷人的地方。这叫你心情舒畅，你环顾大厅，想找个人点头致意，恰好站着罗伯托，那位真正精通葡萄酒的一流侍者。

「早上好，罗伯托。你又回来了。太好了，见着你真高兴！」

罗伯托腼腆地咧嘴一笑，从腰部一躬到底，仿佛你赏了他的脸——这正是罗伯托可爱的地方，他叫你觉得自己像位苏丹。那边还有乔万尼，他也在笑，笑着搓手——乔万尼，他知道你的冷牛肉要什么火候：不能太生，恰恰粉红。

「早上好，乔万尼！你安然无恙。回到这儿高兴吧？」

乔万尼显得受宠若惊：「谢谢您，先生。先生身子可好？」

哦，还有米洛本人，也在鞠躬，等着接你的目光——这正是米洛可爱的地方，客人的脸他从不忘记；一种了不起的天赋，这天赋大有助于让你常来多立克用餐。

#### II / 二
===Original===

This then was the atmosphere of well-being that Millo had managed to create. He believed in giving people exactly what they wanted, for someone was certain to gain in the process, and as a rule it was the giver. Millo might sometimes smile rather wryly, he might even shake his wise head, but then he would murmur: “*Le monde est ainsi fait.*” And remember that he had not made it.

Of his vast staff whose duty it was to see that everyone got what they wanted, none so deft, so proficient, so agreeable as Gian-Luca, whose lips had acquired an automatic smile which he kept expressly for the clients. Millo, watching his clever headwaiter, would consider him well-nigh perfect. He would think:

“After all he was right to be ambitious; the man knows the worth of his talent, and why not? I am lucky to have him in days like these, when everyone feels so hungry!” And then he would chuckle a little to himself, thinking of the people who felt hungry.

But sometimes, if nobody happened to be looking, Gian-Luca’s face altered completely; it grew sullen and tired, it was quite a different face from the one that he showed to the clients. Backwards and forwards superintending the service went Millo’s clever headwaiter, and all that he did was supremely well done, for so many years had gone to his making that now he must work like a perfect machine, without hitch, without flaw, without hurry. There had been many days during that time in France when Gian-Luca had tried to work badly, to be inefficient, clumsy, forgetful of orders; hoping against hope to get sent to the Front if he ceased to give satisfaction. But then, even as now, his long training held, and he could not be other than himself, the perfect mess-sergeant, the perfect headwaiter, the master and slave of innumerable details, the man who could not see a fork out of place but that he must put it straight. He had slipped back into his groove at the Doric as though he had never been away, taking up his work just where he had left it; ordering his waiters, humoring his clients; with a watchful eye, too, for his master’s interests, which he served whenever he might.

===Chinese===

这便是米洛营造出来的那种事事如意的气氛。他信奉人要什么就给什么，因为这一过程中必有人得利，而得利的照例是给的人。米洛有时会笑得相当发苦，甚至会摇他那颗智慧的头，但随即咕哝一句：「le monde est ainsi fait.（世界就是这样造的。）」并且记住：这不是他造的。

在他那支保证人人得偿所愿的庞大队伍里，没人像吉安-卢卡这样巧、这样精、这样叫人熨帖；吉安-卢卡的唇上练出了一种自动的微笑，专为客人保留。米洛看着他那能干的领班，觉得他近乎十全十美。他想：

「他志向高终究是对的；这人知道自己的才能值几个钱，有何不可呢？赶上人人这么饿的年头，有他是我走运！」然后想到那些饿着的人们，自己轻声笑笑。

可有时，恰好没人看的时候，吉安-卢卡的脸会整个变掉；变得阴沉而疲惫，同摆给客人看的那张脸完全是两张。米洛的能干领班来回督视着上菜，他所做的一切都做得无可挑剔，因为这么多年的造就，如今他干起活来必定像一台完美的机器，不卡、不涩、不赶。在法国的那些日子里，他曾有许多天故意想把活干坏，想无能、笨拙、忘掉订单——指望一旦不称职就能被送上前线。可那时同现在一样，多年的训练占着上风，他只能是他自己：完美的食堂军士、完美的领班、无数细节的主人与奴仆，那个看见一把叉子放歪就必得扶正的人。他滑回多立克的旧轨，仿佛从不曾离开，工作从撂下的地方拾起；调度他的侍者，将就他的客人；同时留一只眼照看主人的利益，一有机会就为主人效力。

===Original===

Yet all the while, and herein lay the change, Gian-Luca cared nothing at all. If the Doric had suddenly fallen to pieces, and he himself with it, he would not have cared; a curious indifference had begun to possess him, he worked without interest or pleasure or ambition, because he had the habit of working. Now he would try not to think of his work, because if he thought about it too much he was filled with a sense of smallness. Everything he did now seemed infinitely small, and he himself seemed small in the doing; Millo, the Doric, Gian-Luca, all small﻿—the servants of poor Lilliputians. And yet he could never stop doing small things, he would pause to pick up a pin; a badly-drawn curtain would worry his eyes until he must go and arrange it. A crumb on the carpet, a chair out of place, would cause him acute discomfort; but after he had spoken pretty sharply to Daniele, he would think: “*Dio Santo!* what does it matter? What do all these trifles matter?”

He began to grow anxious about himself, because of this curious indifference. He who had all but reached the height of his ambition, and could buy his own restaurant now if he chose, or if he preferred it get a manager’s job and work up in time to be a rival to Millo; he who had achieved so much single-handed in the face of unpropitious fate, what had he got to complain of? Nothing﻿—and yet, somehow, the thought of his days lay heavy, like a cold, hard stone on his brain. The clashing of music, the clattering of dishes, the incessant talking and laughing; the perpetual movement of knives and forks, the perpetual chewing of food.

“Maître d’hotel!”

“*Si, signore?*”

“I want the wine-waiter.”

“*Si, signore*, I will send him at once!”

“Gian-Luca﻿—”

“*Si, signore?*”

“I’d like some more hors d’oeuvres.”

“*Si, signore*, I will send you your waiter.”

Food, food, food﻿—all those dozens of people thinking of it, talking of it, eating. His business in life just to see that they got it, just to see that they ate more and more. His head would feel dizzy with the fumes of the food, until he would grow almost stupid; but then he would suddenly pull himself up, while a kind of terror seized him.

“This is my life, my whole life,” he would mutter, “and I like it﻿—I like my work.”

===Chinese===

然而自始至终——变化正在于此——吉安-卢卡全不在乎了。多立克哪怕轰然垮塌，连他一起埋进去，他也不在乎；一种古怪的冷漠缠住了他，他工作，却无兴趣、无欢愉、无雄心，只因他习惯工作。如今他尽量不去想他的工作，因为想得太多，他便被一种渺小感灌满。他如今做的每件事都显得无限渺小，做事的他自己也显得渺小；米洛、多立克、吉安-卢卡，全都渺小——可怜的小人国的仆役。可他偏偏停不下做小事：他会弯腰拾一枚别针；一幅拉歪的窗帘会硌他的眼，直到他走去拉齐。地毯上一粒屑、一把放歪的椅子，都会叫他浑身难受；可等他相当严厉地说完丹尼尔，又会想：「dio santo!（圣天哪！）这有什么要紧？这些琐事有什么要紧？」

他为这古怪的担忧起自己来。他这个几乎攀到雄心顶峰的人，如今想要就能盘下自己的餐馆，或者换个口味，先当经理、一步步做成米洛的对手；他这个在命运多舛中孤身挣下这么多的人，还有什么可抱怨？什么也没有——可是不知怎的，一想到他的日子，心上就压上一块又冷又硬的石头。音乐的铿锵、杯盘的脆响、无休止的谈笑；刀叉的永动，咀嚼的永动。

「领班！」

「是，先生？」

「叫斟酒的来。」

「是，先生，我马上叫他来！」

「吉安-卢卡——」

「是，先生？」

「再来点冷盘。」

「是，先生，我这就叫您的侍者来。」

食物，食物，食物——那几十号人想它、谈它、吃它。他的人生业务就是保证他们得到它，保证他们越吃越多。食物的气味熏得他头晕，直到他几乎发木；随即他会猛然惊醒，一阵恐怖攫住他。

「这就是我的生活，我的全部生活，」他喃喃，「我喜欢它——我喜欢我的工作。」

===Original===

To assure himself how much he liked his work, he took to going down to the basement again, in the time between luncheon and dinner. He would stand on the steps that led into the great kitchen, and listen to the pulsing of that mighty heart; he would think of Millo, the heart of that heart, while he tried to recapture the lure of the place that had held him before the war. Food, food, food﻿—great cauldrons of food. Food on the tables of wood and of iron; splashes of food on the walls, on the floor, on the clothes and the hands of men. The vastness of the thing would begin to oppress him﻿—grotesque that it should be so vast﻿—the vastness of the Doric and all that it stood for, the vastness of the appetites that it must appease, the vastness of that long vista of jaws. Yet the larger the Doric grew in his imagination, the smaller it seemed to become; so small that now it was crushing and squeezing; a prison, a press that closed in and in until he could scarcely breathe. But how could a thing be vast and yet small? Surely that way lay madness.

His hand would go up to his throat. He would think: “It is awful﻿—it suffocates!” And then he would hurry away to the larders, afraid of these fantastic fancies.

In the principal larder there would be much to see, the chopping and slicing of meat, for instance; great chunks of raw meat, which a chef called Henri was forever dividing with his knife. There were also those long rows of newly-plucked chickens, with their necks swinging over the shelves, and those deep, white dishes of quaking entrails, and those slabs full of slow-moving, beady-eyed lobsters, who protested that they were alive. Henri would look up from his work with a smile, and perhaps he would say: “Good morning, Gian-Luca. Would you like to select a nice, fat lobster for your pretty Milady? You are always so fussy, and no wonder, for she is extremely gourmande, she will eat it up shell and all!”

And Gian-Luca might answer: “She is little and lovely﻿—they are always the greediest kind.” But he would not select the fat lobster for Milady, instead he would just stand staring at Henri, and one day he said: “Do you like what you do? Do you like the feeling of food?”

“*Ma foi!* But why not?” laughed Henri, surprised. “Do I not live by their food?”

===Chinese===

为了向自己证明他多么喜欢他的工作，他又开始在午饭晚饭之间下地下室了。他站在通向大厨房的台阶上，听那颗大心脏的搏动；他想米洛，那心脏的心脏，一面试着重新捕捉战前迷住他的此地的魔力。食物，食物，食物——大锅大锅的食物。木桌上铁台上的食物；食物的汤汁溅在墙上、地上、人的衣上手上。这庞然大物开始压他——它竟这样庞大，多么怪诞——多立克及其所代表的一切的庞大，它须填满的食教的庞大，那一长列颁骨的庞大。可多立克在他的想象里长得越大，它反而显得越小；小到成了压迫和挤压的东西：一座监狱，一台越夹越紧的压床，夹得他几乎喘不上气。可一样东西怎能既庞大又渺小？那样想下去准是疯了。

他的手会不由自主地抬到喉咙上。他想：「可怕——憋得慌！」于是他赶紧逃到食品库去，怕这些荒唐的念头。

主库里可看的东西很多，比如切肉剁肉：大块大块的生肉，一个叫亨利（Henri）的厨师拿刀分个没完。还有那一长排新拔了毛的鸡，脖子在架子上晃荡；还有那一盘盘白瓷深盘里颤动的下水；还有一板板动作迟缓、眼睛骨碌的龙虾，抗议着它们还活着。亨利会从活计里抬头一笑，也许说：「早上好，吉安-卢卡。给你那位漂亮的夫人挑只肥龙虾好吗？你总是这么挑剔，也难怪，她嘴太馋，连壳都能吃光！」

吉安-卢卡也许答：「她又小又可爱——这一种总是最馋的。」可他并不替夫人挑肥龙虾，只管站着瞪亨利，有一天他说：「你喜欢你的活计吗？你喜欢食物的手感吗？」

「ma foi!（真的！）怎么不喜欢？」亨利诧异地笑道，「我不就靠这些吃的活着吗？」

#### III / 三
===Original===

Gian-Luca still found consolation in his books, he still read the “Gioia della Luce,” and now Ugo Doria had written a new poem, an epic of peace and of peaceful things, of vineyards and hillsides and valleys. It had come to Gian-Luca like a breath of sweet air, like a cool hand laid on the forehead. He said to Maddalena:

“When I read Doria’s words I can almost believe in the Spirit﻿—I can almost believe in your God, Maddalena, for that is how Doria writes sometimes﻿—as though he wrote with his spirit.”

One day he went off to see the Librarian, taking the poem in his pocket. “Have you read it?” he demanded. “It is wonderful, sublime﻿—it is equal to the ‘Gioia della Luce.’ ”

The Librarian shook his head. “No,” he said slowly; “I am sick unto death of books.”

Gian-Luca stared: “You are tired of your books? But I thought you loved books as I do.”

“A man may change﻿—” the Librarian said softly, “perhaps every man must change.”

Then Gian-Luca was silent because he felt frightened, terribly frightened of change. But presently he said:

“A man must not change, he might lose himself in the process!”

“Would that matter very much?” inquired the Librarian. “Suppose he should find something better.”

“He is mad!” thought Gian-Luca. “He is obviously mad!” And he wished that he had not come. “It must be the shock of those boys getting killed within a week of each other.”

“Do you still care for books and food and stomachs?” the Librarian asked him gravely. “I am disappointed in books myself, such a lot of them seem to suggest indigestion﻿—a kind of deranged mental stomach.”

“I care very much indeed,” said Gian-Luca, and his voice was loud and aggressive; “I care for the things that I know to be real; I cannot afford to be a dreamer like you. I am just a headwaiter at the Doric.”

“I know nothing so inexpensive as dreams﻿—” said the little Librarian, smiling.

But Gian-Luca did not smile: “He is quite mad,” he mused; “I suppose they keep him on out of pity!”

===Chinese===

吉安-卢卡仍能从书里找到安慰，仍读《光之欢》；如今乌戈·多利亚又写了首新诗，一部关于和平与和平事物的史诗，关于葡萄园、山坡和谷地的史诗。它来到吉安-卢卡这里，像一口清甜的空气，像一只清凉的手按在额头上。他对马达莱娜说：

「读多利亚的字句时，我几乎能相信灵了——我几乎能相信你的上帝了，马达莱娜，因为多利亚有时就是那样写——仿佛用他的灵在写。」

有一天他揣着那首诗去看图书馆员。「你读了吗？」他劈头问，「妙极了，崇高极了——抵得上《光之欢》。」

图书馆员摇头。「没有，」他慢慢地说，「我对书厌倦到死。」

吉安-卢卡瞪着他：「你厌倦你的书？我还以为你像我一样爱书呢。」

「人是会变的——」图书馆员轻声说，「也许人人都必得变。」

吉安-卢卡沉默了，因为他害怕，怕变化怕得要命。可过了一会儿他说：

「人不该变，变了会把自己弄丢的！」

「弄丢了很要紧吗？」图书馆员反问，「万一他找到更好的呢？」

「他疯了！」吉安-卢卡想，「明摆着疯了！」他后悔不该来。「必是那两个小子在一星期里接连阵亡给他的打击。」

「你还喜欢书、喜欢食物和肠胃吗？」图书馆员郑重地问他，「我对书自己是很失望的，那么多书好像都在暗示消化不良——一种错乱的精神肠胃。」

「我当然喜欢，」吉安-卢卡说，嗓门又高又冲，「我喜欢我认定是实在的东西；我可赔不起当个像你这样的梦呓者。我只是多立克的一个领班。」

「再没有比梦更便宜的东西了——」小个子图书馆员微笑着说。

可吉安-卢卡笑不出来：「他彻底疯了，」他想，「大概是出于可怜才留用他吧！」

#### IV / 四
===Original===

That summer Teresa said to Maddalena: “What is the matter with Gian-Luca?” For even Teresa, immersed in her business, had noticed a change in her grandson.

And then it was Rosa and Mario who spoke, and they both looked hard at Maddalena. “What is the matter with Gian-Luca?” they demanded, as though Maddalena were to blame.

Two days later Nerone called on Maddalena, and there he found the Padrona. The Padrona had just been giving advice as to how one should manage a husband.

“One should never allow him to sulk,” she was saying; “now I think your Gian-Luca is sulking.”

“*Per Bacco*, he is not!” Nerone protested; “the boy is doing nothing of the kind. I think he is probably ill, our Gian-Luca, I think you should send for a doctor.”

Rocca waylaid Maddalena one morning and beckoned her into the shop: “I do not much like the look of Gian-Luca, his face has grown dull and he speaks very little﻿—I hope you take care of our child, Maddalena? He was born in our midst﻿—such a queer little boy, with a positive terror of goats. You must not mind an old fellow like me speaking frankly: I think he is ill; he works very hard, and he needs his small comforts, and I hope you see that he gets them.”

Maddalena reassured them all as best as she could, but her own heart was deeply anxious. Gian-Luca looked weary and discontented; he was sleeping very badly, and when he got up his eyes would be vague and unhappy.

“What is the matter, *amore*?” she would say. “Try to tell me what is the matter.”

But he always replied: “There is nothing the matter; leave me in peace, *donna mia*.”

He was growing coldly unkind to Maddalena; never angry, just coldly unkind. At times he would treat her as though she were a stranger, preferring his books to her company, it seemed, and that summer he refused to go out of London when the time for their holiday arrived.

“I do not want people,” he told her firmly; “wherever one goes there are too many people.” So he sat at the open window and read, sometimes he would not speak for hours. Once he looked up and noticed her eyes: “You are always watching,” he said sharply; “stop staring, Maddalena, I do not like your eyes﻿—they are frightened, they remind me of a rabbit in a trap; I wish you would look more cheerful.”

===Chinese===

那年夏天，特蕾莎问马达莱娜：「吉安-卢卡到底怎么了？」因为连埋首生意的特蕾莎都看出孙子的变化。

接着开口的是罗莎和马里奥，两人都紧盯着马达莱娜。「吉安-卢卡到底怎么了？」他们质问，仿佛该怪马达莱娜。

两天后内罗内来望马达莱娜，恰逢老板娘在座。老板娘刚发表完一套怎样管丈夫的高见。

「绝不能容他闷着，」她正说，「我看你那个吉安-卢卡就是在闷。」

「per Bacco!（老天！）才不是！」内罗内抗议，「那孩子绝没有闷。我看他多半是病了，咱们的吉安-卢卡，我看你该去请个医生。」

一天早晨，罗卡拦住马达莱娜，招手让她进铺子：「我不大喜欢吉安-卢卡那副样子，他的脸发木，话也少了——我想你把咱们的孩子照看好吗，马达莱娜？他生在我们中间——那么个古怪的小家伙，见了山羊怕得要命。老头子说话直，你别介意：我看他是病了；他干活太累，需要些小小的舒息，望你都何候到。」

马达莱娜竭力宽慰了他们大家，可她自己的心沉甸甸的。吉安-卢卡一脸倦怠和不痛快；睡得很坏，起来时眼神总是茫然而不快。

「怎么了，爱人？」她会说，「想法子告诉我你怎么了。」

可他总答：「没什么；让我清静点，我的女人。」

他对马达莱娜变得冷冷的狠心；从不发火，只是冷冷的狠心。有时他待她像待陌生人，仿佛宁要他的书也不要她的陪伴；那年夏天，休假的时节到了，他拒绝出伦敦。

「我不想见人，」他斩钉截铁地告诉她，「不管去哪儿，人都太多。」于是他坐在敞开的窗前读书，有时几个钟头不说话。有一次他抬头撞上她的眼睛：「你老在看，」他尖刻地说，「别盯着了，马达莱娜，我不喜欢你的眼睛——它们受了惊，叫我想起落网的兔子；你高兴点行不行。」

===Original===

There were days when he found fault with everything she cooked. “How can I eat this mess!” he would grumble. “It is all greasy butter, you use too much butter. *Dio Santo!* I get enough grease at the Doric; cannot you cook more simply?” But then he would proceed to finish the whole dish. “I must eat, a man must eat,” he would mutter. And the watchful Maddalena would know that he was not hungry﻿—would know that he forced himself to eat.

At moments her self-control would desert her, and then she would go down on her knees: “I love you so﻿—” she would tell him wildly. “I am childless, Gian-Luca, I have no one but you; all these long years I have waited for a child﻿—be kind to me﻿—kiss me, Gian-Luca!”

He would look at her dumbly as though trying to be kind, and after a moment he would kiss her. “There, there, *mia donna*,” he would say, getting up; “there, there, *mia donna*, you are tired.”

Oh, indeed Maddalena had need of her prayers, had need of the kindly Madonna at St. Peter’s, had need of the Child who stood at her knee, and of Father Antonio, their priest. Father Antonio counselled much patience, it would surely come right, he told Maddalena. God worked in strange ways, but He knew His own business, and His business might well be a prodigal son﻿—Maddalena must leave it to God.

And she honestly did try to leave it to God, try to have courage and patience, but her great faithful heart was failing a little. She would think:

“He came home to me safely from the war, but was it for this that he came home safely﻿—so that he might grow to hate me?” Then she would remember her prayers for his safety, and something within her would tremble, and something within her would start to ask a question, over and over again it would ask it. “My God!” she would answer in a kind of desperation, “I was right to pray for my husband’s safety, every poor wife all over Europe was praying for her husband’s safety!”

===Chinese===

有些日子，她做的样样菜他都挑眼。「这摊东西怎么下咽！」他嘟囔，「全是油腻的黄油，你黄油放得太多。dio santo!（圣天哪！）多立克的油水我受够了；你就不能做得清淡点？」可说完，他又把一整盘吃个精光。「我必须吃，人必须吃。」他咕哝。而留神的马达莱娜知道他并不饿——知道他在强逼自己吃。

有时她会控制不住自己，跪倒在他面前：「我这么爱你——」她会忘情地告诉他，「我没有孩子，吉安-卢卡，除了你我谁都没有；这么多年我一直盼一个孩子——对我好一点——吻我，吉安-卢卡！」

他会木木地看她，像在努力好待她，过一会儿便吻她。「好了，好了，我的女人，」他起身说，「好了，好了，我的女人，你累了。」

哦，马达莱娜确实需要她的祈祷，需要圣彼得堂那位慈爱的圣母，需要立在圣母膝旁的圣婴，需要他们的神父安东尼奥神父。安东尼奥神父嘱咐她多多忍耐，事情终会好转的，他告诉马达莱娜。上帝行事的方式古怪，可祂懂祂的业务，而祂的业务没准正是一个浪子——马达莱娜只管把它交给上帝。

她也确实诚心诚意想交给上帝，想拿出勇气和耐心，可她那颗忠厚的大心渐渐支撑不住。她想：

「他从战场上平平安安回到我身边，可他平安回来，难道就为的这个——就为了渐渐恨我？」随后她想起自己为他平安做的祈祷，心里有什么东西发抖，有什么东西开始问一个问题，一遍又一遍地问。「我的上帝！」她近乎绝望地回答，「我为他求平安没有错，全欧洲每一个可怜的妻子都在为丈夫求平安！」

#### V / 五
===Original===

Gian-Luca worked better than ever in the autumn, determined to slay this thing that possessed him, this spirit of indifference and depression. People were hurrying back from the country, and the Doric was full to overflowing once more; then one morning the telephone rang loud and long, it was someone demanding the headwaiter.

Gian-Luca put the receiver to his ear: “Yes, I am the headwaiter﻿—the restaurant, yes﻿—”

“I want a table for two,” came a voice, “it must be a small little table and quiet﻿—for two, yes, next Thursday for lunch, half-past one﻿—I want a quiet little table for two. Are you an Italian? Then for God’s sake talk Italian!” And the voice proceeded to order a luncheon that spoke well for its owner’s palate.

“What name, signore?” inquired Gian-Luca when at last the order was completed.

“Doria﻿—have you got it?” came the voice less distinctly. “A table for Ugo Doria.”

Then Gian-Luca suddenly ceased to be a waiter. “Ugo Doria, the poet?” he babbled.

“*Ma si*, I am the poet﻿—never mind about that, have you got it all down about the luncheon?”

Gian-Luca walked straight into Millo’s office. “Ugo Doria is lunching here next Thursday, signore; I have come to ask if I may serve him myself﻿—”

Millo looked up from a bundle of papers. “Doria the poet?” he inquired.

“*Si*, signore, Ugo Doria, and coming to the Doric﻿—I have just written down his order.”

Gian-Luca’s voice sounded young with excitement. Millo glanced at him in surprise. “You are fond of poetry then, Gian-Luca? Doria is certainly a great writer, but I did not know that you were a great reader. Have you read his new epic, ‘The Sowing of Peace’? It is very lovely, I think.”

“Yes, but have I your permission to serve him myself, signore?”

“Certainly, Gian-Luca, why not? Let Daniele take your place in the restaurant during luncheon, I would wish you to serve Ugo Doria.”

“Thank you,” said Gian-Luca, “you have made me very happy.” And his eyes were actually shining.

“How curious; he reads Ugo Doria’s poems,” thought Millo as his headwaiter left him. “How little one knows about other men’s minds, and as for their hearts﻿—well, nothing.”

That afternoon Gian-Luca went back to Maddalena. “Maddalena! Where are you?” he shouted.

“I am here,” she answered, coming quickly towards him. “I am waiting for you, *piccino*.”

===Chinese===

秋天，吉安-卢卡干得比以往更卖力，决心杀死缠着他的那个东西，那冷漠与消沉的精灵。人们纷纷从乡间赶回，多立克再度人满为患；一天早晨，电话响得又急又长，有人指名找领班。

吉安-卢卡把听筒贴上耳朵：「是，我就是领班——餐厅，是——」

「我要订一桌两人，」一个声音说，「要一张小小的、安静的桌——两位，是，下星期四午饭，一点半——我要一张安静的小桌，两位。你是意大利人吗？那看在上帝份上说意大利话！」那声音接着报起一桌午饭，报得主人舌头的品位尽露。

「贵姓，先生？」等单子记完，吉安-卢卡问。

「多利亚——记下了吗？」那声音已不大清楚，「给乌戈·多利亚留一桌。」

霎时间吉安-卢卡不成其为侍者了。「乌戈·多利亚，那位诗人？」他讷讷地说。

「ma si（是呀），我就是诗人——这个且不管，午饭都记全了吗？」

吉安-卢卡径直走进米洛的办公室。「乌戈·多利亚下星期四要来这里用午饭，先生；我来请求准许我亲自伺候他——」

米洛从一叠文件上抬起头。「诗人多利亚？」他问。

「是，先生，乌戈·多利亚，要来多立克——我刚记下他点的菜。」

吉安-卢卡的声音兴奋得年轻了。米洛惊讶地瞥他一眼。「这么说你喜欢诗，吉安-卢卡？多利亚确实是位大作家，可我不知道你还是个大读者。你读过他的新史诗《播种和平》吗？很美，我看。」

「读过，可您准我亲自伺候他了吗，先生？」

「当然，吉安-卢卡，有何不可？午饭时间让丹尼尔替你管餐厅，我希望你亲自伺候乌戈·多利亚。」

「谢谢，」吉安-卢卡说，「您叫我非常快乐。」他的眼睛竟然发亮。

「真奇怪；他读乌戈·多利亚的诗，」领班退出去后米洛想，「人的心思，别人知道的何其少；至于人的心——唉，一无所知。」

那天下午吉安-卢卡回到家。「马达莱娜！你在哪儿？」他喊。

「在这儿，」她应声快步迎上来，「我在等你呢，小家伙（piccino）。」

===Original===

Then he hugged her, and laughed, and told her the great news﻿—Ugo Doria was coming to the Doric. “And I shall serve him myself, Maddalena; my hands only shall serve him!” Presently he said: “When I was very young﻿—before I left the Capo, Maddalena﻿—I used to long for this thing to happen, I used to long to serve Doria. I used to see myself pouring out his wine and passing his food with a flourish; I used to wish to impress him, I remember﻿—I distinctly remember that I wished to impress him﻿—but he never came to the Capo.”

“And now you can do it all!” she said gladly; “you, who are a headwaiter! I hope he will know who it is who serves him, but of course he will see by your necktie.”

Gian-Luca teased her: “He will know by my appearance; he will know by my splendid appearance! He will say: ‘Why here comes the great Gian-Luca!’ And then no doubt he will write me a poem, inspired by my exquisite waiting.”

She scarcely dared to believe her own eyes, Gian-Luca was laughing with pleasure. He was young and gay and affectionate again, and all because Doria was coming to the Doric! But what did that matter, so long as he was happy, happy and kind to Maddalena?

She said: “I will bless this man Ugo Doria, because he has made you smile.”

Then Gian-Luca kissed her: “It is foolish perhaps, but yes, I feel as excited as a schoolboy.” After which he must needs get out Doria’s poems, and read her the “Gioia della Luce.”

===Chinese===

他一把搂住她，笑着宣布这天大的消息——乌戈·多利亚要来多立克了。「而且我要亲自伺候他，马达莱娜；只许我这双手伺候他！」过了一会儿他说：「我很小的时候——还没离开卡波那阵，马达莱娜——我就盼着这件事发生，盼着伺候多利亚。我想象自己给他斟酒、花哨地给他上菜；我想给他留下深刻印象，我记得——清清楚楚记得我想给他留下深刻印象——可他始终没来卡波。」

「如今你都能办到了！」她欢喜地说，「你，一个领班！但愿他知道伺候他的是谁；当然，他一看你的领带就知道了。」

吉安-卢卡逗她：「他看我的仪表就知道；看我堂堂的仪表就知道！他会说：『哟，大名鼎鼎的吉安-卢卡来了！』然后八成会给我写一首诗，灵感得自我精妙的伺候。」

她几乎不敢信自己的眼睛：吉安-卢卡笑得那样欢。他又年轻、快活、多情了，全因为多利亚要来多立克！可那有什么要紧，只要他快乐，快乐而且善待马达莱娜？

她说：「我要为这个乌戈·多利亚祈福，因为他让你笑了。」

于是吉安-卢卡吻她：「也许傻气，可真的，我兴奋得像个学童。」说罢他必得搬出多利亚的诗，给她朗读《光之欢》。

### II / 二
===Original===



===Chinese===

二

#### I / 一
===Original===

On the following Thursday Gian-Luca arrived at the Doric an hour before his time. He must interview the head chef regarding certain dishes that Doria had specially ordered; he must speak to Roberto about the champagne; he must give the final directions to Daniele; in fact he must rouse the whole staff of the Doric by announcing Doria’s prospective visit.

Doria’s table would be in the octagon room, in a quiet corner by the window, and at each of the plates there should be a red carnation made up as a buttonhole, for Gian-Luca felt sure in his own mind, somehow, that Doria’s friend would be a man. He might be bringing a well-known statesman, or a soldier famous in war, or perhaps a poet less great than himself, to whom he was doing a kindness. But whoever he brought was sure to be important, if not now, then at some future date, for the man who had written the “Gioia della Luce” was not likely to cast his pearls before swine﻿—and then greatness attracted greatness.

Gian-Luca, who was now nearly thirty-two years old, was weaving romances like a boy; he pictured Doria as slender and tall, with the strong, lean flanks of a racehorse; his eyes would be inspired, the eyes of a poet, but above all the man would convey a sense of bigness, bigness of mind, bigness of spirit; oh, yes, and bigness of sin when he sinned﻿—for nothing small or despicable or mean could have written the “Gioia della Luce,” nor could it have conceived those many other poems that burnt with the fires of the flesh. Serene and enduring he must be, this Doria, in spite of those outbursts of passion﻿—a pasture swept by occasional storms, a valley that harbored a mountain torrent, a mountain whose peak was covered in snow, and illumined by swift crude lightning.

To everyone he met at the Doric that morning, Gian-Luca said: “Doria is coming here today.”

And they answered: “Ugo Doria the poet?” Then Gian-Luca nodded and smiled.

Monsieur Martin, the head of the army of chefs, was inclined to be short in his manner, however. For no war stopgap was Monsieur Pierre Martin, but a culinary king returned to his kingdom, and now on his coat showed a green and red ribbon which stood for the Croix de Guerre.

===Chinese===

接下来的那个礼拜四，吉安-卢卡提前一个钟头到了多立克。他得就多里亚特意点的几道菜同大厨碰头；得同 Roberto 商量香槟；得给 Daniele 交代最后的嘱咐；实际上，他得靠宣布多里亚即将光临，把多立克上上下下全体搅动起来。

多里亚的桌子设在八角厅，靠窗一个清静的角落，每个座位前摆一朵红康乃馨，做成花徽章的样子——因为吉安-卢卡不知怎么，心里笃定多里亚的朋友是个男人。也许带来一位知名的政治家，或一位战功赫赫的军人，或一位不及他伟大的诗人，他这是提携后进。可他带来 whoever 都必定要紧——不是现在，便是将来——因为写出《光之欢》的人，是不会把珍珠丢在猪而前的——何况伟大自会吸引伟大。

吉安-卢卡已近三十二岁，却像个少年那样编织起传奇来；他把多里亚想象成瘦高个子，两肋精瘦像赛马；眼睛该是诗人灵感的眼睛；而尤其重要的是，这个人该透出一股大——心胸的大，气魄的大；哦，是的，还有他犯罪时罪也犯得大——因为写出《光之欢》的，不可能是狭隘、卑劣、小气的东西；那些燃烧着肉体之火的许许多多别的诗，也不可能是那样的东西构想出来的。这位多里亚，必然宁静而持久，尽管有那些激情的爆发——一片偶有暴风扫过的牧场，一条藏着急流的峡谷，一座顶上覆雪、又被候忽而租野的闪电照亮的山。

那天早晨在多立克，他逢人便说：“多里亚今天要来。”

人家便答：“诗人乌戈·多里亚（Ugo Doria）？”吉安-卢卡点头微笑。

可厨师大军的首领 Pierre Martin 先生对他有点怠慢。这位可不是战时的凑数货色，而是重回王国的烹饪之王，如今衣襟上挂着一枚绿红相间的绶带，那是战争十字勋章。

===Original===

“*Parbleu*, is he God?” inquired Monsieur Martin. “Is he God, this poet you speak of? I have never so much as heard his name; now if it could have been Rostand﻿—but Rostand is dead, a very great pity, for only the French can write verses.”

“As you please,” said Gian-Luca; “let us not discuss writers, I am here to discuss their food.”

“Why not cook it yourself!” suggested Monsieur Martin. Gian-Luca smiled: “I should do it so badly; it is you who are the poet of the kitchen!”

After which Monsieur Martin had himself to smile, remembering his exquisite dishes.

“You may leave it to me,” he said, graciously enough; “I will care for your poet’s digestion.”

===Chinese===

“得厂，他是上帝吗？”Pierre Martin 先生问，“你说的这位诗人是上帝吗？我连他名字都没听说过；要是来的是罗斯当倒也罢了——可惜罗斯当死了，真可惜，因为只有法国人写得出诗。”

“随您便，”吉安-卢卡说，“咱们不谈作家，我来是谈他们的吃的。”

“那你怎么不自己动手做！”Pierre Martin 先生提议。吉安-卢卡笑了：“我做出来就砸了；厨房里的诗人是您呀！”

这话叫 Pierre Martin 先生想起自己的精美菜式，也不由得笑了。

“交给我吧，”他说得相当客气，“你那位诗人的肠胃，我包了。”

#### II / 二
===Original===

At ten o’clock Gian-Luca put on his dress-suit, taking quite a long time in the process; he was almost as fussy about this ritual as he had been about all his other arrangements. But once dressed, there was nothing left for him to do, and more than two hours must elapse before luncheon; so he said to Roberto:

“*I* will open the wine, I wish to serve everything myself.”

“You have told me that three times already, signore,” Roberto replied, somewhat nettled.

“Very well then, I tell you again!” snapped Gian-Luca, who was feeling like an overwrought *piccolo*.

He sat down and anxiously studied his lists; nearly all the tables were booked. He said to Daniele:

“Can you manage, do you think? For God’s sake do not disturb me.”

“I can surely manage, Signor Gian-Luca,” Daniele replied, much aggrieved.

“Very well, then, remember all the things that I have told you,” warned Gian-Luca in an ominous voice.
